The stochastic heat equation with multiplicative Lévy noise: Existence, Moments, and Intermittency
Quentin Berger, Carsten Chong, Hubert Lacoin

Abstract
. We study the stochastic heat equation (SHE) ∂ t u = 1 2 ​ Δ ​ u + β ​ u ​ ξ \partial_{t}u=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u+\beta u\xi driven by a multiplicative Lévy noise ξ \xi with positive jumps and amplitude β > 0 \beta>0 , in arbitrary dimension d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 . We prove the existence of solutions under an optimal condition if d = 1 , 2 d=1,2 and a close-to-optimal condition if d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 . Under an assumption that is general enough to include stable noises, we further prove that the solution is unique. By establishing tight moment bounds on the multiple Lévy integrals arising in the chaos decomposition of u u , we further show that the solution has finite p p th moments for p > 0 p>0 whenever the noise does. Finally, for any p > 0 p>0 , we derive upper and lower bounds on the moment Lyapunov exponents of order p p of the solution, which are asymptotically sharp in the limit as β → 0 \beta\to 0 . One of our most striking findings is that the solution to the SHE exhibits a property called strong intermittency (which implies moment intermittency of all orders p > 1 p>1 and pathwise mass concentration of the solution), for any non-trivial Lévy measure, at any disorder intensity β > 0 {\beta}>0 , in any dimension d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 60H15, 82D60, 37H15; Secondary: 60K37, 60G51

1.
Introduction

We consider the stochastic partial differential equation

\partial_{t}u=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta u+\beta u\xi\,,\qquad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0},

(1.1)

where
\xi
is a space-time Lévy noise,
{\beta}>0
is an intensity parameter and
u_{0}
is some initial condition. In most parts of the paper, we assume that
\xi
is spectrally positive (
i.e.
, only has positive jumps) without a Gaussian part; extensions to the general case will be discussed in Section
3.3
below.
The equation (
1.1
) is usually referred to as the
stochastic heat equation (SHE)
with multiplicative noise
or
the
parabolic Anderson model (PAM)
; see
[
17
,
28
]
for early works on the subject and
[
30
,
34
,
63
,
69
,
70
,
71
]
for a selection of more recent contributions in the case where
\xi
is Gaussian.
We opt for the SHE denomination since the expression
parabolic Anderson model
is also often used in the literature to designate the equation

\partial_{t}u=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta u+\beta uV,

(1.2)

where the multiplicative noise term
V
does not depend on time (see
[
60
,
62
]
for examples of continuous models and
[
52
,
54
,
72
]
for lattice models). In this case, Equation (
1.2
) is the real-valued analogue of the Schrödinger equation associated with the Anderson Hamiltonian
H=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u+\beta V
. A lattice version of this Schrödinger equation appears in one of the original papers concerning Anderson localization
[
6
, Equation (1)]
; see also
[
54
, Equation (0.2)]
.
The study of the localization properties of
H
(which bears some connection to (
1.2
)) has been and remains an important area of research in physics. We refer the reader to the reviews
[
80
,
90
]
and the references therein.

Compared to (
1.2
), Equation (
1.1
) corresponds to a related but different problem: the diffusion of particles in a
random medium that varies at small time scales.
This equation has been widely studied in the literature, firstly and mostly in the case where
\xi
is a space-time Gaussian white noise
[
17
]
.
In this case, the equation can be related, via the Cole–Hopf transform, to the KPZ equation, a model for the progression of growth fronts that has attracted a lot of attention in the literature; see
[
5
,
19
,
61
,
68
]
and the two review papers
[
45
,
86
]
. Furthermore, in the case of a Dirac initial condition, the solution to (
1.1
)
has been used to describe the scaling limit of a one-dimensional directed polymer in a random environment
[
3
,
4
]
, called the
continuum directed polymer model
.
With Lévy noise, the existence of solutions to (
1.1
) has only been proved under some additional integrability assumptions on the Lévy measure; see
[
13
,
36
,
89
]
as well as Sections
1.2
and
1.3
below for a more detailed review. Recently,
[
14
]
further showed that directed polymers in certain heavy-tailed environments have scaling limits that, as we shall show below, agree with solutions to (
1.1
) with
{\alpha}
-stable
\xi
, providing a physical motivation for studying the SHE with Lévy noise.

Another central point of interest in past studies of the SHE is the phenomenon of localization of solutions, or
intermittency

[
28
,
54
,
96
]
.
One striking manisfestation is the
appearance of sharp peaks at large times
t
in the random field

(u(t,x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, where
u
is the solution to (
1.1
) with initial condition
u_{0}\equiv 1
.
These peaks result from mass concentration of
u(t,\cdot)
on a fraction of space that decreases exponentially in
t
.
A common way to quantify this concentration property (see Section
1.4
for more details) is to prove that for some
p<p^{\prime}
, the ratio of moments
\mathbb{E}[u(t,x)^{p^{\prime}}]/\mathbb{E}[u(t,x)^{p}]
grows exponentially in time, and to compute the growth rate. For the SHE with Gaussian noise, this type of moment intermittency was studied in depth by, for example,
[
17
,
30
,
33
,
47
,
69
]
.
We also refer to

[
70
,
71
]
for a path-by-path analysis of the intermittency peaks.
For the SHE with Lévy noise, the issue of moment intermittency was investigated only much more recently in
[
38
]
. In
[
39
]
, it was further proved that unusually large peaks (on a logarithmic scale) already appear in the solution to the SHE with an
additive
Lévy noise.

Before we describe our main results in Section
2
, we provide a detailed technical introduction to Equation (
1.1
), which aims at being as self-contained as possible.

1.1.
Lévy noise with positive jumps

Given a measure
\lambda
on
(0,\infty)
that satisfies
{\lambda}([1,\infty))<\infty
and
\int_{(0,1)}z^{2}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty
, we provide a constructive definition of a pure-jump Lévy noise with intensity
{\lambda}
. General Lévy noises, with a Gaussian part or with negative jumps, will be considered in Section
3.3
.
Let
{\omega}
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)
with intensity
\nu:=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}x\otimes\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)
.
The law of
{\omega}
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}
.
For convenience, we sometimes split
{\omega}
into two processes
{\omega}_{<}
and
{\omega}_{\geq}
that correspond to the restriction of
{\omega}
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,1)
and
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[1,\infty)
, respectively. We let
\mathbb{P}_{<}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
denote the associated probabilities, so that
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{<}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
.
For
a\in(0,1]
, consider the measure

\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}:=\sum_{(t,x,z)\in{\omega}}z\mathbf{1}_{\{z\geq a\}}\delta_{(t,x)}-\kappa_{a}\mathcal{L}\,,

(1.3)

where
\mathcal{L}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
\kappa_{a}=\int_{[a,1)}z\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)
.
We also set

\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}^{a}:=\sum_{(t,x,z)\in{\omega}}z\mathbf{1}_{\{z\in[a,1)\}}\delta_{(t,x)}-\kappa_{a}\mathcal{L}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}:=\sum_{(t,x,z)\in{\omega}}z\mathbf{1}_{\{z\geq 1\}}\delta_{(t,x)}\,.

(1.4)

Under the assumption
\int_{(0,1)}z^{2}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty
,
the measure
\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}
converges a.s., in
the local Sobolev space
H_{\mathrm{loc}}^{-s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
s>(1+d)/2
, towards a limit
\xi_{{\omega}}\in H_{\mathrm{loc}}^{-s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
(this is a standard result, we refer to
[
13
, Appendix A]
for a proof of this exact statement).
For
k\geq 1
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
, the Sobolev space
H^{u}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is the Hilbert space of all Schwartz distributions
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
for which

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{u}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}:=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{u}|\hat{\varphi}(\xi)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty,

where
\hat{\varphi}
denotes the Fourier transform of
\varphi
. When
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
(
i.e.
,
\varphi
is smooth and compactly supported), then
\hat{\varphi}
is defined by

\hat{\varphi}(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

The local Sobolev space
H^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is then defined by

H^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{k}):=\Big\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\ :\ \forall\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\ \rho\varphi\in H^{u}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\Big\}\,,

equipped with the topology induced by the family of seminorms
\{\varphi\mapsto\|\rho\varphi\|_{H^{u}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}:\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\}
.
The random distribution
\xi_{{\omega}}
is called a
Lévy (space-time white) noise
with intensity or Lévy measure
\lambda
. In the
the case where
\lambda(\mathrm{d}x)=\alpha x^{-(1+\alpha)}\,\mathrm{d}x
for some
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
\xi_{\omega}
is referred to as an
\alpha
-stable noise
.
We refer the reader to
[
7
]
and
[
46
]
for more background on Lévy noises.
In the remainder, we let
\mathcal{F}:=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the
completed natural filtration associated with
\xi_{{\omega}}
, that is,

\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\overline{\sigma\Big({\omega}\cap([0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))\Big)}.

(1.5)

Remark 1.1
.

Note that the Poisson process
{\omega}
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\xi_{{\omega}})
. Hence the solution
u
to (
1.1
) that we construct in this paper (which is defined in terms of
{\omega}
) is a function of the noise
\xi
.
Our choice to index by
{\omega}
instead of
\xi
is only for convenience because
many quantities in our proofs are easier to express in terms of
{\omega}
than in terms of
\xi
; in particular, this is not because we need an extension of the probability space in our construction. We mention this explicitly because there are martingale constructions which are similar to the one used in this paper and that do require an extension of the probability space: this is, for instance, the case in Kahane’s construction of Gaussian multiplicative chaos associated with a kernel of
\sigma
-positive type (see
[
66
]
for the seminal paper or
[
88
, Section 2.1]
for a review).

1.2.
Mild solutions to SHE

A random field
(u(t,x))_{t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is called a
mild solution
to
the SHE (
1.1
) with initial condition
u_{0}
if for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

u(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t,x-y)u_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)+\beta\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)u(s,y)\,\xi(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\qquad\text{a.s.}

(1.6)

The statement includes the requirement that the integrals be well-defined and finite. In (
1.6
),

\rho(t,x):=(2\pi t)^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{2t}}

is the
d
-dimensional heat kernel and
\lVert\cdot\rVert
denotes the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 1.2
.

If
\int_{(0,1)}z\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty
, the noise
\xi
does not have a locally finite total variation. In this case, the stochastic integral on the right-hand side of (
1.6
) cannot be defined as a path-by-path Lebesgue integral (
i.e.
, an integral with respect to a random measure defined on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
) and must be interpreted in Itô’s sense.

In
[
89
]
, it was shown that (
1.1
) has a unique mild solution if the Lévy measure
{\lambda}
satisfies

\exists\,q\in(1,1+\tfrac{2}{d})\ :\ \int_{(0,\infty)}z^{q}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty\,.

(1.7)

While this condition covers many examples, it is not fully satisfying, in particular because it rules out
{\alpha}
-stable noises for all
{\alpha}\in(0,2)
. Subsequent papers that considered the case of
\alpha
-stable noise (
e.g.
,
[
9
,
13
,
36
,
37
]
) neither established uniqueness nor the finiteness of moments of the solution to (
1.1
).
At this point, let us also mention
[
81
,
83
]
, who investigated an SHE with stable noise but with a non-Lipschitz nonlinearity.

1.3.
The truncation approach to SHE

Prior to this article,
the most general existence condition for the solution to the SHE (
1.1
) was obtained, for a Lévy noise with positive jumps, in
[
13
]
, but using a (possibly) weaker notion of solution than (
1.6
).
The approach in
[
13
]
consists in solving the equation

\partial_{t}u^{a}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta u^{a}+\beta u^{a}\xi^{a}_{\omega}\,,\qquad u^{a}(0,\cdot)=u_{0},

(1.8)

with
\xi^{a}_{{\omega}}
from (
1.3
) and then taking the limit of
u^{a}
as
a\downarrow 0
.

Remark 1.3
.

In
[
13
]
, it is not proved that the obtained limit satisfies (
1.6
). In fact, the main focus of
[
13
]
is not the SHE, but rather constructing a
continuum path measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:
the continuum directed polymer in Lévy noise
. This is further discussed in Section
3.1
below.

In order to review the results of
[
13
]
, let us start by introducing some important notations and quantities.
For
0<s<t<\infty
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
let

{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(s,t):=\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:s<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<t\}
denote the
k
-dimensional simplex delimited by
s
and
t
and define, for
\boldsymbol{t}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(s,t)
and

\boldsymbol{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}
,

\rho_{s,x;t,y}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})\,,

(1.9)

where

\Delta t_{i}:=t_{i}-t_{i-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta x_{i}:=x_{i}-x_{i-1}\,,

(1.10)

with the convention that
t_{0}:=s
,
x_{0}:=x
and
t_{k+1}:=t
,
x_{k+1}:=y
.
Given
a>0
we let
|\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}|
and
\xi_{{\omega}}^{a,+}
denote the total variation and the positive part of
\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}
(considered as a measure),
that is,

|\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}|=\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}+2\kappa_{a}\mathcal{L}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\xi_{{\omega}}^{a,+}=\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}+\kappa_{a}\mathcal{L}.

(1.11)

We define the
point-to-point partition function
associated with (
1.8
)
and truncated Lévy noise
\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}
as

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,y):=\rho(t-s,y-x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(s,t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{s,x;t,y}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}),

(1.12)

under the assumption that the sum of integrals is absolutely convergent, that is,

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(s,t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{s,x;t,y}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}|\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}|(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})<\infty.

(1.13)

It is proved in
[
13
, Prop. 2.5 and 2.6]
that (
1.13
) holds if and only if

\int_{[1,\infty)}(\log z)^{\frac{d}{2}}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty.

(1.14)

The case where
x=0
and
s=0
is of particular interest to us, hence we introduce the notational convention

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(x;t,y):={\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(0,x;t,y),\qquad{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x):={\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(0,0;t,x),

(1.15)

which applies similarly to other quantities such as
{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t):={\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(0,t)
and
\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x}):=\rho_{0,0;t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})
.
When the condition (
1.14
) holds,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a mild solution solution to the SHE with noise
\xi^{a}_{\omega}
and initial condition
\delta_{0}
. Indeed, from (
1.13
), the integrals are absolutely convergent, so (
1.12
) can be rewritten as

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+{\beta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\,.

(1.16)

In the same manner,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(y;\cdot,\cdot)
is a mild solution to the SHE with initial condition
\delta_{y}
and noise
\xi^{a}_{\omega}
, and given some uniformly bounded
u_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
, the random field
u^{a}
defined by

u^{a}(t,x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{0}(y){\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(y;t,x)\,\mathrm{d}y

(1.17)

is a mild
solution to the SHE with initial condition
u_{0}
and noise
\xi_{\omega}^{a}
; see
[
13
, Prop. 2.19]
.

Remark 1.4
.

Note that by translation invariance, we have

\begin{split}\Big({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,x;t,y)\Big)_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\Big({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t-s,y-x)\Big)_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\bigg(e^{-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}}{2(t-s)}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t-s,y)\bigg)_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}.\end{split}

(1.18)

For this reason, we present most results only for the case
s=0
and
x=0
, without loss of generality.

The next step is to investigate existence and “relevance” of the limit
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
as
a\downarrow 0
.
If (
1.14
) holds,
integrating the “Lebesgue part” of
\xi_{{\omega}}^{a}
(see
[
13
, Prop. 2.15]
for details), we obtain the alternative expression

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)\\
=e^{-\beta\kappa_{a}t}\left(\rho(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}}^{a,+}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\right),

(1.19)

which entails the positivity of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)
.
Still under (
1.14
), considering the reverse filtration
\mathcal{G}:=(\mathcal{G}_{a})_{a\in(0,1)}
defined by

\mathcal{G}_{a}:=\sigma(\xi^{a}_{\omega})=\sigma({\omega}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[a,\infty))),

(1.20)

one can observe that for almost every realization of
{\omega}_{\geq}
,

({\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x))_{a\in(0,1]}
is a non-negative càdlàg time-reversed
\mathbb{P}_{<}
-martingale with respect to
\mathcal{G}
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 3.5]
)
and thus admits a limit as
a\downarrow 0
.
To determine whether
\lim_{a\to 0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)
is a good candidate for being a solution to (
1.6
), a first step is to determine whether this limit is degenerate or not.
The answer depends on the intensity measure
{\lambda}
and the following result summarizes the main findings of
[
13
, Thm. 2.7 & Prop. 2.10–2.15]
.

Theorem A
.

Assume that
{\lambda}
satisfies the condition in (
1.14
).

(i)

If, in addition,

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{2}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty\ \quad\ &\text{if }d=1,\\
\exists\,p\in\big(1,1+\frac{2}{d}\big)\ :\ \displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{p}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty&\text{if }d\geq 2,\end{cases}

(1.21)

then for every
0<s<t<\infty
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(s,x;t,y):=\lim_{a\to 0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,y)>0\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

(1.22)

Moreover, the convergence holds in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{<})
for
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.e. realization of
{\omega}_{\geq}
, that is,

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\Big|{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(s,x;t,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,y)\Big|\Big]=0\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}_{\geq}$-a.s.}

(ii)

If, on the other hand, we have

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{2}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty\ \quad\ &\text{if }d=1,\\
\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{2}\lvert\log z\rvert\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty\ \quad\ &\text{if }d=2,\\
\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty&\text{if }d\geq 3,\end{cases}

(1.23)

then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., we have
\lim_{a\to 0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,y)=0
.

One can also consider a
free-end
(or
point-to-line
) version of the partition function
with truncated Lévy noise, given by

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,\ast):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,x;t,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

(1.24)

Theorem
A
applies to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,\ast)

mutatis mutandis
. Following the convention (
1.15
), we use the notations
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{{\beta}}(s,x;t,\ast)
,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{{\beta}}(x;t,\ast)
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{{\beta}}(t,\ast)
for the limits we obtain as
a
tends to zero (as a consequence of the reverse martingale property).
Furthermore, under the assumptions of Theorem
2.1
,
[
13
, Prop. 2.20]
showed
that

\lim_{a\to 0}u^{a}(t,x)
exists a.s. for
u^{a}
defined in (
1.17
), without proving that the limit is a solution to (
1.6
).
Since moments of the measure
{\lambda}
play an important role in our assumptions, we introduce a notation for partial moments of the measure
{\lambda}
by setting

\mu_{a,b}(p):=\int_{[a,b)}z^{p}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)\,,

(1.25)

for
0\leq a\leq b\leq\infty
.
We simply write
\mu:=\mu_{1,\infty}(1)
for the first moment restricted to
[1,\infty)
.

Remark 1.5
.

In the case of a finite mean
\mu
, given
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
the convergence of both
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)
and

{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)
as
a\to 0
also holds in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
.

If
\mu<\infty
, it is convenient to consider the normalized partition functions, defined as

\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x):=e^{-\beta\mu t}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x),\qquad\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast):=e^{-\beta\mu t}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast).

(1.26)

In the same manner, we can center the noise by setting

\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}:=\xi^{a}_{{\omega}}-\mu\mathcal{L},

(1.27)

which has the effect that integrals with respect to
\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}
have mean zero. Note that we have

\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\xi}_{{\omega}}^{a}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})

(1.28)

and a similar identity for
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
.

1.4.
Intermittency and related notions

Consider a random field
(v(t,x))_{t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
where
d\geq 0
; when
d=0
this means that
(v(t))_{t>0}
is a stochastic process only indexed by
t
. Assuming that the
moment Lyapunov exponents

{\gamma}(p)=\gamma(v,p):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\lvert v(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]

(1.29)

exist on the extended real line for all
p\in(0,\infty)
and are independent of
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we let

I=I(v):=\{p\geq 0\ :\ {\gamma}(v,p)<\infty\},

(1.30)

with the convention
{\gamma}(0)=0
.
Further assuming
\lvert{\gamma}(1)\rvert<\infty
in the following, we define
the
normalized moment Lyapunov exponents
of
v
by

\bar{\gamma}(p):=\gamma(p)-p\,\gamma(1).

(1.31)

Clearly,
\bar{\gamma}(1)=0
.
Following the terminology of
[
28
,
54
,
96
]
, if
p>1
and
\lvert{\gamma}(1)\rvert<\infty
, we say that
v
exhibits

(moment) intermittency of order
p
if
\bar{\gamma}(p)\in(0,\infty)
.
We say that
v
exhibits

full intermittency
if
v
is intermittent of all orders
p\in(1,\infty)\cap I
.

Let us also introduce some new terminology when we consider moments of order
p\in(0,1)
:
we say that
v
exhibits

strong intermittency
(in analogy with “very strong disorder” used in the directed polymer context, see the discussion below) if
\bar{\gamma}(p)\in(-\infty,0)
for some
p\in(0,1)
. As
p\mapsto\bar{\gamma}(p)
is convex on
I
(cf. Proposition
2.9

(i)
below), strong intermittency implies
\bar{\gamma}(p)<0
for all
p\in(0,1)
but also that
\bar{\gamma}(p)>0
for all
p>1
. Thus, strong intermittency implies full intermittency. As Section
3.2
below reveals, strong intermittency, plus some ergodic properties in
x
, yields a
geometric characterization
of intermittency: we have a mass concentration of the paths of
v
at large times, characterized by the appearance of exponentially large peaks on islands covering only an exponentially small fraction of space (a more quantitative study of this phenomenon was undertaken in
[
70
,
71
]
, and
we also refer to
[
53
]
for a similar work concerning the parabolic Anderson model on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
).

Intermittency for the SHE

Let us first discuss the known intermittency result in a semi-discrete setting (this is the setup where the most is known).
For the SHE on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with either a Gaussian or a finite-variance Lévy noise, the results of
[
1
,
2
,
28
]
and
[
16
]
show that

•

if
d=1,2
, then strong intermittency holds for every
\beta>0
;

•

if
d\geq 3
, then strong intermittency holds if and only if
\beta>\bar{\beta}_{c}
for some
\bar{\beta}_{c}>0
.

For the SHE on
\mathbb{R}
driven by a multiplicative Gaussian white noise
\xi
, the analysis of intermittency has a long history: if
\xi
has variance
\beta^{2}
and
u_{0}\equiv 1
, the authors of
[
17
]
derived the formula

{\gamma}(p)={\gamma}(u,p)=\frac{p(p^{2}-1)}{24}\beta^{4}

(1.32)

for
p\in\mathbb{N}
and proved it for
p=2
. Later, a proof of (
1.32
) was given in
[
30
]
and
[
78
]
for all integers
p\in\mathbb{N}
and all real numbers
p\geq 2
, respectively. The formula (
1.32
) for all
p>0
(and in particular, strong intermittency) was only established recently in
[
47
,
56
]
using integrable probability methods. For
d\geq 2
, there is no notion of solution to the SHE with a Gaussian space-time white noise (cf. Section
3.3
). For the SHE on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
driven by a Gaussian noise that is white in time and colored in space with, say, a compactly supported correlation function, one has a similar picture to the discrete-space setting: strong intermittency always holds if
d=1,2
and only for large
\beta
if
d\geq 3
; see
[
32
,
75
]
. The situation may be different if the noise has long-range spatial correlation, as shown in
[
32
,
51
,
75
]
.

For the solution to the Lévy-driven SHE,
under the assumption (
1.7
),
[
38
]
established intermittency of order
p
in the following cases: for all
p\in(1,3)
if
d=1
; for
p
close enough to (but smaller than)
1+\frac{2}{d}
if
d\geq 2
; for any fixed
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
in any dimension
d\geq 2
if
\beta>\beta_{p,d}
for some
\beta_{p,d}>0
. Whether strong intermittency (or full intermittency in dimensions
d\geq 2
) holds in the Lévy case or not, has been an open problem so far.

Very strong disorder for the directed polymer model

To complete the former discussion, let us mention some results that have been proved for the directed polymer in a random environment (DPRE), whose partition function formally corresponds to the solution to an SHE in discrete space and time.
In the context of the directed polymer model, the notion equivalent to strong intermittency is that of
very strong disorder
(see for instance
[
4
,
27
,
74
]
). For cultural reasons (DPRE is a statistical mechanics model), very strong disorder is a property of the

free energy
, that is, of the asymptotic behavior of

\mathbb{E}[\log Z_{\beta}(N,\ast)]
, rather than a property of the moments of order
p\in(0,1)
of the partition function;
but this is not relevant for the present discussion.
Very strong disorder has been proved to hold for directed polymers in a various settings:

•

When
d=1

[
43
]
and
d=2

[
74
]
for any
\beta>0
;

•

When
d\geq 3
, if the environment has a power-law distribution with exponent
\alpha\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d}]
, for any
\beta>0
, in
[
93
]
(this roughly corresponds to
\alpha
-stable noise in the SHE context).

The method used in
[
74
]
does not rely much on the discrete nature on the model and has been adapted to prove analogous results when either space or time are continuous: Let us mention the case of the SHE on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with Gaussian white noise
[
16
]
,
the SHE on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a Gaussian noise which is white in time but colored in space
[
75
, Theorem 1.2]
, or directed polymers in a Poisson environment
[
40
, Remark 3.4.3]
(the partition function of which corresponds to that of the SHE with a spatially convoluted Lévy noise).
In fact, the techniques can also be adapted to the SHE with Lévy noise, and the content of Section
9.2
in the present paper is strongly inspired by the proofs in
[
74
]
(for
d=1
) and
[
93
]
(for the case of heavy-tailed noise).

On the other hand there are other situations where
the directed polymer model
does not
display very strong disorder:

•

When
d\geq 3
, if the environment has bounded second moment and
\beta
is small
[
22
,
64
]
;

•

When
d\geq 3
, if the environment has a power-law distribution with exponent
\alpha>1+\frac{2}{d}
and
\beta
is sufficiently small
[
93
]
.

In both cases, it has been shown that the directed polymer displays
weak disorder
, a property which implies but goes beyond the absence of very strong disorder. Weak disorder implies, for instance, that the sample paths drawn from the polymer measure have diffusive behavior
[
44
]
.

For this reason, the question of whether
strong intermittency
holds for the SHE with Lévy space-time white noise is particularly intriguing when
d\geq 3
and
\beta
is small.

2.
Main results

Our main results can be summarized as follows:

\bullet
Firstly, we considerably reduce the gap
conditions (
1.21
) and (
1.23
) leave
regarding the non-degeneracy of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
if
d\geq 2
. We obtain, in Theorem
2.1
and Proposition
2.2
, a necessary and sufficient condition in dimension
d=2
and a close-to-optimal condition in dimensions
d\geq 3
.
We further prove the
L^{p}
-convergence of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
for all
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
as soon as the noise
\xi_{\omega}
has a finite
p
th moment (
i.e.
,
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
).

\bullet
Secondly, in Theorem
2.5
, we show that the limit
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
, when non-degenerate, is indeed a mild solution to the SHE with initial condition
\delta_{0}
. Theorem
2.7
further establishes the uniqueness of solutions under conditions that are general enough to include the case of
{\alpha}
-stable noise.

\bullet
Thirdly,
after establishing the existence of the moment Lyapunov exponents of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
in Proposition
2.8
, we show our most striking result in Theorem
2.10
: the solution to the SHE with a multiplicative Lévy noise and
\delta_{0}
-initial condition exhibits strong intermittency—and thus, in particular, full intermittency—for any non-trivial environment with finite expectation, that is, for any
{\beta}>0
, in any dimension
d\geq 1
, for any non-trivial intensity measure
{\lambda}
with
\mu<\infty
.
In Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
, we further complement this result by deriving sharp asymptotic estimates on the moment Lyapunov exponents as
\beta
tends to
0
.

The proof of the aforementioned results relies on two main methodological achievements:

\bullet
By combining decoupling inequalities with an iterative partition of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(0,\infty)^{k}
(the integration domain of
(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})
), we establish, in Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
, sharp moment bounds of order
p>1
on the multiple Lévy integrals that arise in the series representation (
1.28
) of
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
. From these, we will then derive the upper bounds for
p>1
and, by convexity, the lower bounds for
p<1
in Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
. The proof of Theorems
2.1
,
2.5
and
2.7
will also rely on (variants of) these moment bounds.

\bullet
By combining a change-of-measure technique with a coarse-graining approach, summarized in Lemma
9.1
, we obtain moment upper bounds of order
p\in(0,1)
for
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)

which are then used to show Theorem
2.10
as well as the lower bounds in Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
. Moreover, we apply a simpler version of this method to prove Proposition
2.2
.
While the coarse-graining and change-of-measure approach is derived from that used in
[
74
]
, which itself was inspired from earlier work on disordered pinning
[
50
,
58
,
91
]
, its implementation to prove Proposition
9.5
(which is the most important part of the proof of Theorem
2.10
) and Proposition
2.2
relies on important novel ideas, which we discuss in more details at the beginning of Section
9
).

2.1.
Optimal conditions for the non-degeneracy of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)

When
d\geq 2
, Theorem
A
displays a gap between the sufficient condition (
1.21
) and the necessary condition (
1.23
) for the non-degeneracy of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,x;t,y)
.
Our first result reduces this gap when
d\geq 3
and identifies the necessary and sufficient condition in dimension
d=2
. Our new sufficient condition reads as follows:

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{2}\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty\ \quad\ &\text{if }d=1\,,\\
\displaystyle\int_{(0,1)}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\lvert\log z\rvert\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty&\text{if }d\geq 2\,.\end{cases}

(2.1)

For later reference, we also included the case
d=1
, which is identical to (
1.21
).

Theorem 2.1

(Non-degeneracy)
.

Let
d\geq 1
and assume that (
1.14
) and (
2.1
) hold.
Then, for any
t
and
x
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(t,x):=\lim_{a\to 0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)>0\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

(2.2)

In addition, for any
p\in[1,1+\frac{2}{d})
, we have

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\Big|{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)-{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(t,x)\Big|^{p}\Big]=0\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}_{\geq}$-a.s.}

(2.3)

If furthermore
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
, then

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)-{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(t,x)\Big|^{p}\Big]=0.

(2.4)

The same convergence results hold for the free-end partition function
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
.

Together with (
1.23
), this shows that (
2.1
) is necessary and sufficient for the non-degeneracy of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(t,x)
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
in dimension
d=2
. If
d\geq 3
, the following result improves upon the necessary condition in (
1.23
).

Proposition 2.2

(Degeneracy)
.

Suppose that (
1.14
) holds and that
d\geq 3
. If for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, we have that

\int_{(0,e^{-e})}\,\frac{z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\lvert\log z\rvert}{(\log\lvert\log z\rvert)^{5+\frac{4}{d}+{\varepsilon}}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty\,,

(2.5)

then for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\lim_{a\to 0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)=\lim_{a\to 0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)=0

\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

While there is still a small gap between (
2.1
) and (
2.5
) if
d\geq 3
, we believe that (
2.1
) is necessary and sufficient for non-degeneracy.

Remark 2.3
.

There is a small abuse of language when we say that we establish an almost necessary and sufficient condition for existence of solutions to the SHE, since Proposition
2.2
does not
a priori
exclude the existence of solutions that are not given by limits of solutions with truncated noise.

Remark 2.4
.

Besides replacing (
1.21
) by the weaker condition (
1.23
) and the inclusion of
L^{p}
-convergence, Theorem
2.1
contains a third important improvement. Contrary to Theorem
A
, the proof of (
2.3
),
which is sufficient to establish non-triviality of the limit, does not rely on the positivity of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)
and can thus be directly adapted to the case of signed noise, an observation that we will further elaborate on in Section
3.3
. On the other-hand, our proof of Proposition
2.2
strongly relies on having only positive jumps.

2.2.
Existence and uniqueness of solutions to SHE

Our next result shows that the point-to-point partition function
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,x)
solves the SHE with Lévy noise
\xi_{{\omega}}
and initial condition
\delta_{0}
.

Theorem 2.5

(Existence)
.

Suppose that
(
1.14
) and (
2.1
) hold.

(i)

The point-to-point partition function
{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,x)

is a mild solution to the SHE (
1.6
) with initial condition
\delta_{0}
,
that is, for every
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the stochastic integral below is well defined and the following identity holds a.s.:

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+{\beta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{{\beta}}(y,s)\,\xi_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\,.

(2.6)

(ii)

If
u_{0}
is a locally finite signed measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\limsup_{r\to\infty}r^{-2}\log\Big(\lvert u_{0}\rvert([-r,r]^{d})\Big)<\frac{1}{2T}

(2.7)

for some
T>0
, then

v(t,x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(y;t,x)\,u_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)

(2.8)

is well defined and finite for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
is a solution to the SHE (
1.6
) with initial condition
u_{0}
,
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 2.6
.

The proof presented in this paper actually implies that

{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}),

(2.9)

where the right-hand side is well defined as a convergent sum of iterated stochastic integrals (see Section
4.3
for the corresponding framework).

Under the stronger condition (
1.21
), we prove that the solution found above is unique.

Theorem 2.7

(Uniqueness)
.

We assume that
{\lambda}
satisfies (
1.14
) and (
2.1
) and that
u_{0}
and
T
satisfy (
2.7
).
We let
(v(t,x))_{t\in[0,T],\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
be the solution to the SHE defined in (
2.8
). Then there exists
\theta>1
such that
for all
p\in(1,1+\tfrac{2}{d})
,
t\in(0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\begin{cases}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]<\infty\qquad&\text{$\mathbb{P}_{\geq}$-a.s.,}\\
\displaystyle\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(t-s),x)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(s,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\qquad&\text{$\mathbb{P}_{\geq}$-a.s.}\end{cases}

(2.10)

Furthermore, if
{\lambda}
satisfies (
1.21
),
v
is the unique—up to modifications—predictable random field that is a mild solution to the SHE (
1.6
) and satisfies (
2.10
).

2.3.
Lyapunov exponents and intermittency

Having established the finiteness of moments in Theorem
2.1
, we now investigate the growth rate of these moments as
t\to\infty

and their dependence on
\beta
.
Our first task is to establish the existence of the

(moment) Lyapunov exponents
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Assume that (
1.14
) and (
2.1
) hold.

(i)

If
p\in(0,1+\tfrac{2}{d})
and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
, then the moment Lyapunov exponent

\gamma_{\beta}(p):={\gamma}({\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast),p)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]

(2.11)

exists and is finite.

(ii)

The exponents
\gamma_{\beta}(p)
also capture the growth of the point-to-point partition function, in the sense that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,0)^{p}\Big]=\gamma_{\beta}(p)\,.

(2.12)

By
[
38
, Theorem 3.1]
, we have
\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast)^{1+2/d}]=\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(t,0)^{1+2/d}]=\infty
for any
t>0
and non-trivial
{\lambda}
. We also have
\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast)^{p}]=\infty
if
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)=\infty
.
Hence,
\gamma_{\beta}(p)
is well defined (possibly infinite) for every
p\geq 0
.
Letting

p_{\max}=p_{\max}({\lambda}):=\sup\Big\{p\in[0,1+\tfrac{2}{d})\ :\ \mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty\Big\}\,,

(2.13)

we obtain from the above observation
that the set

I_{{\lambda}}:=I({\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast))=\{p\geq 0\ :\ \gamma_{\beta}(p)<\infty\}

(2.14)

is either
[0,p_{\max})
or
[0,p_{\max}]
,
the latter occurring if
p_{\max}<1+\tfrac{2}{d}
and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p_{\max})<\infty
.

The next result lists a few (partly classical) qualitative properties of
{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
.
If
\mu<\infty
(in which case
1\in I_{{\lambda}}
), we write

\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p):=\bar{\gamma}({\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast),p)=\gamma_{{\beta}}(p)-p\gamma_{\beta}(1)=\gamma_{\beta}(p)-p\mu={\gamma}(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast),p).

Note that
by Jensen’s inequality, we have
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\geq 0
for
p>1
and
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq 0
for
p<1
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
2.8
, we have the following:

(i)

The map
p\mapsto{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
is convex on
I_{{\lambda}}
.

(ii)

If
\mu<\infty
and
p\in I_{{\lambda}}
, the map
{\beta}\mapsto\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)

is non-decreasing if
p\geq 1
and non-increasing if
p\in(0,1]
.

(iii)

If
p_{\max}>1
, then
p\mapsto{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)/p
is strictly increasing on
\{p\in I_{{\lambda}}:\gamma_{{\beta}}(p)-p\gamma_{\beta}(1)>0\}
.

Our next goal is to understand under what conditions we have strong intermittency. Based on the results reviewed in Section
1.4
, one may conjecture that the solution to the SHE on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
driven by a Lévy noise (that is white in time
and
space) exhibits a similar behavior: strong intermittency in dimensions
d=1,2
and strong intermittency only for large
\beta
if
d\geq 3
.
This turns out to be a fallacy: the SHE with a non-trivial Lévy noise
always
exhibits strong intermittency, irrespective of
\beta
,
\lambda
,
d
or
p
.

Theorem 2.10

(Strong intermittency)
.

Let
d\geq 1
. If
{\lambda}\not\equiv 0
,
\mu<\infty
and
\beta>0
, we have
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)<0
for
p\in(0,1)
and
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)>0
for
p\in I_{{\lambda}}\cap(1,\infty)
.

To shed more light on this peculiar result, we further investigate the detailed behavior
of
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
in the small
\beta
limit. Let us start with the case of light-tailed noise at infinity.

Theorem 2.11

(Light-tailed noise)
.

Assume that (
2.1
) holds.

(i)

If
d=1
and
\mu_{1,\infty}(2)<\infty
, then for every
p\in I_{{\lambda}}\setminus\{1\}
, there exists
C_{p}\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
\beta\in(0,1]
, we have

(C_{p})^{-1}\beta^{4}\leq\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\rvert\leq C_{p}\beta^{4}.

(2.15)

In the special case
p=2
, we have
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(2)=\frac{1}{4}\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{2}{\beta}^{4}
.

(ii)

If
d\geq 2
and
\mu_{1,\infty}(1+\frac{2}{d})<\infty
, then for every
p\in\big(0,1+\frac{2}{d}\big)\setminus\{1\}
, we have

\lim_{\beta\to 0}\frac{\log|\log|\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)||}{|\log\beta|}=1+\tfrac{2}{d}.

(2.16)

If
d=1
and
d=2
, these results match the asymptotics
for the free energy of the directed polymer in a random environment (see
[
84
]
for
d=1
and
[
11
]
for
d=2
).
In dimensions
d\geq 3
, however, one has in the directed polymer setting that
\bar{\gamma}_{{\beta}}(p)=0
for sufficiently small
{\beta}
, see
[
22
]
and
[
93
, Prop. 4.1]
.
Note that (
2.16
) implies that
|\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)|
vanishes very fast as
\beta
goes to zero. As a consequence, there is not a lot of margin to play with to prove that
\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{{\beta}}(p)\rvert>0
when
\beta
is small, and the proof indeed relies on a fine tuning of parameters.
The behavior of
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
is different if
{\lambda}
displays an heavier tail at infinity. In this case,
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
rather has a power-law behavior close to
\beta=0
.
Let us introduce

\nu_{p}:=1-\tfrac{d}{2}(p-1).

(2.17)

Theorem 2.12

(Heavy-tailed noise)
.

Assume that (
2.1
) holds and that
\mu<\infty
.

(i)

If
\mu_{1,\infty}(q)<\infty
for some
q\in(1,\min(2,1+\frac{2}{d}))
,
then for every
p\in(0,q]\setminus\{1\}
, there exists
C_{p,q}\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
\beta\in(0,1]
,

\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\rvert\leq C_{p,q}\beta^{\frac{q}{\nu_{q}}}\,,

(2.18)

which implies

\liminf_{\beta\to 0}\frac{\log\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\rvert}{\log\beta}\geq\frac{q}{\nu_{q}}.

(2.19)

(ii)

If
\mu_{1,\infty}(q)=\infty
for some
q\in(1,\min(2,1+\frac{2}{d}))
, then
for every
p\in(0,q)\setminus\{1\}
,

\limsup_{\beta\to 0}\frac{\log\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\rvert}{\log\beta}\leq\frac{q}{\nu_{q}}.

(2.20)

In particular, if

\lim_{z\to\infty}\frac{\log{\lambda}([z,\infty))}{\log z}=-\alpha

(2.21)

for some
1<\alpha<\min(2,1+\frac{2}{d})
(which includes the
\alpha
-stable case), then for all
p\in(0,{\alpha})\setminus\{1\}

\lim_{\beta\to 0}\frac{\log\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\rvert}{\log\beta}=\frac{\alpha}{\nu_{\alpha}}\,.

(2.22)

2.4.
Overview of the rest of the paper

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section

3
, we discuss some extensions of our results and some related conjectures concerning geometric localization.
Section
4
introduces a few important tools
that are needed throughout the paper.
In Section
5
, we prove all basic properties concerning the moment Lyapunov exponents, that is, Propositions
2.8
and
2.9
.
Section
6
is devoted to proving our first main technical achievement: Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
, which contain upper estimates on moments of order
p>1
of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
.
In Section
7
, we then use
these moment bounds to derive
Theorem
2.1
and the first halves of Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
. The existence and uniqueness of solutions (
i.e.
, Theorems
2.5
and
2.7
) are addressed in Section
8
.
Finally,
Section
9
gathers the proofs that rely on a coarse-graining/change-of-measure approach summarized in Lemma
9.1
.
In particular, we show the remaining halves of Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
as well as Proposition
2.2
.
The Appendix contains proofs of some of the results introduced in Section
4
.

3.
Extensions and consequences of our results

3.1.
Continuum directed polymer model

As mentioned in Remark
1.3
, the main purpose of introducing
\mathcal{Z}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
in
[
13
]
is not to study the SHE but rather to define a random probability measure on the space of continuous functions

C_{0}([0,T]):=\{\theta:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\ :\ \theta\text{ continuous and }\theta(0)=0\},

called the continuum directed polymer in a Lévy environment.
For
T>0
the directed polymer in the truncated environment
\xi^{a}_{{\omega}}
is the probability measure
\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}
on
C_{0}([0,T])
defined via the integral of bounded Borel measurable functions as follows:

\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}(f)=\frac{e^{-\beta\kappa_{a}t}}{{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(T,\ast)}\left(\mathbf{Q}\Big[f((B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})\Big]+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(T)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},f)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}}^{a,+}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\right),

(3.1)

where
\rho(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},f):=\mathbf{Q}[f((B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})\ |\ \forall i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket:B_{t_{i}}=x_{i}]
and
\llbracket k\rrbracket:=\{1,\dots,k\}
. Here,
\mathbf{Q}
denotes the distribution of a standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion and, with some light abuse of notation,
\mathbf{Q}[\cdot\ |\ \forall i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket:B_{t_{i}}=x_{i}]
is the law of the concatenation of Brownian bridges obtained by conditioning a Brownian motion on the null event
\{\forall i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket:B_{t_{i}}=x_{i}\}
.
The main result in
[
13
]
is to prove that
\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}
converges in distribution to a limit

\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega}}_{T,\beta}
under the assumptions (
1.14
) and (
1.21
). By the findings of the present paper, we can replace the assumption (
1.21
) by (
2.1
) in the results of
[
13
]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

If the measure
{\lambda}
satisfies (
1.14
) and (
2.1
),
then there exists a measure
\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega}}_{T,\beta}
on
C_{0}([0,T])
such that almost surely, for every bounded continuous function
f
in
C_{0}([0,T])
, we have

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}(f)=\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega}}_{T,\beta}(f).

(3.2)

Proof.

The convergence of
\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}(f)
for a fixed
f
is a consequence of Theorem
2.1
. Indeed,
\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}(f)
can be written as a quotient
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(T,f)/{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(T,\ast)
.
Theorem
2.1
asserts that the denominator converges to a positive limit and the martingale argument below (
1.20
) entails the almost-sure convergence of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(T,f)
.
The fact that convergence holds simultaneously for all continuous bounded functions is a consequence of tightness.
The proof given in
[
13
, Section 4.8]
shows that the family of non-normalized measures
\{{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(T,\ast)\times\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}:a\in(0,1]\}
is tight, with no other assumption than (
1.14
).
The tightness of
\{\mathbf{Q}^{{\omega},a}_{T,\beta}:a\in(0,1]\}
then follows from the almost-sure positivity of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(T,\ast)
; cf. (
2.2
).
∎

3.2.
Geometric localization of the solution to the SHE

The case
u_{0}\equiv 1
.

The intermittency result given in Theorem
2.10
(especially the part concerning strong intermittency) has direct implications on the distribution of mass for the solution to (
1.6
) with initial condition
u_{0}\equiv 1
. To illustrate this, let us present an argument from
[
17
, §2.4]
, adapted to the case of non-integer moments—the argument in
[
17
, §2.4]
is about mass concentration for the square of the solution.

If
\mu<\infty
and (
2.1
) holds, then the solution to (
1.6
) with
u_{0}\equiv 1
is given by

U(t,x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(y;t,x)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Let us define the normalized solution
\bar{U}(t,x):=e^{-\mu t}U(t,x)
.
By symmetry, we have that
\bar{U}(t,x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
for any fixed
x
.
For a fixed value of
t
, let us assume that the field
\bar{U}(t,x)_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is ergodic (in the case of a Gaussian noise, this was proved in
[
31
]
): we then get

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\int_{\|x\|\leq R}\bar{U}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x}{\sigma_{d}R^{d}}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)\Big]=1\,,

(3.3)

where
\sigma_{d}=\pi^{d/2}{\Gamma}(\tfrac{d}{2}+1)
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
On the other hand, if
p\in(0,1)
and
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)<0
(which is ensured by Theorem
2.10
), then for any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)}{p-1})
we have

\begin{split}\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\int_{\|x\|\leq R}\bar{U}(t,x)\mathbf{1}_{\{\bar{U}(t,x)\leq e^{\alpha t}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x}{\sigma_{d}R^{d}}&=\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)\mathbf{1}_{\{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)\leq e^{\alpha t}\}}\Big]\\
&\leq e^{(1-p)\alpha t}\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}]\leq e^{[(1-p)\alpha+\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)]t},\end{split}

(3.4)

where the last inequality relies on super-multiplicativity, see Lemma
5.1
below.
Setting
\delta:=\alpha(p-1)-\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)>0
and
combining (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), we obtain

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\int_{\|x\|\leq R}\bar{U}(t,x)\mathbf{1}_{\{\bar{U}(t,x)\geq e^{\alpha t}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\|x\|\leq R}\bar{U}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x}\geq 1-e^{-\delta t}\,.

(3.5)

Note that we also have

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\int_{\|x\|\leq R}\mathbf{1}_{\{\bar{U}(t,x)\geq e^{\alpha t}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x}{\sigma_{d}R^{d}}=\mathbb{P}\Big(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)\geq e^{\alpha t}\Big)\leq e^{-\alpha t}\,,

(3.6)

thanks to Markov’s inequality.
Hence, the two identities (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) show that in the large
t
limit, the mass of the solution concentrates on a very small portion of space.

The case
u_{0}=\delta_{0}
.

Although this is much more difficult to prove rigorously, we
believe that intermittency in Theorem
2.10
also has implications on the localization of the solution
\mathcal{Z}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
to the SHE with
\delta_{0}
initial condition.
Let us consider the probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)}{\mathcal{Z}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)}\,\mathrm{d}x.

When
\beta=0
,
P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}
is simply
\rho(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x
.
In this case, for large
t
, its mass is roughly homogeneously spread out on the centered Euclidean ball of radius
t^{1/2}
.
By contrast, if
\beta>0
, the large time behavior of

P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}(\mathrm{d}x)
is conjectured to be different:
the mass of
P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}
should typically be concentrated on a set of bounded volume (the volume here should not depend on
t
).

Conjecture 1
.

Given
{\varepsilon}>0
and
\beta>0
, there exist two constants
k=k({\varepsilon},\beta)\in\mathbb{N}
and
R=R({\varepsilon},\beta)>0
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\exists z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ :\ P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B(z_{i},R)\bigg)\geq 1-{\varepsilon}\Bigg)\geq 1-{\varepsilon},

where
B(z,R):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert x-z\rVert<R\}
.

A weaker version of the above conjecture is that localization holds in Cesàro mean, that is, for
{\varepsilon}>0
and
\beta>0
fixed, there exist two constants
k=k({\varepsilon},\beta)\in\mathbb{N}
and
R=R({\varepsilon},\beta)>0
such that

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int^{T}_{0}\max_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B(z_{i},R)\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 1-{\varepsilon}.

(3.7)

In fact, such statements have been rigorously proved for a discrete analogue of the SHE: the partition function of the discrete polymer in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
In this setup, the link between
very strong disorder
and localization in the Cesàro sense was first explored in
[
26
,
42
]
. More recently, in
[
10
]
, it was shown that for directed polymers,
very strong disorder implies (
3.7
); the result was extended to continuous space and discrete time in
[
8
]
.
The approach in
[
8
,
10
]
is to prove that the measure
\frac{1}{T}\int^{T}_{0}P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}\,\mathrm{d}t
converges to a limiting object, using a specific topology (introduced in
[
82
]
) on the space of finite measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The corresponding localization result has also been shown for a continuum directed polymer model (and SHE) with spatially convoluted Gaussian white noise in
[
23
]
, using a Gaussian multiplicative chaos approach.

While many details of the proofs presented in
[
8
,
10
]
use the discrete nature of the polymer model, the general ideas the proof is based on do not seem to rely on it. For this reason, we believe that while there are technical challenges to be overcome, these proofs could in principle be adapted to the continuum case, to show that (
3.7
) holds whenever
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)<0
for
p\in(0,1)
and hence, by Theorem
2.10
, for any
\beta>0
as soon as
\mu<\infty
.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that the localization result in
[
10
]
holds for every
\beta
in dimensions
1
and
2
but only for
\beta
above a certain threshold in dimensions
d\geq 3
. This is because for the directed polymer, the analogue of our Theorem
2.10
does not hold. Quite the contrary, if
d\geq 3
and
\beta
is small, the end-point distribution of the directed polymer satisfies the central limit theorem
[
22
,
44
,
64
]
.

Remark 3.3
.

The above discussion gives a justification of Conjecture
1
in the case when
\mu<\infty
(which implies that
\mathcal{Z}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
has a finite expectation).
As a general rule, an environment with heavier tail is expected to only increase geometric localization (cf.
[
92
]
).

Remark 3.4
.

When
d=1
, known results concerning the distribution of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,x)
in the case of Gaussian white noise
[
5
]
strongly suggest that, in fact, only one big ball (that is,
k=1
) is sufficient to capture most of the mass of
P^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}
. For a proof of a result of this kind in the discrete polymer setup (with a special boundary condition), we refer to
[
41
]
.
In dimension
d\geq 2
, whether one or finitely many balls are needed is a challenging open problem, even at the heuristic level, see
[
10
, Section 9, (1)]
.

3.3.
The case of more general noise

In general, a Lévy white noise
\xi
(on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
) is a random distribution in
\bigcap_{s>({1+d})/{2}}H^{-s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
that can be decomposed as follows:

\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}+\alpha_{1}\xi_{3}+\alpha_{2}\mathcal{L}

(3.8)

where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are two independent Lévy noises with respective jump intensities
{\lambda}_{1}
and
{\lambda}_{2}
,
\xi_{3}
is a standard Gaussian space-time white noise that is independent of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, and
\alpha_{1}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
are constants. In our exposition so far, we have chosen to focus on the case
\xi_{2}\equiv 0
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
. The constant
\alpha_{2}
is irrelevant for any discussion of (
1.1
) as it only adds a multiplicative exponential in
t
to the solution.

Adding a Gaussian part in dimension
1
does not pose a problem for solving (
1.1
); see
[
35
]
. Given that
{\gamma}(p)
in (
1.32
) exhibits the same behavior in
\beta
as
{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
in (
2.15
), we believe that both Theorem
2.11
and
2.12
remain valid when Gaussian noise is added. If
d\geq 2
, the SHE with multiplicative Gaussian white noise is known to be degenerate in the following sense. If
\xi^{({\varepsilon})}
denotes the noise obtained by space convolution of
\xi
with a smooth kernel
\theta_{{\varepsilon}}:={\varepsilon}^{-d}\theta({\varepsilon}^{-1}\cdot)
, then
the solution of

\partial_{t}u^{({\varepsilon})}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u^{({\varepsilon})}+\beta\xi^{({\varepsilon})}u^{({\varepsilon})},\quad u^{{\varepsilon}}(0,\cdot)=\delta_{0}

satisfies
\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}u^{({\varepsilon})}(t,\cdot)=0
(not only pointwise but also in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any fixed
t>0
).
In the past years, important progress has been made to obtain convergence of the solutions to non-trivial (non-Gaussian) limits for
d=2
when
\beta
is sent to zero (in a specific critical window) jointly with
{\varepsilon}
,
see
[
24
,
59
]
and the recent breakthrough
[
25
]
.

One can also consider a noise
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}
that includes negative jumps.
In this case, one can approximate
\xi
by
\xi^{a}=\xi^{a}_{1}-\xi^{a}_{2}
.
Then
the criterion (
1.14
) applied to both jump intensities
{\lambda}_{1}
and
{\lambda}_{2}

still implies the convergence of the integrals in (
1.13
), and
many of the proofs presented in this paper can be applied verbatim.
Note that when negative jumps are present in the noise, the solution is not necessarily positive.
In particular, in this case,
\mathcal{Z}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
does not correspond to the partition function of a polymer model.

Applying the proofs presented below, Theorems
2.1
,
2.5
and
2.7
remain valid when signed noise is considered, and the same holds true for the upper bounds of

\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)|^{p}]
for
p>1
. In fact, this translates into half of the inequalities (the upper bounds) proved in Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
.
On the other hand, our proof of Proposition
2.8
and the upper bounds on
\mathbb{E}\big[|{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)|^{p}\big]
for
p\in(0,1)
rely in a crucial manner on the positivity of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)
, and thus break down when allowing for negative jumps. Note that the upper bounds on fractional moments of the partition function are required for the other half of the inequalities (the lower bounds) in
Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
, and in particular, for Theorem
2.10
.
We believe nonetheless that intermittency occurs in the following sense.

Conjecture 2
.

If
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}
is a signed Lévy white noise as above, with jump intensities that satisfy (
2.1
) and
\int^{\infty}_{0}z^{p_{0}}\,({\lambda}_{1}+{\lambda}_{2})(\mathrm{d}z)
for some

p_{0}<\min(2,1+\tfrac{2}{d})
, then

\gamma_{\beta}(p):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}[|Z^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)|^{p}]

is well defined for
p\in[0,p_{0}]
and
p\mapsto\gamma_{\beta}(p)/p
is an increasing function of
p
on the interval
[1,p_{0}]
.

4.
Preliminary tools

4.1.
Size-biased measure

If
\mu<\infty
, note that

\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)
is non-negative by (
1.19
) and satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)]=1
.
We can therefore define a new measure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{a}_{\beta,t}
for the environment by

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{a}_{\beta,t}({\omega}\in A):=\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)\mathbf{1}_{A}\Big]\,,

(4.1)

which is referred to as the
size-biased measure
.
Note that when

(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast))_{a\in(0,1)}
is uniformly integrable, then using martingale convergence, we can also consider

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}_{\beta,t}
whose density with respect to
\mathbb{P}
is given by
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
.

Lemma 3.7 in
[
13
]
gives a useful representation of
the size-biased measure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{a}_{\beta,t}
:
the distribution of
{\omega}
under
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{a}_{\beta,t}

is obtained by adding to
{\omega}
an independent Poisson process on the trajectory of a Brownian motion. More precisely, we let
{\omega}^{\prime}
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
with intensity
\mathrm{d}t\otimes{\beta}z\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)
and we denote its law by
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}
.
Furthermore, let
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
be a standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion starting from
0
that is independent of both
{\omega}
and
{\omega}^{\prime}
and denote its distribution by
\mathbf{Q}
.
For
a\in[0,1]
, we then define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}({\omega}^{\prime},B)

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(\tau,B_{\tau},\zeta):(\tau,\zeta)\in{\omega}^{\prime}\Big\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{a,t}({\omega}^{\prime},B)

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(\tau,B_{\tau},\zeta):(\tau,\zeta)\in{\omega}^{\prime},\tau\in[0,t]\,,\zeta\geq a\Big\}\,,

which corresponds to putting the Poisson process
{\omega}^{\prime}
on the trajectory of
B
, with some restrictions on
\tau
and
\zeta
in the second case. When
a=0
, the dependence in
a
is sometimes omitted.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
\mu<\infty
, then for any
\beta,t>0
and
a\in(0,1]
and for any measurable bounded function
g
, we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{a}_{\beta,t}[g({\omega})]=\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}[\widehat{g}_{a,t}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)],

(4.2)

where
\widehat{g}_{a,t}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B):=g({\omega}\cup\widetilde{\omega}_{a,t}({\omega}^{\prime},B))
.
If

(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast))_{a\in(0,1)}
is uniformly integrable, then (
4.2
) is also satisfied for
a=0
.

If
a>0
, this was shown in
[
13
, Lemma 3.7]
. For
a=0
, the result can be deduced from the case
a>0
by observing that
\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)\mid\mathcal{G}_{a}]=\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
for any
a\in(0,1]
, which is why
(
4.2
) with
a=0
is true for any bounded
\mathcal{G}_{a}
-measurable
g
. The monotone class theorem yields (
4.2
) for general
g
. Note that by Theorem
2.1
,
(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast))_{a\in(0,1)}
is uniformly integrable as soon as (
2.1
) holds.

Remark 4.2
.

Let us make a quick comment about notation. In (
4.2
) as well as in the rest of the paper, we denote the expectation with respect to a measure
\mathbb{P}
, possibly with decorations, by
\mathbb{E}
with the same decorations.
For example, we write
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{a}_{\beta,t}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}
for expectation with respect to
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{a}_{\beta,t}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}
, respectively.

4.2.
Decoupling inequalities

In the proofs below, we shall occasionally make use of
moment decoupling inequalities
for multiple Poisson integrals. The results that we review in this section are based on
[
67
]
, where discrete-time decoupling techniques from
[
48
,
73
]
are extended to continuous time. We also refer the reader to these references for further literature (and history) on the subject.
Let
N\geq 1
be an integer, let
\mathbb{X}:=(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)
, with generic point
w:=(t,x,z)
, and for concreteness let
M=M^{\omega}
be the compensated Poisson random measure

M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w)=(\delta_{\omega}-\nu)(\mathrm{d}w)
(recall that
\nu:=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}x\otimes{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)
). Furthermore, suppose that
f:\mathbb{X}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable and
tetrahedral
, that is,

f((t_{1},x_{1},z_{1}),\dots,(t_{N},x_{N},z_{N}))=0\quad\text{unless}\ t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}\,.

In that case, subject to integrability conditions on
f
, the multiple integral

\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M(\mathrm{d}w_{N}):=\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Biggl(\cdots\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\Biggr)\cdots\Biggr)\,M(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\,,

with
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})
,
can be defined as an iterated Itô integral (recall Remark
1.2
). The general definition of Itô integrals used in this paper is detailed in Section
4.3
.
We let
({\omega}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
denote i.i.d. copies of our environment
{\omega}
and we use the notations
\mathbb{P}^{\otimes N}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\otimes N}
for the associated probability and expectation, respectively. The following theorem is proved in Appendix
A
.

Theorem 4.3
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for every
1<p\leq 2
, for any integer
N\geq 1
and any measurable and tetrahedral function
f:\mathbb{X}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}\lvert f(\boldsymbol{w})\rvert^{p}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\nu(\mathrm{d}w_{N})<\infty\,,

(4.3)

the stochastic integrals below are well defined and have finite moments of order
p
satisfying

\begin{split}&\bigg(\frac{p-1}{C}\bigg)^{N}\mathbb{E}^{\otimes N}\Biggl[\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{{\omega}_{1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr\rvert^{p}\Biggr]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr\rvert^{p}\Biggr]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\bigg(\frac{C}{p-1}\bigg)^{N}\mathbb{E}^{\otimes N}\Biggl[\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{{\omega}_{1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr\rvert^{p}\Biggr].\end{split}

(4.4)

4.3.
Stochastic integration in the absence of moments

Because
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,x)
does not possess any finite moments in general, the proof of Theorem
2.5
has to make use of a stochastic integration theory that does not assume existence of any moments a priori. For the reader’s convenience, let us give a brief review of this
L^{0}
-theory, which was developed by
[
20
]
in its most general form (see also
[
37
, Appendix A]
for a summary). Let
M=M^{\omega}
denote either the Poisson measure
\delta_{\omega}
or its compensated version
\delta_{\omega}-\nu
.

A
predictable step process

H
is of the form
H=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{A_{i}}
, where
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, and for each
i
we have
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))
(where
\mathcal{P}
is the usual predictable
\sigma
-field) and
A_{i}\subseteq{\Omega}\times(0,T)\times[-N,N]^{d}\times(a,\infty)
for some
T,N,a>0
.
Then, the integral of the predictable step process
H=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathbf{1}_{A_{i}}

is canonically defined as

\int_{\mathbb{X}}H(\boldsymbol{w})\,M(\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{w}):=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}M(A_{i})\,.

Denoting by
{\mathcal{S}}
the collection of predictable step processes, we can extend the integral to a larger subset of predictable processes by using the metric induced by

\lVert K\rVert_{M,p}:=\sup_{H\in{\mathcal{S}},\lvert H\rvert\leq\lvert K\rvert}\Biggl\lVert\int_{\mathbb{X}}H(\boldsymbol{w})\,M(\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{w})\Biggr\rVert_{L^{p}},

(4.5)

defined for
{\mathcal{P}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))
-measurable processes
K
,
where we have used the notation
\lVert X\rVert_{L^{p}}:=\mathbb{E}[\lvert X\rvert^{p}]^{1/p}
and
\lVert X\rVert_{L^{0}}:=\mathbb{E}[1\wedge\lvert X\rvert]
. Such a process
K
is
called
L^{p}
-integrable with respect to
M
if there exists a sequence
(H_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert K-H_{n}\rVert_{M,p}=0
.
The stochastic integral of
K
with respect to
M
is then defined as the
L^{p}
-limit of
\int_{\mathbb{X}}H_{n}(\boldsymbol{w})\,M(\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{w})
,
which exists and does not depend on the choice of
(H_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. If
p=0
, we simply say that
K
is
integrable with respect to
M
. According to
[
20
, (2.8)]
,
K
is
L^{p}
-integrable with respect to
M
if and only if

\begin{cases}\lVert K\rVert_{M,p}<\infty&\text{if }p>0,\\
\displaystyle\lim_{u\to 0}\lVert uK\rVert_{M,0}=0&\text{if }p=0.\end{cases}

(4.6)

Furthermore, by
[
37
, Lemma A.2]
,

•

if
1\leq p<\infty
and
M^{\omega}=\delta_{\omega}-\nu
, there are
c=c_{p}>0
and
C=C_{p}>0
such that

c\lVert K\rVert_{M,p}\leq\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}}K(\boldsymbol{w})^{2}\,\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{w})\biggr)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Biggr]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C\lVert K\rVert_{M,p};

(4.7)

•

if
0<p\leq 1
and
M^{\omega}=\delta_{\omega}
, then

\lVert K\rVert_{M,p}^{p}\leq\int_{\mathbb{X}}\mathbb{E}[\lvert K(\boldsymbol{w})\rvert^{p}]\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{w}).

In particular, the
L^{p}
-theory,
p\geq 1
, for
\delta_{\omega}-\nu
encompasses the
L^{p}
- and
L^{2}
-integrals considered in
[
89
]
and
[
94
]
. Even in the case when
\delta_{\omega}-\nu
has a finite first moment (
i.e.
, is a martingale measure), the
L^{0}
-theory is more general since, for example, it does not require the integrands have finite moments.

Let us end this section with a simple integrability criterion that we need in the proof of Theorem
2.5
. Its proof can be found in the appendix.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose that for
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.e. realization of
{\omega}_{\geq}
, the process
({\omega}_{<},t,x)\mapsto K({\omega}_{<}\cup{\omega}_{\geq},t,x)
is
L^{p}(\mathbb{P}_{<})
-integrable with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
for some
p>0
. Then
K
is
L^{0}(\mathbb{P})
-integrable with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
.

4.4.
A technical lemma

The following technical result will be used repeatedly in the paper.
Its proof comes from a straightforward calculation and can be found in
[
13
, Lemma A.3]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
t>0
,
k\geq 0
and
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k+1}>0
,

\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\zeta_{i}-1}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}=t^{\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\zeta_{i}-1}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(\zeta_{i})}{\Gamma(\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\zeta_{i})}\,,

where
\Delta t_{i}:=t_{i}-t_{i-1}
as defined in (
1.10
), with the convention
t_{0}=0
and
t_{k+1}=t
.

5.
Qualitative properties of Lyapunov exponents

In this section, we prove the statements made in Section
2.3
concerning existence and basic properties of moment Lyapunov exponents (Propositions
2.8
and
2.9
), assuming that Theorem
2.1
as well as the bounds in Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
below are true. These are proved independently in Sections
6
and
7
, respectively.

5.1.
Existence of Lyapunov exponents

The existence of
{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
readily follows from sub-/ supermultiplicative properties of the moments of the partition function.

Lemma 5.1
.

Under assumption (
1.14
), for every
a\in(0,1]
,
s,t>0
and
p\in[0,1+\frac{2}{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t+s,\ast)^{p}]\leq\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,\ast)^{p}]\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}]\qquad\text{ if }p\geq 1,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t+s,\ast)^{p}]\geq\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,\ast)^{p}]\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}]\qquad\text{ if }p\leq 1.

(5.2)

Proof.

The statement being trivial if
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)=\infty
, we can assume
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for the remainder of the proof.
Let
P_{{\beta},t}^{{\omega},a}
denote the (random) probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose density with respect to the Lebesgue measure is given by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)/{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
and
X
denote an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable with distribution
P_{{\beta},t}^{{\omega},a}
.
Recalling the definition (
1.24
) of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;t,\ast)

and using the Markov property for Brownian motion,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s+t,\ast)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,x){\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;s+t,\ast)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,x;s+t,\ast)\,P_{{\beta},s}^{{\omega},a}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)\,E_{{\beta},s}^{{\omega},a}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,X;s+t,\ast)\Big]\,.

For
p\geq 1
, we get by Jensen’s inequality
that

{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s+t,\ast)^{p}\leq{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)^{p}\,E_{{\beta},s}^{{\omega},a}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,X;s+t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\,,

and the inequality goes in the other direction if
p\in(0,1)
.
Now, recalling (
1.5
) and (
1.18
), we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)^{p}\,E_{{\beta},s}^{{\omega},a}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,X;s+t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\mathrel{\Big|}\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big]&={\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)^{p}\,E_{{\beta},s}^{{\omega},a}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s,X;s+t,\ast)^{p}\mathrel{\Big|}\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big]\Big]\\
&={\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)^{p}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\,.\end{split}

Altogether, we have proven that for
p\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(s+t,\ast)^{p}\mathrel{\Big|}\mathcal{F}_{s}\Big]\leq{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(s,\ast)^{p}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega},a}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\,,

which yields (
5.1
).Repeating the same computation with reversed inequalities when
p\in(0,1)
, we obtain (
5.2
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

As a consequence of (
2.4
) in Theorem
2.1
, we have

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big].

Therefore, the previous lemma remains valid for
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
instead of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
.
An immediate consequence is that the limit in (
2.11
) exists by the continuous version of Fekete’s subadditive (or superadditive) lemma. It remains to check that
\gamma_{\beta}(p)
is finite when (
2.1
) is satisfied and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
.
When
p\in[1,1+\tfrac{2}{d})
,
{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)<\infty
follows from

{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(n,\ast)^{p}\Big]\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(1,\ast)^{p}\Big]^{n}=\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(1,\ast)^{p}\Big],

(5.3)

and the finiteness of
\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(1,\ast)^{p}]
, see Theorem
2.1
.

For
p\in(0,1)
, we prove
\gamma_{\beta}(p)>-\infty
and
\gamma_{\beta}(p)<\infty
separately.
For the first part,
let
\gamma^{<}_{\beta}(p)
be the moment Lyapunov exponents that one obtains after replacing
{\omega}
by
{\omega}_{<}
.
Thus, by an obvious comparison and convexity (see Proposition
2.9
, applied to
\lambda_{<}(\mathrm{d}z)=\mathbf{1}_{(0,1)}(z)\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)
), it follows that

\gamma_{\beta}(p)\geq\gamma^{<}_{\beta}(p)\geq\frac{{\gamma}_{\beta}^{<}(1)-(1-\theta){\gamma}_{\beta}^{<}(1+\frac{1}{d})}{\theta}>-\infty,

where
\theta\in(0,1)
is such that
1=\theta p+(1-\theta)(1+\frac{1}{d})
.
Note that
{\gamma}_{\beta}^{<}(1)=0
and that
{\gamma}_{\beta}^{<}(1+\frac{1}{d})<\infty
thanks to (
5.3
).

In order to show that
{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)<\infty

when
p\in(0,\infty)
and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
, we use Jensen’s inequality for
\mathbb{E}_{<}
so that for every
a\in[0,1)
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big].

To bound the right-hand side, we use the following simple inequality, which will be used extensively in the remainder of the paper:
given
\kappa\in(0,1)
and any countable collection of non-negative numbers
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
,
we have

\bigg(\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\bigg)^{\kappa}\leq\sum_{i\in I}a^{\kappa}_{i}.

(5.4)

We will refer to (
5.4
), which can be proved by induction (using the fact that
(a+b)^{\kappa}\leq a^{\kappa}+b^{\kappa}
), as the
subadditivity property
; note that it obviously extends to stochastic integrals with respect to discrete measures.

Moreover, note that we have

\rho(t,x)^{p}=t^{\nu_{p}-1}\vartheta(p)\rho(\tfrac{t}{p},x),

(5.5)

where
\nu_{p}=1-\frac{d}{2}(p-1)
was defined in (
2.17
) and
\vartheta(p):=(2\pi)^{\nu_{p}-1}p^{-\frac{d}{2}}
.
Thus, using (
5.4
) for the first step, (
5.5
) and the fact that
\rho
is a density for the second step, and Lemma
4.5
for the last step, we derive

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta^{p}\mu_{1,\infty}(p))^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta^{p}\vartheta(p)\mu_{1,\infty}(p))^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\beta^{p}\vartheta(p)\mu_{1,\infty}(p)\Gamma(\nu_{p})t^{\nu_{p}})^{k}}{\Gamma(\nu_{p}k+1)}\,.

Using Stirling’s formula for the gamma function (or the estimate (
7.13
) below), one can check that the right-hand side
grows exponentially in
t
: as a consequence, we have
\gamma_{\beta}(p)<\infty
. This ends the proof of part
(i)
of the proposition.

For part
(ii)
, we are going to prove the upper and lower bounds in (
2.12
) separately.
Using Jensen’s inequality for the first inequality and the translation invariance (
1.18
) for the last identity, we have, for
p>1
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t,x)\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\rho(t,x)^{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\Big)^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\rho(t,0)^{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)\Big)^{p}\Big].

(5.6)

Since
\rho(t,0)^{-1}
is of order
t^{d/2}
, after taking logarithm, dividing by
t
and taking the limit as
t\to\infty
, we obtain

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]\geq\gamma_{\beta}(p).

(5.7)

The same proof yields for
p\in(0,1)
that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]\leq\gamma_{\beta}(p).

(5.8)

Let us now prove the complementary bound, first in the case
p>1
. We introduce the probability measure
\bar{P}_{{\beta},t}^{{\omega}}
with Lebesgue density
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x)/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x)\,\mathrm{d}x
.
Then we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x;t+1,0)\rho(1,x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigg)\bar{E}_{{\beta},t}^{{\omega}}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,X;t+1,0)\rho(1,X)^{-1}\Big].

Proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
5.1
and using the translation invariance (
1.18
), we obtain
for
p>1
