\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)^{p}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{p}\bigg]\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(1,0)^{p}\rho(1,0)^{-p}\Big]\,.

(5.9)

In particular, we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)^{p}\Big]\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{pd}{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(1,0)^{p}\rho(1,0)^{-p}\Big],

which implies, for
p>1
,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)^{p}\Big]\leq\gamma_{\beta}(p)\,.

When
p\in(0,1)
, repeating the proof of (
5.9
), but using concavity instead of convexity, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)^{p}\Big]\geq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{p}\bigg]\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(1,0)^{p}\rho(1,0)^{-p}\Big].

(5.10)

Now setting
\mathcal{C}_{z}:=z+[0,1)^{d}
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{z}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\bigg(\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{z}}\rho(t,x)\bigg)Y_{z}(t)

(5.11)

with

Y_{z}(t):=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{z}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(t,x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Note that the variables
Y_{z}
are identically distributed by (
1.18
), which together with (
5.4
) implies

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\leq\bigg(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{z}}\rho(t,x)^{p}\bigg)\mathbb{E}[Y_{0}(t)^{p}]\leq Ct^{\frac{d}{2}{(1-p)}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{0}(t)^{p}]

(5.12)

for
t>1
. Moreover, also for
t>1
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)\rho(1,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\bigg(\min_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\rho(1,x)\rho(t,x)\bigg)Y_{0}(t)\geq(2\pi)^{-d}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}Y_{0}(t)\,.

(5.13)

Therefore, combining (
5.10
) with (
5.12
) and (
5.13
), we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)^{p}\Big]\geq C^{\prime}{t^{-\frac{d}{2}}}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\,.

This allows us to conclude that, for
p\in(0,1)
,

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t+1,0)^{p}\Big]\geq\gamma_{\beta}(p).\qed

5.2.
Monotonicity and convexity properties

To show the monotonicity in
\beta
of the Lyapunov exponents, we prove a more general result linking monotonicity and convexity. Its proof is inspired by an analogous result proved in the discrete setup
[
44
, Lemma 3.3]
. Recall the definition of the renormalized partition function (
1.26
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume that
\mu_{1,\infty}(q)<\infty
for some
q\in[1,1+\frac{2}{d})

and let
\varphi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex function that satisfies

\sup_{u\geq 1}\ |\varphi(u)|u^{-q}<\infty\,.

Then
\beta\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0))]
is a non-decreasing function, for any
a>0
.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

As a consequence of Lemma
5.2
, if

\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for some
p\in[1,1+\frac{2}{d})
,
then

\beta\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}]

is non-decreasing if
p>1
and non-increasing if
p\in(0,1)
(apply Lemma
5.2
to the convex function
x\mapsto-x^{p}
). Letting
a\to 0
, we obtain the same results for
\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}]
because of (
2.4
).
Since we have

\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}]\,,

this completes the proof of
(ii)
in Proposition
2.9
.
Both
(i)
and
(iii)
are standard: The convexity of
p\mapsto{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
follows from that
of
p\mapsto\log\mathbb{E}[X^{p}]
, valid for an arbitrary non-negative random variable
X
by Hölder’s inequality;

(iii)
is a direct consequence of convexity since for
1<p<p^{\prime}
we have

\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq\frac{p-1}{p^{\prime}-1}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p^{\prime})
, recalling that
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(1)=0
by definition.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Since
\varphi
is convex, there exists
a
and
b
such that

\varphi(u)+au+b\geq 0
for every
u\geq 0
.
Hence, replacing
\varphi(u)
by
\varphi(u)+au+b
, we can assume that
\varphi
is non-negative, since
\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)]=\rho(t,0)
does not depend on
\beta
.
We may also assume without loss of generality that
\varphi
is differentiable on
[0,\infty)
and that
\varphi^{\prime}
is bounded.
Indeed, if this is not the case, we can find a sequence
\varphi_{n}\uparrow\varphi
that has these properties and then use monotone convergence.

Recalling the notation (
1.11
) and setting
\bar{\kappa}_{a}=\kappa_{a}+\mu
, we have, similarly to (
1.19
),

\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)=e^{-\beta\bar{\kappa}_{a}t}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).

Taking the derivative with respect to
\beta
, we obtain, after an index shift for the first term,

\begin{multlined}\partial_{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)=e^{-\beta\bar{\kappa}_{a}t}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\bigg((k+1)\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k+1}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k+1}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\\
-\bar{\kappa}_{a}t\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\bigg)\,.\end{multlined}

(5.14)

The first term in the sum can be viewed as the sum of
k+1
integrals
where the variables of integration are
(t_{1},\dots,t_{i},s,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{k})\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{k+1}(t)
and
(x_{1},\dots,x_{i},y,x_{i+1},\dots,x_{k})
for
i=0,\dots,k
.
Recombining the terms, we arrive at the identity (recall the convention
t_{0}:=0
and
t_{k+1}:=t
)

(k+1)\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k+1}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k+1}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\\
=\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\Bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}),

where

\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x}):=\frac{\rho(s-t_{i-1},y-x_{i-1})\rho(t_{i}-s,x_{i}-y)}{\rho(t_{i}-t_{i-1},x_{i}-x_{i-1})}\quad\text{ if }s\in(t_{i-1},t_{i})

and
\rho(t_{i},y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x}):=0
for all
i=1,\dots,k
.
As
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}y=1
, the second term in (
5.14
) corresponds to a centering of the noise
\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)
, and we have

\begin{split}&\partial_{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\\
&=e^{-\beta\bar{\kappa}_{a}t}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\Bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).\end{split}

(5.15)

Note that we have

|\partial_{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)|\\
\leq e^{-\beta\bar{\kappa}_{a}t}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\Bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,|\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}|(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\,,

where
\lvert\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}\rvert=\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}+2\bar{\kappa}_{a}\mathcal{L}
.
Since we have reduced to the case where
\varphi^{\prime}
is bounded,
the expression above implies (cf.
[
13
, Prop. 2.5]
) that for any
\beta_{0}>0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\beta\in[0,\beta_{0}]}\Big|\partial_{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\varphi^{\prime}(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0))\Big|\right]<\infty.

(5.16)

This allows to interchange derivative and expectation and by (
5.15
), we obtain that

\begin{split}\partial_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\Big[\varphi\big(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\big)\Big]&=\mathbb{E}\Big[\partial_{\beta}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\,\varphi^{\prime}\big(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\big)\Big]\\
&=e^{-\beta\bar{\kappa}_{a}t}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}U^{\omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\Bigg]\,,\end{split}

(5.17)

where we have set

U_{k}^{\omega}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x}):=\Bigg(\int_{[(0,t)\setminus\{t_{i}\}^{k}_{i=1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)\varphi^{\prime}\big(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\big).

Given
(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z})\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\times(0,\infty)^{k}
, we let
{\omega}\cup(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z})
be the point process obtained by adding the
k
points
(t_{i},x_{i},z_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
to
{\omega}
.
By Mecke’s multivariate equation (see
[
77
, Thm. 4.4]
),
we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}U_{k}^{\omega}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi^{a,+}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\Bigg]\\
&\qquad=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\times(0,\infty)^{k}}U_{k}^{\omega}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[a,\infty)}(z_{i})\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})\Bigg]\\
&\qquad=\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\times(0,\infty)^{k}}\mathbb{E}\Big[U_{k}^{{\omega}\cup(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z})}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\Big]\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{[a,\infty)}(z_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}).\end{split}

(5.18)

If
k
,
\boldsymbol{t}
,
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{z}
are fixed,
the functionals

{\omega}\mapsto\varphi^{\prime}\big(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}\cup(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z}),a}_{\beta}(t,0)\big)\quad\text{ and }\quad{\omega}\mapsto\int_{[(0,t)\setminus\{t_{i}\}^{k}_{i=1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)

are non-decreasing for the inclusion order. Thus we can apply the FKG inequality for Poisson point processes (see
[
65
, Lemma 2.1]
) and obtain that

\mathbb{E}\Big[U^{{\omega}\cup(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z})}_{k}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\Big]\geq\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{[(0,t)\setminus\{t_{i}\}^{k}_{i=1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s,y\,|\,\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg]\mathbb{E}\Big[\varphi^{\prime}(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}\cup(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z}),a}_{\beta}(t,0))\Big]=0.

Combining this with (
5.17
) and (
5.18
), we conclude that
\partial_{\beta}\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi\big(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)\big)\big]\geq 0
.
∎

6.
Moments of order
p>1

The goal of this section is to formulate and prove Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
below,
which form the core of all moment upper bounds for
p>1
in this paper.

6.1.
The statements

Recall that
\nu_{p}:=1-\frac{d}{2}(p-1)
and let
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},0}_{\beta}(t,x):=\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)
.

6.1.1.
The case of dimension
d=1

We start with
d=1
, where the statement is easier to state (and easier to prove).

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume that
d=1
and that (
2.1
) holds.

(i)

If
\mu_{1,\infty}(2)<\infty
, then for any
a\in[0,1)
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-2}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{2}\Big]&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\frac{\beta^{2}\mu_{a,\infty}(2)\sqrt{t}}{2}\Bigg)^{k}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{{\Gamma}((k+1)/2)}\\
&=1+\beta^{2}\mu_{a,\infty}(2)\sqrt{\pi t}\exp\Big(\tfrac{1}{4}\beta^{4}\mu_{a,\infty}(2)^{2}t\Big)\Phi\Big(\beta^{2}\mu_{a,\infty}(2)\sqrt{\tfrac{t}{2}}\Big),\end{split}

(6.1)

where
\Phi
is the standard normal distribution function.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
such that if

\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for some
p\in(1,2)
, then for any value of
\eta\in(0,1]
,
a\in[0,1)
and
t,\beta>0
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sqrt{2}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{C\beta}{p-1}\bigg)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\Big(\mu_{0,\eta}(2)t^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big)^{\frac{k_{1}}{2}}\frac{(\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p)t^{\nu_{p}})^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}.

(6.2)

(iii)

There exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
such that if

\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for some
p\in(2,3)
, then for any value of
a\in[0,1)
and
t,\beta>0
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t&,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\leq\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\big(C\beta\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}t^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigg(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\big(C^{\prime}\beta\mu_{0,\infty}(p)^{\frac{1}{p}}t^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{\ell}}{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}(\ell+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}\Bigg)^{k_{2}+1}\,.\end{split}

(6.3)

Remark 6.2
.

The formula (
6.1
) is rather straightforward to prove. Starting from (
1.28
) and using that
\bar{\xi}_{{\omega}}^{a}
is centered, the second moment is given by

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-2}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{2}\Big]=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}({\beta}^{2}\mu_{a,\infty}(2))^{k}\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho(t,x)^{-2}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,,\end{split}

with the notation (
1.10
) for
\Delta t_{i}
and
\Delta x_{i}
.
Integrating over
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we get

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-2}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{2}\Big]=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}({\beta}^{2}\mu_{a,\infty}(2))^{k}(2\sqrt{\pi t})\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\Delta t_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,,\end{split}

which gives the first equality in (
6.1
) by Lemma
4.5
. The second equality follows from the formula
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}/\Gamma((k+1)/2)=\pi^{-1/2}+2xe^{x^{2}}\Phi(\sqrt{2}x)
. Let us also mention that this formula coincides with the one we obtain if
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
in (
6.1
) is replaced by the solution to the SHE (
1.1
) with a space-time
Gaussian
noise with variance
\mu_{a,\infty}(2)
(and
u_{0}=\delta_{0}
); cf.
[
30
, Eq. (2.31)]
.

6.1.2.
The case of dimension
d\geq 2

For the statement with
d\geq 2
, we need to introduce a few auxiliary quantities.
With the usual convention
t_{0}:=0
and
t_{k+1}:=t
and
\Delta t_{i}:=t_{i}-t_{i-1}
, we define
\Lambda(0,t,p):=1
and

\Lambda(k,t,p):=t^{1-\nu_{p}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}

(6.4)

for
k\geq 1
, where

G_{p}(s):=\begin{cases}s^{\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p}-1}\quad&\text{ if }t\leq 1,\\
s^{\nu_{p}-1}&\text{ if }t\geq 1.\end{cases}

(6.5)

Also, we let

\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t):=\Big(8\,\mu^{\log}_{0,\eta}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big)(1+\log_{+}t)\Big)^{\frac{p}{1+2/d}},\qquad\zeta_{2}(\eta,p):=\mu_{0,\eta}^{\log}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big)+\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p),

(6.6)

where we have set
\log_{+}t:=\log(t\vee 1)
and

\mu_{0,\eta}^{\log}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big):=\int_{(0,\eta)}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}(3\lvert\log z\rvert+1){\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)

Note that both
\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)
and
\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)
are finite if (
2.1
) holds and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Assume that
d\geq 2
and that (
2.1
) holds.
There exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
that only depends on
d
such that if

\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
, then for any value of
\eta\in(0,1]
,
a\in[0,1)
and
t,\beta>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{C\beta}{p-1}\bigg)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{\frac{k_{1}}{p}}\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(6.7)

Remark 6.4
.

In our proofs of Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
, we keep track of the dependence in
p
. The reason for this is that in the proof of Theorem
2.11
—more precisely in the proof of (
2.16
)—we need to apply (
6.7
) for
p
that depends of
\beta
. The only important point we need to make sure is that our estimates remain uniform for
p
in an interval around
1+\frac{2}{d}
.

6.1.3.
Finiteness of the moments

An almost immediate consequence of the proposition is the following uniform moment bound.

Corollary 6.5
.

If (
2.1
) holds and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for some
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
, then for any
\beta
and
T
there exists
C(\beta,p,T)
such that

\sup_{a\in[0,1)}\sup_{(t,x)\in(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\rho(t,x)^{-1}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)\Big)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}<C(\beta,p,T).

Proof.

First let us note that the bounds in Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
already are uniform in
a
and
x
, so we only need to check uniformity in
t
.

Let us start with the case
d=1
. The statement for
p=2
is obvious from (
6.1
). If
p\in(1,2)
, by reorganizing (
6.2
),
we obtain that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C^{\prime}\Bigg(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\Big(C_{p}\beta\mu_{0,\eta}(2)^{\frac{1}{2}}T^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big)^{k_{1}}\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Big(C_{p}\beta\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p)^{\frac{1}{p}}T^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}}\Big)^{k_{2}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}\Bigg),

where
C_{p}=C/(p-1)
.
The first sum is finite if one chooses
\eta
such that
C\beta\mu_{0,\eta}(2)^{1/2}T^{1/4}<\frac{1}{2}
and the second one is always finite since
\Gamma(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))
grows super-exponentially. If
p\in(2,3)
,
we have from (
6.3
) that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C_{p}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\big(C_{p}\beta T^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigg(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\big(C^{\prime}_{p}\beta T^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}}\big)^{\ell}}{\Gamma(\nu_{p}(\ell+1))}\Bigg)^{k_{2}+1}\,.

Since
\Gamma(\nu_{p}(\ell+1))
and
\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})
both grow super-exponentially, the two sums are finite.

When
d\geq 2
, since
\zeta_{1}
is monotone in
t
, we have, in the same manner,

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\Biggl(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\Bigl(C_{p}\beta\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,T)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Bigr)^{k_{1}}\Biggr)\Biggl(\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\Bigl(C_{p}\beta\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Bigr)^{k_{2}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Biggr)

(6.8)

for
t\leq T
.
The first sum is finite provided that
\eta
is chosen sufficiently small.
Considering the second term, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)

\displaystyle\leq t^{1-\nu_{p}}(t\vee 1)^{\frac{2}{3}\nu_{p}k_{2}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}(\Delta t_{k_{2}+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{2}}(\Delta t_{i})^{\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p}-1}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}

\displaystyle=(t\vee 1)^{\frac{2}{3}\nu_{p}k_{2}}t^{\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p}k_{2}}\frac{{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p})^{k_{2}}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})}{{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p}(k_{2}+3))}\,,

where we used Lemma
4.5
for the last identity.
As a result, assuming that
T\geq 1
, we obtain that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\Big(C_{p}\beta\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big)^{k_{2}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\Big(C_{p}\beta\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{\frac{1}{p}}{\Gamma}({\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}T^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}}\Big)^{k_{2}}\frac{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}}{{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p}(k_{2}+3))^{\frac{1}{p}}},

and since
{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}[(k_{2}/3)+1])
grows super-exponentially, the sum over
k_{2}
in (
6.8
) is finite.
∎

6.2.
Bounding moments in the chaos expansion: the first term

From now on, we focus on the case
x=0
, which yields no loss of generality by (
1.18
).
Also, in both Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
above, the case
a=0
can be deduced from the case
a>0
using Fatou’s lemma: in the following, we can always assume that
a>0
.
Starting from the chaos decomposition (
1.28
), we can use Minkowski’s inequality to get

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[|W_{a,k}(t)|^{p}\right]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,,

(6.9)

where

W_{a,0}(t):=1,\qquad W_{a,k}(t):=\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}{\rho(t,0)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\quad\text{ for }k\geq 1.

(6.10)

The estimates for
\mathbb{E}[|W_{a,k}(t)|^{p}]
are intricate, so let us spend some time on the case
k=1
to illustrate the intuition behind our proof.
In what follows, we write

{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}:={\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(0,\infty)^{k},\quad X^{(k)}_{t}:={\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k},\quad\mathbb{X}^{(k)}_{t}:={\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\times(0,\infty)^{k}.

(6.11)

We drop the superscript
k
when
k=1
.
For simplicity, let us consider the expansion of the free-end partition function.
Because the integrals in (
6.9
) are martingales in
t
for the filtration (
1.5
), we can apply the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy (BDG) inequality and obtain

\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s,x)\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)\Biggr)^{p}\Biggr]\\
\leq C_{p}\,\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[a,\infty)}(\rho(s,x)z)^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z)\Biggr)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Biggr].

(6.12)

Since we are tracking the dependence in
p
,
it is worth noting that it is possible to take
C_{p}=(4p)^{p}\leq 64
if
p\leq 2
; see
[
49
, Chapter VII, Theorem 92]
.
In order to bound the right-hand side uniformly in
a
, we replace
[a,\infty)
by
(0,\infty)
.
Let us further restrict ourselves to the case
d\geq 3
for simplicity (note that in particular
p<2
).
By Jensen’s inequality and the subadditivity property (
5.4
), we have,
for
\theta\in[p,2]
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\rho(s,x)^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]&\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\rho(s,x)^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{\theta}{2}}\Bigg]^{\frac{p}{\theta}}\\
&\leq\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\rho(s,x)^{\theta}z^{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{\theta}}\\
&=\vartheta(\theta)^{\frac{p}{\theta}}\bigg(\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}}s^{\nu_{\theta}-1}z^{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}s\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{\theta}}\,,\end{split}

(6.13)

recalling also (
5.5
) and
\nu_{\theta}=1-\frac{d}{2}(\theta-1)
for the last line.
On the right-hand side, we see that when
\theta
increases, the integrability in
z
around
0
improves but the one in
s
worsens, forcing us to chose
\nu_{\theta}>0
(
i.e.
,
\theta<1+\frac{2}{d}
) to obtain a finite integral. As a consequence, we need to assume
\mu_{0,\infty}(\theta)<\infty
for some
\theta\in[p,1+\frac{2}{d})
. If this holds, then, in fact, the same estimate can be applied iteratively in order to obtain bounds for any of the multiple integrals in (
6.9
). Let us remark that this is essentially the assumption (and the method) used in
[
89
]
to obtain existence, uniqueness and moments for the solution to (
2.6
).

Clearly,
\mu_{0,\infty}(\theta)<\infty
does not hold, for any
\theta>0
, if
\xi_{\omega}
is an
\alpha
-stable noise with
{\alpha}\in(1,2)
; recall that we assume
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for some
p>1
. In that case, at least when
k=1
, it is easy to do better than (
6.13
). The
key point is to first separate
z\geq 1
and
z<1
and then, for
z<1
, further whether
z\leq s^{d/2}
or
z>s^{d/2}
. For
z\geq 1
, we can simply apply (
6.13
) with
\theta=p
, that is,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\rho(s,x)^{2}z^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\geq 1\}}\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]\leq\vartheta(\theta)\mu_{1,\infty}(p)\nu_{p}^{-1}t^{\nu_{p}}\,.

For
z\leq 1
, we first consider the contribution coming from
z\leq s^{d/2}
. By
Jensen’s inequality, we can take the exponent
\frac{p}{2}
outside the expectation on the right-hand side of (
6.12
), which leads to the bound

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\leq s^{d/2}\wedge 1\}}\rho(s,x)^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z)\Bigg]^{\frac{p}{2}}=\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\leq s^{d/2}\wedge 1\}}\rho(s,x)^{2}z^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\Bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}.

Since
d\geq 3
, we have, integrating first with respect to
x
(recall (
5.5
)), then with respect to
s
and finally with respect to
z
,

\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\leq s^{d/2}\wedge 1\}}\rho(s,x)^{2}z^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}}\frac{z^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\leq s^{d/2}\wedge 1\}}}{(4\pi s)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\leq\frac{1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1)(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\mu_{0,1}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big).

For the contribution to the integral coming from
z>s^{d/2}
,
applying (
6.13
) with
\theta=p
,
we get that it is smaller than

\displaystyle\vartheta(p)\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z>s^{d/2},z<1\}}s^{\nu_{p}-1}z^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\frac{\vartheta(p)}{\nu_{p}}\int_{(0,1)}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\frac{\vartheta(p)}{\nu_{p}}\mu_{0,1}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big)\,,

where we have first integrated with respect to
s
, using that
\nu_{p}=1-\frac{d}{2}(p-1)>0
for
p<1+\frac{2}{d}
, and then with respect to
z
.
Altogether, we have shown the following bound.

Lemma 6.6
.

In dimension
d\geq 3
, there exists a constant
C
(which may depend on
d
but not on
p
) such that for all
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s,x)\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)\bigg)^{p}\Bigg]\leq\frac{C}{\nu_{p}}\Big[\mu_{0,1}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}+\mu_{0,1}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{d}\Big)+\mu_{1,\infty}(p)t^{\nu_{p}}\Big].

In other words, the single integral has a finite
p
th moment for some
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
if the intensity measure satisfies
\mu_{0,1}(1+\frac{2}{d})+\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
. In fact, this condition is necessary and sufficient by
[
87
, Theorem 3.3]
, so Lemma
6.6
is optimal for single integrals.

6.3.
Decoupling and partitioning: Key tools in proving Propositions
6.1
and
6.3

6.3.1.
Decoupling

When
k\geq 2
, there is no direct analogue of (
6.12
) since we cannot apply the BDG inequality for the
k
-fold iterated integral.
The first step of our proof is to use the decoupling inequalities from Section
4.2
in order to obtain, instead of
W_{a,k}(t)
, multiple Poisson integrals with respect to
k
independent copies of the original noise. An important advantage of the decoupled integral is that we can change the order of integration without losing the martingale property.
Using Theorem
4.3

with the tetrahedral function

f(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z}):=\rho(t,0)^{-1}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq a\}}
(the reader can check that (
4.3
) is satisfied whenever
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
), we have

\mathbb{E}[\lvert W_{a,k}(t)\rvert^{p}]\leq\bigg(\frac{C}{p-1}\bigg)^{k}\,\mathbb{E}^{\otimes k}[\lvert V_{a,k}(t)\rvert^{p}]\,,

(6.14)

where

\begin{split}V_{a,k}(t):=&~\int_{X_{t}^{(k)}}\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}{{\rho(t,0)}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}_{i}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\\
=&~\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k)}}\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}{{\rho(t,0)}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq a\}}\,(\delta_{{\omega}_{i}}-\nu)(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})\end{split}

(6.15)

and
{\omega}_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,k
, are i.i.d. copies of
{\omega}
. For simplicity, and with a small abuse of notation, we write
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{P}^{\otimes k}
,
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\otimes k}
in the remainder of the proof.

6.3.2.
Partitioning and integrating over space

As in Section
6.2
, we want to estimate the
p
th moment of the right-hand side of (
6.15
)
using a combination of the BDG inequality, Jensen’s inequality and subadditivity and then integrate over space.
Because we want to use an intermediate exponent as in (
6.13
) that depends on the value of
\boldsymbol{t}
and
\boldsymbol{z}
, a first task is to decompose

V_{a,k}(t)
by considering a (non-random) partition
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
of the parameter space
{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}
.
For each element
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
of our partition, we will further determine a partition
J_{1}(\mathcal{P})\cup J_{2}(\mathcal{P})
of
\llbracket k\rrbracket
and first integrate with respect to
(t_{i},z_{i})
with
i\in J_{1}(\mathcal{P})
and then with respect to
(t_{i},z_{i})
with
i\in J_{2}(\mathcal{P})
.
We let
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
denote the respective cardinalities of
J_{1}=J_{1}({\mathcal{P}})
and
J_{2}=J_{2}({\mathcal{P}})
.

When
J\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket
, we use the following notation:

\Delta_{J}t_{i}:=t_{i}-t_{i(J,-1)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{J}x_{i}:=x_{i}-x_{i(J,-1)}

(6.16)

where
x_{0}=x_{k+1}=0
,
t_{0}=0
and
t_{k+1}=t
as usual
and where for
i\in J\cup\{k+1\}
we denote the predecessor of
i
in
J\cup\{0,k+1\}
by
i(J,-1)
.
As a result of our partitioning procedure, we obtain the following estimate:

Lemma 6.7
.

Given
p\in(1,2\wedge(1+\frac{2}{d}))
and
\theta\in(p,2]
, we have for all
a\in(0,1]
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\lvert V_{a,k}(t)\big\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C^{k}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}}t^{\frac{1-\nu_{p}}{p}}\Bigg(&\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}^{(k_{2})}}\Biggl(\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}^{(k_{1})}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{\theta}-1}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}z^{\theta}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\Biggr)^{\frac{p}{\theta}}\\
&\times\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{(\nu_{p}-1)+\frac{p}{\theta}(1-\nu_{\theta})}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}z^{p}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}.\end{split}

(6.17)

Proof.

For
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
, let us define

V_{a,k}(t,\mathcal{P}):=\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k)}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}{{\rho(t,0)}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq a\}}\,(\delta_{{\omega}_{i}}-\nu)(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})\,.

(6.18)

Then, by (
6.15
) and Minkowski’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\lvert V_{a,k}(t)\big\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\lvert V_{a,k}(t,\mathcal{P})\big\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Because the
{\omega}_{i}
’s are independent, we can, similarly to (
A.4
) in the appendix, permute the integrals in (
6.15
) and integrate with respect to the indices in
J_{1}
first and
J_{2}
afterwards. In conjunction with the BDG inequality, subadditivity (recall
p\leq 2
) and Jensen’s inequality (applied in the fashion as in (
6.13
)), we obtain

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\lvert V_{a,k}(t,\mathcal{P})\rvert^{p}]\\
&\qquad\leq\begin{multlined}C^{k_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{2})}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{1})}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}{{\rho(t,0)}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq a\}}\,(\delta_{{\omega}_{i}}-\nu)(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]\\
\times\prod_{i\in J_{2}}z_{i}^{p}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq a\}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\end{multlined}\\
&\qquad\leq C^{k}\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{2})}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{1})}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\mathbf{z})\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})^{\theta}}{\rho(t,0)^{\theta}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}z^{\theta}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\bigg)^{\frac{p}{\theta}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}z^{p}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}).\end{split}

(6.19)

Remark 6.8
.

In the above integrals and for the remainder of the proof, with a small abuse of notation, the coordinates of elements of
\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{1})}
are indexed by
J_{1}
instead of
\llbracket k_{1}\rrbracket
(and similarly for
J_{2}
). With this convention,

\mathbb{X}^{(k)}_{t}
is a strict subset of
\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{1})}\times\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k_{2})}
, but this is not a problem since the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}
restricts the integral to a subset of
\mathbb{X}^{(k)}_{t}
.

The next step is to carry out
integration with respect to
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
explicitly.
First of all, notice that for any
m\geq 1
and any
p>1
, we have for every
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathfrak{X}_{m}(s)

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}}\rho_{s,y}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})^{p}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\vartheta(p)^{m}\rho(s,y)^{p}s^{1-\nu_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}\,.

(6.20)

This can be proved by induction on
m
after checking the case
m=1
by hand, using (
5.5
); we leave the details to the reader.

We then successively apply (
6.20
) with
p=\theta

to the segments of
J_{1}
(i.e., maximal sets of consecutive indices in
J_{1}
). Recalling the notation (
6.16
), we get

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k_{1}}}\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})^{\theta}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\vartheta(\theta)^{k_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{\theta}-1}\prod_{j\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{j})^{1-\nu_{\theta}}\rho(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{j},\Delta_{J_{2}}x_{j})^{\theta}\,.

(6.21)

Applying (
6.20
) in the case
y=0
,
we also get

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k_{2}}}\prod_{j\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}\rho(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{j},\Delta_{J_{2}}x_{j})^{p}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\vartheta(p)^{k_{2}}\rho(t,0)^{p}t^{1-\nu_{p}}\prod_{j\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{j})^{\nu_{p}-1}\,.

(6.22)

We conclude the proof of Lemma
6.7
by inserting (
6.21
) and (
6.22
) in (
6.19
).
∎

6.4.
The proof of Proposition
6.1

The second moment was computed in Remark
6.2
. In order to prove (
6.2
),
we only need to separate small and large values of
z
.
We consider a partition
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
indexed by the subsets
J\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket
and we define

\mathcal{P}(J):=\{(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}\ :\ z_{i}<\eta\text{ for all }i\in J\text{ and }z_{i}\geq\eta\text{ for all }i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket\setminus J\}

(6.23)

as well as
J_{1}:=J
and
J_{2}:=\llbracket k\rrbracket\setminus J
.
Applying Lemma
6.7
with
\theta=2
and noting that
\nu_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\nu_{p}=1-\frac{1}{2}(p-1)
in dimension
d=1
, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\Big[\big\lvert V_{a,k}(t)\big\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C^{k}\sum_{J\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket}t^{\frac{1-\nu_{p}}{p}}\mu_{0,\eta}(2)^{\frac{k_{1}}{2}}\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p)^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}\\
&\times\Bigg(\int_{(0,t)^{k_{2}}}\Biggl(\int_{(0,t)^{k_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\Biggr)^{\frac{p}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\frac{2-p}{4}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}.\end{split}

(6.24)

Integrating successively over the segments of
J_{1}

and writing
\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)=i-i(J,-1)
for the distance between
i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}
and the previous index in
J_{2}
(recall (
6.16
)),
we get from Lemma
4.5

that

\int_{(0,t)^{k_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\boldsymbol{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}})\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)-1}\frac{{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{2})^{\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)}}{{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i))}\,.

(6.25)

Now, if we bound
(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{(\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)-1)/2}\leq t^{(\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)-1)/2}
and
{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{2}\ell)\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\pi}
for any
\ell\geq 1
, we get that

\int_{(0,t)^{k_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\boldsymbol{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}})2^{k_{2}+1}(\pi t)^{\frac{1}{2}k_{1}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Going back to (
6.24
) and taking the factor
[(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{-1/2}]^{p/2}
over to the outer integral, we compute

\int_{(0,t)^{k_{2}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}})\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\frac{1-p}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}=t^{\nu_{p}k_{2}+\nu_{p}-1}\frac{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{k_{2}+1}}{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))}\,,

(6.26)

thanks again to Lemma
4.5
.
We therefore conclude that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\lvert V_{a,k}(t)\big\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}&\leq\sum_{J\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket}\Big(\sqrt{\pi t}\mu_{0,\eta}(2)\Big)^{\frac{k_{1}}{2}}\Big(2^{\frac{p}{2}}\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p)t^{\nu_{p}}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})\Big)^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}\frac{\sqrt{2}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}}{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}\\
&\leq(2C)^{k}\max_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\Big(\sqrt{\pi t}\mu_{0,\eta}(2)\Big)^{\frac{k_{1}}{2}}\Big(2^{\frac{p}{2}}\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p)t^{\nu_{p}}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})\Big)^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}\frac{\sqrt{2}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}}{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}.\end{split}

(6.27)

Absorbing
\pi^{k_{1}/4}
and
(2^{p/2}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}))^{k_{2}/p}
, which is uniformly bounded in
p\in[1,2]
, into the constant
C
and replacing the maximum by a sum, we derive (
6.2
) from
(
6.9
), (
6.14
) and (
6.27
).

If
p\in(2,3)
, the subadditivity argument in (
6.19
) does not apply. Instead, we shall use a variant of the BDG estimate (
6.12
) that only contains the intensity measure
\nu:=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}x\otimes{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)
instead of
\delta_{\omega}
.
For any
p\geq 2
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
such that for all
{\mathcal{P}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))
-measurable process
K=K({\omega},w)=K({\omega},t,x,z)
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}}K(w)\,(\delta_{\omega}-\nu)(\mathrm{d}w)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]\leq C^{\prime}_{p}\left\{\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{X}}K(w)^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}w)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]+\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{X}}\lvert K(w)\rvert^{p}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}w)\Bigg]\right\},

(6.28)

where
C^{\prime}_{p}=(2^{p-1}p(p-1)(p/(p-1))^{p})^{p/2}\vee 2(p/(p-1))^{p}(2^{p}+1+p)\leq 729
when
p\in[2,3]
;
see
[
85
, Theorem 1 (b)]
(and its proof for the value of
C^{\prime}_{p}
). We also refer to
[
79
]
for a survey of various versions and proofs (and names) of this inequality.
Let us define

\widetilde{W}_{a,0}(t,x):=\rho(t,x),\qquad\widetilde{W}_{a,k}(t,x):=\int_{X^{(k)}_{t}}{\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\xi}^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\quad\text{ for }k\geq 1,

so that in particular
W_{a,k}(t)=\widetilde{W}_{a,k}(t,0)/\rho(t,0)
.
With this definition, applying (
6.28
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[|\widetilde{W}_{a,k}(t,x)|^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg|\int_{X_{t}}\rho(t-t_{k},0-x_{k})\widetilde{W}_{a,k-1}(t_{k},x_{k})\,\bar{\xi}^{a}_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t_{k},\mathrm{d}x_{k})\bigg|^{p}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\,\Bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\mu_{0,\infty}(2)\int_{X_{t}}(\rho(t-t_{k},x_{k})\widetilde{W}_{a,k-1}(t_{k},x_{k}))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t_{k}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k}\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\bigg(\mu_{0,\infty}(p)\int_{X_{t}}\rho(t-t_{k},x_{k})^{p}\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{W}_{a,k-1}(t_{k},x_{k})|^{p}]\,\mathrm{d}t_{k}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Bigg\}\,.

Thus, applying Minkowski’s integral inequality,
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[|\widetilde{W}_{a,k}(t,x)|^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\Bigg\{\,\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\int_{X_{t}}\rho(t-t_{k},x_{k})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\lvert\widetilde{W}_{a,k-1}(t_{k},x_{k})\rvert^{p}]^{\frac{2}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{k}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mu_{0,\infty}(p)^{\frac{1}{p}}\bigg(\int_{X_{t}}\rho(t-t_{k},x_{k})^{p}\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{W}_{a,k-1}(t_{k},x_{k})|^{p}]\,\mathrm{d}t_{k}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Bigg\}\,.

Repeating this estimate and recalling (
6.9
), we get

\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\rho(t,0)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(C^{\prime}\beta)^{k}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\{2,p\}^{k}}\mu_{0,\infty}(p)^{\frac{k_{1}}{p}}\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{\frac{k_{2}}{2}}\lVert\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\theta},t},

(6.29)

where
k_{1}
is the number of
p
’s and
k_{2}
the number of
2
’s in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
,
and where we have defined

\lVert f\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\theta},t}:=\bigg(\int_{X_{t}}\bigg(\cdots\bigg(\int_{X_{t}}\bigg(\int_{X_{t}}f(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})^{\theta_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\bigg)^{\frac{\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\bigg)^{\frac{\theta_{3}}{\theta_{2}}}\cdots\bigg)^{\frac{\theta_{k}}{\theta_{k-1}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{k}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\theta_{k}}}

for
f:((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}\to[0,\infty)
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\in[1,\infty)^{k}
.

Now in order to conclude, we want to replace
\lVert\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\theta},t}
by an integral which is analogous to that found in the on the right-hand side of (
6.19
). To do so, we use the following identity, valid for any positive function
f
and any measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
:

\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}f(w_{1},w_{2})^{2}\,\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}w_{2})\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}f(w_{1},w_{2})^{p}\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\,\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}w_{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

This inequality is a special case of
[
73
, Lemma 3.3.1]
. Now, letting
J_{1}
denote the set of indices for which
\theta_{i}=p
and
J_{2}
those for which
\theta_{i}=2
,
we apply the above inequality iteratively to take all the integrals with respect to
J_{1}
inside. We obtain (recall Remark
6.8
)

\lVert\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\theta},t}\leq\bigg(\int_{X_{t}^{(k_{2})}}\bigg(\int_{X_{t}^{(k_{1})}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\boldsymbol{t})\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})^{p}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{p}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.30)

Now we can first integrate with respect to the
x_{i}
’s using (
6.21
) and (
6.22
) (with
p
instead of
\theta
and
2
instead of
p
). Recalling that
\nu_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
, we obtain

\lVert\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\theta},t}\leq\vartheta(p)^{\frac{k_{1}}{p}}\vartheta(2)^{\frac{k_{2}}{2}}\rho(t,0)\\
\times t^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg(\int_{(0,t)^{k_{2}}}\bigg(\int_{(0,1)^{k_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\boldsymbol{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{p}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\frac{2}{p}(1-\nu_{p})-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using Lemma
4.5
to integrate first with respect to
(t_{i})_{i\in J_{1}}
,
we get as in (
6.25
)

\begin{split}\int_{(0,1)^{k_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\boldsymbol{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}&=\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}})\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}\frac{(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{p}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)-1}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)}}{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i))}\\
&\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}}){\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{k+1}t^{\nu_{p}k_{1}}\frac{\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}}{\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i))}\,,\end{split}

(6.31)

because
(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{p}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)-1}\leq t^{\nu_{p}(\Delta_{J_{2}}(i)-1)}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}
. Pulling
(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}
to the outer integral, we evaluate

\displaystyle\int_{(0,t)^{k_{2}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}})\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}=t^{\frac{k_{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})^{k_{2}+1}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})}\,,

using once more Lemma
4.5
.
Altogether, bounding
\vartheta(p),\vartheta(2)\leq 1
, we obtain that

\lVert\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\rVert_{\boldsymbol{\theta},t}\leq\rho(t,0)t^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}k_{1}+\frac{1}{4}k_{2}}\frac{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}(k+1)}\pi^{\frac{1}{4}(k_{2}+1)}}{{\Gamma}(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i))^{\frac{1}{p}}}\,.

Going back to (
6.29
), bounding
\vartheta(p),\vartheta(2)\leq 1
and expressing everything in terms of
J_{2}
and
k_{2}=\lvert J_{2}\rvert
, we get that
\mathbb{E}[\rho(t,0)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}]^{1/p}
is bounded by

\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{J_{2}\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket}\frac{\big(C^{\prime}\beta\mu_{0,\infty}(p)^{\frac{1}{p}}t^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{k_{1}}\big(C^{\prime}\beta\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i))^{\frac{1}{p}}}\\
=\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k}\frac{\big(C^{\prime}\beta\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\pi^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k_{2}+1}\geq 1\\
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{k_{2}+1}=k+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{2}+1}\frac{\big(C^{\prime}\beta\mu_{0,\infty}(p)^{\frac{1}{p}}t^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\big)^{\ell_{i}-1}}{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p}\ell_{i})^{\frac{1}{p}}}\,,

where we have used a change of variable and the fact that
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{k_{2}+1}=k+1
implies that
k_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}+1}(\ell_{i}-1)
.
Exchanging the first two sums and factorizing the last one yields (
6.3
).
∎

6.5.
Intermezzo:
d\geq 2
under stronger moment conditions

The method we have used for
d=1
can further be used to prove boundedness of moments under the more restrictive assumption given in
(
1.21
).
We illustrate this by Proposition
6.9
below whose proof can be achieved by replicating that of (
6.2
).
This quantitative estimate is sufficient to prove the upper bound parts of Theorem
2.12
and similar ideas could in principle be used to prove Theorem
2.5
under the more restrictive assumption (
1.21
). The more involved method used in the proof of Proposition
6.3
below, however, is necessary to bridge the gap between the conditions (
1.21
) and (
1.23
); recall that the latter is optimal in dimension
2
and very close to optimal when
d\geq 3
.

Proposition 6.9
.

There exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
that only depends on
d
such that if

d\geq 2
,
\mu_{0,1}(q)<\infty
and
\mu_{1,\infty}(p)<\infty
for some
1<p\leq q<1+\frac{2}{d}
, we have, for all
a\in[0,1]
,

\eta\in(0,1]
and
\beta,t>0
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{C\Gamma(\nu_{q})\beta}{p-1}\bigg)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(\mu_{0,\eta}(q)t^{\nu_{q}})^{\frac{k_{1}}{q}}\frac{({\Gamma}(\nu_{p})\mu_{\eta,\infty}(p)t^{\nu_{p}})^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}\Gamma(\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{p}(k_{2}+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}.

Proof.

Let us give a very short guideline for the proof: we use the same partition
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
as in (
6.23
)
and apply Lemma
6.7
with
\theta=q
.
The proof is identical to that of Proposition
6.1

(ii)
except that (
6.25
)
has to be replaced by (
6.31
), which together with the bound
\Gamma(\nu_{q}\Delta_{J_{2}}(i))\geq\frac{1}{2}
gives

\int_{(0,t)^{k_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}(\boldsymbol{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{q}-1}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{2}}(t)}((t_{i})_{i\in J_{2}})\,2^{k_{2}+1}\Gamma(\nu_{q})^{k}t^{\nu_{q}k_{1}}\prod_{i\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{q}-1}\,.\qed

6.6.
The proof of Proposition
6.3

We use the same idea as for the proof of Proposition
6.1
.
We will consider a partition
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
(defined below)
of the parameter space
\mathcal{X}_{t}^{(k)}
:
each element
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
induces a partition of
\llbracket k\rrbracket
into two sets
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
and we are going to use Lemma
6.7
with
\theta=1+\frac{2}{d}
(and hence
\nu_{\theta}=0
).
The main technical part of this section will then be to bound

\begin{multlined}U(\mathcal{P},t):=t^{{1-\nu_{p}}}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}^{(k_{2})}}\Bigg(\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}^{(k_{1})}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\mathbf{z})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(\Delta t_{i})^{-1}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\Bigg)^{\frac{p}{1+2/d}}\\
\times\prod_{j\in J_{2}\cup\{k+1\}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{j})^{{\frac{p}{1+2/d}}+\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}z^{p}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\,.\end{multlined}

(6.32)

The following is the main technical estimate of this section.

Proposition 6.10
.

Recall (
6.4
) and (
6.6
).
With the choice
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
defined in Section
6.6.1
below (see in particular (
6.37
)),
we have for every
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
,
\eta\in(0,1]
and
t>0
that

\begin{split}U(\mathcal{P},t)\leq\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{k_{1}}\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{k_{2}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p),\end{split}

(6.33)

where
k_{1}:=\lvert J_{1}\rvert
and
k_{2}:=\lvert J_{2}\rvert
.

Proposition
6.3
follows immediately from Proposition
6.10
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Using Lemma
6.7
, combined with (
6.14
) and (
6.15
), we obtain that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[W_{a,k}(t)^{p}]^{\frac{1}{p}}&\leq\bigg(\frac{C}{p-1}\bigg)^{k}\lvert\mathfrak{P}_{k}\rvert\max_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}}U(\mathcal{P},t)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\leq 2\bigg(\frac{9C}{p-1}\bigg)^{k}\max_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\Big(\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{k_{1}}\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{k_{2}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,,\end{split}

(6.34)

where we have used that
\lvert\mathfrak{P}_{k}\rvert\leq 2\times 9^{k}
(see below).
Replacing the
\max
by a sum yields (
6.7
).
∎

6.6.1.
Constructing the partition
\mathfrak{P}_{k}

The construction of
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
when
d\geq 2
is considerably more involved,
as it no longer suffices to only differentiate between
z_{i}\geq\eta
and
z_{i}<\eta
as we did for
d=1
. Recall that
in Section
6.2
, in order to obtain optimal bounds, we had to split the integral according to how
z_{i}
and
\Delta t_{i}^{d/2}
compare to each other. A problem arises when trying to generalize this method to
k\geq 2
:
On the right-hand side of (
6.32
),
the value of
\Delta t_{i}
plays the same role as
\Delta t_{i+1}
, which is the reason why we cannot break down the
k
-fold integral into
2^{k}
parts as we did in the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We now describe our solution to this problem.
To give the idea behind our partition and its link to the bound (
6.33
), recall from (
6.32
) that
we first integrate with respect to the variables with indices in
J_{1}
and then with respect to those with indices in
J_{2}
.
Loosely speaking, indices in
J_{1}
correspond to values of

z_{i}
that are small compared to
(\Delta t_{i})^{d/6}
and
(\Delta t_{i+1})^{d/6}
; this gives rise to a factor
\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{k_{1}}
that can be explained by the calculations in (
6.40
)–(
6.41
) below.
Note that after integrating with respect to such an index,
one may have to update the set of parameters since for the next step one has to compare
z_{i}
with time increments formed between the remaining variables. These may differ from
\Delta t_{i}
and
\Delta t_{i+1}
, which explains why the partition has to be defined iteratively.

Each element of our partition
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
is encoded by a finite sequence
(L^{j},I^{j}_{-},I^{j}_{+},D^{j})^{m}_{j=1}
of partitions of
\llbracket k+1\rrbracket
;
the length
m
of the sequence is a variable. With some abuse of notation, we identify
\mathcal{P}
with this sequence. We also use the notation
I^{j}:=I^{j}_{-}\cup I^{j}_{+}
.
Not every sequence is admissible, so let us present the rules for constructing the set of admissible sequences:

•

First we partition
\llbracket k+1\rrbracket
into three sets
L^{1}
,
I^{1}_{+}
, and
I^{1}_{-}
, imposing that
k+1\in I^{1}
, and we define
D^{1}:=\emptyset
.

•

The procedure is then iterative. Assume that
m\geq j
and that one has constructed the sets of the first
j
steps. We let

i(+1,j)
denote the successor of
i
and
i(-1,j)
denote the predecessor of
i
in
L^{j}\cup I^{j}_{-}\cup I^{j}_{+}
, that is,

\begin{split}i(+1,j)&:=\min\{\ell\in L^{j}\cup I^{j}_{-}\cup I^{j}_{+}\ :\ \ell\geq i+1\}\,,\\
i(-1,j)&:=\max\{\ell\in L^{j}\cup I^{j}_{-}\cup I^{j}_{+}\ :\ \ell\leq i-1\}\,,\end{split}

(6.35)

with the convention
\max\emptyset=0
; we only use the notation
i(+1,j)
if the set over which the minimum is taken is non-empty.
Unless

L^{j}_{\times}:=\{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket\ :\ i\in L^{j}\text{ and }i(+1,j)\in I_{-}^{j}\}=\emptyset\,,

(6.36)

we need to add extra terms corresponding to
j+1
to our sequence, which will be described in the next point. This procedure is repeated until at some stage
j
, (
6.36
) is satisfied.
We then define
m=j
and our sequence is complete.

•

When
L^{j}_{\times}\neq\emptyset
, the next sets
(L^{j+1},I^{j+1}_{-},I^{j+1}_{+},D^{j+1})
are only partially determined by
(L^{j},I^{j}_{-},I^{j}_{+},D^{j})
: while we prescribe the choice

D^{j+1}:=D^{j}\cup L^{j}_{\times}
, there is some liberty for choosing the sets

L^{j+1}
,
I^{j+1}_{-}
and
I^{j+1}_{+}
.
We set

I^{j}_{\times}:=\{i(+1,j)\ :\ i\in L^{j}_{\times}\}

and consider an arbitrary partition
\{I^{(j+1)}_{-},I^{(j+1)}_{+},L^{(j+1)}\}
of

I^{j}_{\times}
subject to only one constraint: if
k+1\in I^{j}_{\times}
, then

k+1\in I^{(j+1)}_{-}\cup I^{(j+1)}_{+}
(this is to guarantee that
k+1\in I^{j}
for all
j
). We then
define
L^{j+1}
,
I^{j+1}_{-}
and
I^{j+1}_{+}

as follows:

\left\{\begin{aligned} L^{j+1}&:=(L^{j}\setminus L^{j}_{\times})\cup L^{(j+1)},\\
I^{j+1}_{-}&:=(I^{j}_{-}\setminus I^{j}_{\times})\cup I^{(j+1)}_{-},\\
I^{j+1}_{+}&:=I^{j}_{+}\cup I^{(j+1)}_{+}.\end{aligned}\right.

Given an element in
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
, we further define

J_{1}:=D^{m}\cup\{i\in L^{m}\ :\ i(+1,m)\in L^{m}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad J_{2}:=\llbracket k\rrbracket\setminus J_{1}.

An example of an admissible sequence of partitions as well as the associated sets
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
are shown in Figure
1
.

-
-
-
+
-
+
-
+
-
+
-
+
+
+
+
J_{1}
J_{1}
J_{2}

Notation:
\to L^{j}
-
\to I_{-}^{j}
+
\to I_{+}^{j}
\to D^{j}

Figure 1
.
Example of an admissible sequence of partitions of
\llbracket k+1\rrbracket
with
k=11
. The successive partitions
(L^{j},I_{-}^{j},I_{+}^{j},D^{j})_{1\leq j\leq m}
(with
m=4
) are represented from top to bottom.
Each curved arrow represents an element of
I^{j}_{\times}
and points from an index in
L_{\times}^{j}
to an index in
I_{\times}^{j}
.
The symbol

represents indices that can be chosen to belong to
L^{(j+1)}

(
),

I_{-}^{(j+1)}

(​
-
​)
or
I_{+}^{(j+1)}

(​
+
​).
In the proof of Proposition
6.10
we first integrate with respect to variables with indices in
J_{1}
, proceeding in the order
L^{1}_{\times},L^{2}_{\times},\dots,L^{m-1}_{\times},\{i\in L^{m}:\ i(+1,m)\in L^{m}\}
, and afterwards integrate with respect to variables with indices in
J_{2}
.

Let us now determine, or rather bound from above, the cardinality of
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
, that is, the number of admissible sequences that can be constructed according to the above rules.
Note that we have
2\times 3^{k}
possibilities for choosing
L^{1}
,
I^{1}_{+}
and
I^{1}_{-}
. Afterwards, at each step, one has to assign one out of three labels
I^{(j+1)}_{-}
,
I^{(j+1)}_{+}
or
L^{(j+1)}
to each element in
I^{j}_{\times}
. Since
\lvert I^{j}_{\times}\rvert=\lvert L^{j}_{\times}\rvert
and the
L^{j}_{\times}
’s are disjoint by construction, there are at most
k
choices to be made, hence at most
3^{k}
possibilities. We therefore have
\lvert\mathfrak{P}_{k}\rvert\leq 2\times 9^{k}
.

Next, let us explain how each sequence in
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
is associated to a subset
\mathcal{P}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}
.
With the convention
(L^{(1)},I^{(1)}_{-},I^{(1)}_{+}):=(L^{1},I^{1}_{-},I^{1}_{+})
, we define

\begin{multlined}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z}):=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Bigg(\prod_{i\in I_{-}^{(j)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq(\Delta_{j}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta,\,z_{i(-1,j)}<(\Delta_{j}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta\}}\prod_{i\in I_{+}^{(j)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\wedge z_{i(-1,j)}\geq(\Delta_{j}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta\}}\\
\times\prod_{i\in L^{(j)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<(\Delta_{j}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta\}}\Bigg),\end{multlined}

(6.37)

where
\Delta_{j}t_{i}:=t_{i}-t_{i(-1,j)}
(recall (
6.35
)) and
z_{k+1}:=\infty
by convention.

It is easy to verify that (
6.37
) induces a partition of
{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}
indexed by
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
: Indeed, for any fixed
(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}
, the values of
z_{i}
and
\Delta t_{i}
uniquely determine
(L^{1},I^{1}_{-},I^{1}_{+})=(L^{(1)},I^{(1)}_{-},I^{(1)}_{+})
. Assuming that
(L^{j},I^{j}_{-},I^{j}_{+})
has been identified up to some
j\geq 1
, we can check whether (
6.36
) is satisfied. If so, we set
m=j
and we are done. Otherwise, we again use the values of
z_{i}
and
\Delta t_{i}
to find
(L^{(j+1)},I^{(j+1)}_{-},I^{(j+1)}_{+})
, from which we can then determine
(L^{j+1},I^{j+1}_{-},I^{j+1}_{+})
. Therefore, every
(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})
is contained in some
{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
. The uniqueness of
{\mathcal{P}}
is straightforward.

6.6.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.10

Recall the formula (
6.32
) of
U(\mathcal{P},t)
.
The proof is divided into two parts: first we integrate with respect to variables with indices in
J_{1}
and then with respect to those with indices in
J_{2}
.

Step 1. Integrating with respect to indices in
J_{1}
.

Our first task is to prove that, roughly speaking, the integral with respect to each
z_{i}
and
t_{i}
with indices
i\in J_{1}
yields at most a factor
\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{k_{1}}
. More precisely, we prove that

U(\mathcal{P},t)\leq\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{k_{1}}U_{2}(\mathcal{P},t),

(6.38)

where

U_{2}(\mathcal{P},t):=t^{1-\nu_{p}}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}_{t}^{(k_{2})}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\ (\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}z^{p}_{i}(3\lvert\log z_{i}\rvert+1)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})

and

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}:=\prod_{i\in I_{-}^{(m)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta\}}\prod_{i\in I_{+}^{(m)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\wedge z_{i(-1,J_{2})}\geq(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta\}}\prod_{i\in L^{(m)}\cap J_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<(\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i})^{d/6}\wedge\eta\}}

(6.39)

represents constraints on the values of
\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i}
and
z_{i}
that are inherited from
\mathcal{P}
for
i\in J_{2}
.
Note that compared to (
6.37
), we only have
j=m
and we have replaced

\Delta_{m}t_{i}
by
\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i}
. This makes no difference when
i\in I^{(m)}_{-}
or
i\in I^{(m)}_{+}
since
i(-1,m)\in J_{2}
in both cases; for
i\in L^{(m)}
, the constraint is implied by
\mathcal{P}
since

\Delta_{J_{2}}t_{i}\geq\Delta_{m}t_{i}
.

First, let us reduce to the case where
m=0
, that is,
L^{1}_{\times}=\emptyset
, :
the idea is to integrate with respect to variables with indices in
L^{1}_{\times}
, then with respect to those with indices in
L^{2}_{\times}
, etc. One may refer to Figure
1
to understand how this procedure goes on.
We start with integration with respect to
z_{i}
and
t_{i}
when
i\in L^{1}_{\times}
.
In this case, we have

z_{i}<[\Delta t_{i}\wedge\Delta t_{i+1}]^{d/6}\wedge\eta
.
To treat these indices, note that by symmetry,

\int^{t}_{0}s^{-1}(t-s)^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<[s\wedge(t-s)]^{d/6}\}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 2\int^{t/2}_{0}s^{-1}(t-s)^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<s^{d/6}\}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 4t^{-1}\log\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}tz^{-\frac{6}{d}}\Big)\,,

(6.40)

where for the last bound we used that
t-s\geq t/2
.
From now on, we assume
\eta\leq 1
. Then, if

z\in(0,\eta)
, using also that
6/d\leq 3
,
we have

\int^{t}_{0}s^{-1}(t-s)^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<[s\wedge(t-s)]^{d/6}\}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 8t^{-1}(3\lvert\log z\rvert+1)(1+\log_{+}t)\,.

Recalling the definition (
6.6
) of
\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)
, we end up with

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{(0,\eta)}s^{-1}(t-s)^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{z<[s\wedge t-s]^{d/6}\}}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\leq t^{-1}\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{\frac{1+2/d}{p}}.

(6.41)

So if
i\in L^{1}_{\times}
, using the trivial fact that
\Delta^{2}t_{i}:=t_{i+1}-t_{i-1}\leq t
, we have

\int^{t_{i+1}}_{t_{i-1}}\int_{(0,\infty)}(\Delta t_{i}\Delta t_{i+1})^{-1}z_{i}^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\\
\leq\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{\frac{1+2/d}{p}}({\Delta^{2}t_{i}})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{z}^{(i)}),

(6.42)

where
\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)}
and
\boldsymbol{z}^{(i)}
denote the vectors
\boldsymbol{t}
and
\boldsymbol{z}
with the
i
th coordinate omitted and
\mathcal{P}^{(i)}
is obtained from
\mathcal{P}
by ignoring all the constraints that involve either
z_{i}
or
t_{i}
.
Thus, at the cost of a multiplicative factor
\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{(1+2/d)/p}

one can, for each
i\in L^{1}_{\times}
, simplify the integrals with respect to
{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}
.
After relabeling the indices, we can then iterate this process, making use of the iterative construction of
\mathcal{P}
. As a result, we are left to prove (
6.38
) in the case where
L^{1}_{\times}=\emptyset
. Since
m=1
in this case, we drop the superscript
1
from the notation.

Under the assumption
L^{1}_{\times}=\emptyset
, we have
J_{1}=\{i\in L:i+1\in L\}
. We may treat different segments of
J_{1}
separately. Let us assume, for instance, that
\llbracket i,i+\ell-1\rrbracket\subseteq J_{1}
, where
\llbracket i,j\rrbracket:=\{i,i+1,\dots,j\}
. In the same spirit as (
6.40
)–(
6.42
), we have that

\int^{t_{i+1}}_{t_{i-1}}(\Delta t_{i}\Delta t_{i+1})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\\
\leq\frac{8(1+\log_{+}t)}{\Delta^{2}t_{i}}(1+3\lvert\log(z_{i}\wedge z_{i+1})\rvert)\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{z}^{(i)})\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\vee z_{i+1}<(\Delta^{2}t_{i})^{6/d}\wedge\eta\}}.

Iterating this and noticing that
\lvert\log(z_{i}\wedge z_{i+1})\rvert=\lvert\log z_{i}\rvert\vee\lvert\log z_{i+1}\rvert

since
z_{i},z_{i+1}<\eta\leq 1
, we get

\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{\ell}(t_{i-1},t_{i+\ell})}\prod_{j=0}^{\ell}(\Delta t_{i+j})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{i+\ell-1}\leq\frac{(8(1+\log_{+}t))^{\ell}}{t_{i+\ell}-t_{i-1}}\times\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{\ell}(3\lvert\log z_{i+j}\rvert+1)}{\min_{j\in\llbracket\ell\rrbracket}(3\lvert\log z_{i+j}\rvert+1)}.

The second denominator can be ignored since it is larger than
1
. If one then integrates with respect to
z_{i},\dots,z_{i+\ell-1}
(recall (
6.39
) and the remark afterwards), one obtains

\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{\ell}(t_{i-1},t_{i+\ell})\times(0,\infty)^{\ell}}\Biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{\ell}(\Delta t_{i+j})^{-1}\Biggr)\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}_{i+j}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i+j}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i+j})\\
\leq\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\frac{\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{\frac{1+2/d}{p}\ell}}{t_{i+\ell}-t_{i-1}}(3\lvert\log z_{i+\ell}\rvert+1).

This completes integration with respect to
(t_{i},z_{i})
for
i\in J_{1}
(note that
i+\ell-1\in J_{1}
implies
i+\ell\in L
and hence
z_{i+\ell}<\eta
).
Recalling (
6.32
) and bounding
(3|\log z|+1)^{p/(1+2/d)}\leq 3|\log z|+1
, we obtain (
6.38
).

Step 1. Integrating with respect to indices in
J_{2}
.
To complete the proof, we need to show that

U_{2}(\mathcal{P},t)\leq\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{k_{2}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p).

(6.43)

To simplify notation, let us assume that
J_{2}=\llbracket k\rrbracket
and hence
k_{2}=k
(again, this simply amounts to relabeling the vertices).
Recalling the definition (
6.6
) of
\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)
,
the bound (
6.43
) follows once we have shown that if
(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, then

(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}z^{p}_{i}\leq\mathbf{G}_{p}(\boldsymbol{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg(z^{p}_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq\eta\}}+z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}\bigg),

(6.44)

where, recalling the definition (
6.5
) of
G_{p}(s)
, we set

\mathbf{G}_{p}(\boldsymbol{t}):=\begin{cases}(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})&\text{ if }k\notin L,\\
(\Delta t_{k})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket\setminus\{k\}}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})&\text{ if }k\in L.\end{cases}

Note that in both cases,
t^{1-\nu_{p}}
times the integral of
\mathbf{G}_{p}(\boldsymbol{t})
over the simplex
\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)

is equal to
\Lambda(k,t,p)
as defined in (
6.4
) (in the second case one simply needs to exchange the role of
\Delta t_{k}
and
\Delta t_{k+1}
).

The reason why (
6.44
) is needed is that, on the left-hand side of (
6.44
), the exponent
p
makes
z^{p}_{i}

a priori
not integrable near zero. But since
\nu_{p}-1>-1
, there is some margin for the integrability of
(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}
: the idea is to use the constraints in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to “transfer” a part of the exponent of
\Delta t_{i}
onto that of
z_{i}
or
z_{i-1}
or both.

To this end, we shall use the equivalence

(\Delta t)^{\frac{d}{6}}\leq z\quad\iff\quad(\Delta t)^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{3}}\leq z^{1+\frac{2}{d}-p}

(6.45)

in two cases:
(i) if
i\in I_{-}\cup I_{+}
and
z_{i}<\eta
, with
z=z_{i}
and
\Delta t=\Delta t_{i}
;
(ii) if
i\in L
(hence
z_{i}<\eta
), with
z=z_{i}
and
\Delta t=\Delta t_{i+1}
.
The reader can check that for
(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{z})\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
the left-hand side of (
6.45
) is satisfied in these two cases.
Notice now that if
i\in L
, then necessarily we have
i+1\in I_{+}
: indeed,
i+1
cannot be in
I_{-}
since
L^{1}_{\times}=\emptyset
and it cannot be in
L
, either, since
i\in J_{2}
by assumption. Therefore,
we obtain

\begin{multlined}(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}z^{p}_{i}\leq\prod_{i\in I_{-}}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1-\frac{\nu_{p}}{3}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}}\prod_{i\in I_{+}}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1-\frac{\nu_{p}}{3}(\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{i-1\in L\}})}\\
\times\prod_{i\in L}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg(z^{p}_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq\eta\}}+z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}\bigg).\end{multlined}

(6.46)

The bound (
6.44
) now follows from the estimate

\prod_{i\in I_{-}}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1-\frac{\nu_{p}}{3}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}}\prod_{i\in I_{+}}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1-\frac{\nu_{p}}{3}(\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}<\eta\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{i-1\in L\}})}\prod_{i\in L}(\Delta t_{i})^{\nu_{p}-1}\leq\mathbf{G}_{p}(\boldsymbol{t}),

(6.47)

which is a consequence of the following three observations concerning the exponent of
\Delta t_{i}
on the left-hand side of (
6.47
):

•

It is larger than or equal to
\frac{\nu_{p}}{3}-1
(which we use if
\Delta t_{i}\leq 1
).

•

It is equal to
\nu_{p}-1
if
\Delta t_{i}\geq 1>\eta
(as can be checked from the definition (
6.39
) of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
).

•

It is equal to
\nu_{p}-1
if
i=k
and
k\in L

or if
i=k+1
and
k\notin L
(this follows from the
convention
z_{k+1}=\infty
).

This completes the proof of (
6.43
), which, together with (
6.38
), implies (
6.33
).
∎

7.
Proof of Theorem
2.1
and upper bounds in Theorems
2.11
and
2.12

7.1.
Proof of Theorem
2.1

First let us note that the results for the free-end partition function follows from those for the point-to-point version and Jensen’s inequality, as observed in (
5.6
).
The
L^{p}
-convergence (
2.4
) for
p>1
is a direct consequence of Corollary
6.5
and the fact that

({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x))_{a\in(0,1]}
is a time-reversed martingale for the filtration
\mathcal{G}
defined in (
1.20
).
The convergence in
L^{1}
when
\mu<\infty
cannot be obtained in this manner but can be recovered by a truncation argument. More precisely, we consider the partition function

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},[a,b)}_{\beta}(t,x)
obtained by replacing
{\omega}
by

{\omega}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,b))
(see
[
13
, Equation (2.40) and below]
) and reproduce the argument used in
[
13
, Prop. 4.6 and 4.7]
but with a moment of order
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})\cap(1,2]
instead of
2
.

Let us move to the proof of (
2.3
).
Recall that
{\omega}_{<}
and
{\omega}_{\geq}
denote the set of points in
{\omega}
with third coordinate in
(0,1)
and
[1,\infty)
, respectively.
Consider the reduced partition function

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}^{a}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).

It is easy to obtain that

{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega},a}(t,x)={\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta,t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}^{a}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}),

(7.1)

where we have defined

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta,t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x}):=\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(t_{i-1},x_{i-1};t_{i},x_{i})\,,

with the convention
x_{0}:=0
,
x_{k+1}:=x
,
t_{0}:=0
,
t_{k+1}:=t
.
As a consequence, using Minkowski’s inequality, the identity (
1.18
) and Corollary
6.5
, we have for any
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
,

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
\leq C(\beta,p,t)\rho(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}^{a}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).

Using Corollary
6.5
again,
we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C(\beta,p,t)\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta t_{i})

for every
a\in(0,1)
. Setting
\beta^{\prime}:=\beta C(\beta,p,t)
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C(\beta,p,t){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t,x)<\infty

(7.2)

for every
a\in(0,1)
.
As
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
is a reversed martingale in
a
under
\mathbb{P}_{<}
, this shows (
2.3
). For the proof of the positivity statement in (
2.2
), we refer to
[
13
, Section 4.7]
.
∎

7.2.
Proof of Theorem
2.11
(first half: upper bounds)

In this section,
we prove all upper bounds on

|\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)|
.
By convexity and the fact that
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(1)=0
,
we have for
q\in(0,1)
and
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
that

\frac{q-1}{p-1}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(q)\leq 0.

(7.3)

Hence
it is sufficient to prove only an upper bound on
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
for
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
.
Furthermore, by Proposition
2.8

(ii)
, we can consider the point-to-point partition function in our computations.

7.2.1.
Dimension
d=1

If
d=1
and
p=2
, the first part of Proposition
6.1
immediately yields

\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(2)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-2}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)^{2}\Big]=\frac{\beta^{4}\mu_{0,\infty}(2)^{2}}{4}.

The result for other values of
p\in(1,2)

follows by convexity, which gives
0\leq\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq(p-1)\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(2)
.

If
p\in(2,3)
, we use the bound (
6.3
) for some
t=t(\beta)
.
Indeed, by sub-additivity of
\log\mathbb{E}[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}]
(see Lemma
5.1
),
we get that

\bar{\gamma}_{{\beta}}(p)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]=\inf_{t>0}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}\Big]\,.

Using (
5.6
), we therefore get that for any
p>1
and any
t>0
,

\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]\,.

(7.4)

If
t=t({\beta})=c_{p}{\beta}^{-4}
with a sufficiently small constant
c_{p}
,
the bound (
6.3
) gives that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]&\leq C_{p}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{p}\beta t^{\frac{1}{4}})^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigg(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{p}^{\prime}{\beta}t^{\frac{\nu_{p}}{p}})^{\ell}}{\Gamma(\nu_{p}(\ell+1))^{\frac{1}{p}}}\Bigg)^{k_{2}+1}\\
&\leq 2C_{p}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2C_{p}\beta t^{\frac{1}{4}})^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{2}+1}{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq 4C_{p}\,.\end{split}

Indeed, since
\frac{1}{p}\nu_{p}<\frac{1}{4}
for
p\in(2,3)
, we can choose
c_{p}
sufficiently small so that both the internal sum over
\ell
and the sum over
k_{2}
in the second line are bounded by
2
.
Altogether, thanks to (
7.4
), we get
\bar{\gamma}_{{\beta}}(p)\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{p}{\beta}^{4}
with
C^{\prime\prime}_{p}=c_{p}^{-1}\log 4C_{p}
.

7.2.2.
Dimension
d\geq 2

If
d\geq 2
, we are going to use Proposition
6.3
with
\eta=1
and drop the dependence in
\eta
in the notation.
Our first task is to replace the functions
\zeta_{1}(p,t)
,
\zeta_{2}(p)
and
\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)
in (
6.7
) by something more tractable.
First, we let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be constants, which only depend on the measure
{\lambda}
and are chosen to be larger than
1
, such that for every
p\in[1+\frac{1}{d},1+\frac{2}{d})
and
t\geq e
, we have

\bigg(\frac{C}{p-1}\bigg)^{k}\zeta_{1}(p,t)^{\frac{k_{1}}{p}}\zeta_{2}(p)^{\frac{k_{2}}{p}}\leq\Big(C_{1}(\log t)^{\frac{1}{1+2/d}}\Big)^{k_{1}}C_{2}^{k_{2}},

(7.5)

where
C
is the constant in (
6.7
).
For
\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)
, we use the following lemma, which we prove afterwards.

Lemma 7.1
.

For any
k\geq 0
,
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
,
t\geq 1
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\Lambda(k,t,p)\leq(k+1)^{2}(4\nu_{p}^{-1}\alpha^{-\nu_{p}})^{k}e^{\alpha t}.

Using Proposition
6.3
, (
7.5
) and Lemma
7.1
(and the trivial bound
(k+1)^{2/q}\leq(k+1)^{2}
), we have
for any
\alpha>0
and
q\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-q}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{q}\Big]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{q}t}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}(C_{1}\beta(\log t)^{\frac{1}{1+{2}/{d}}})^{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}(k_{2}+1)^{2}\bigg(4^{\frac{1}{q}}C_{2}\beta\nu_{q}^{-\frac{1}{q}}\alpha^{-\frac{\nu_{q}}{q}}\bigg)^{k_{2}}.

(7.6)

Now, for sufficiently small
{\beta}
we choose
q\in(1+\frac{1}{d},1+\frac{2}{d})
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
t\geq e
in the following way (the dependence in
\beta
may sometimes be omitted in the computations below, for readability):

q({\beta}):=1+\frac{2}{d}-\frac{8e}{d}(2C_{2}\beta)^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,,\quad{\alpha}({\beta}):=(4(2C_{2}\beta)^{q}\nu_{q}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{\nu_{q}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad t(\beta):=e^{(2C_{1}\beta)^{-(1-\frac{2}{d})}}\,.

(7.7)

Note that with this choice we have
\nu_{q}=4e(2C_{2}\beta)^{1+\frac{2}{d}}
and thus

\alpha=(2C_{2}\beta)^{-\frac{2}{d}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{4e(2C_{2}\beta)^{1+\frac{2}{d}}}\bigg)\,,

(7.8)

C_{1}\beta(\log t)^{\frac{1}{1+{2}/{d}}}=\frac{1}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad 4^{\frac{1}{q}}C_{2}\beta\nu_{q}^{-\frac{1}{q}}\alpha^{-\frac{\nu_{q}}{q}}=\frac{1}{2}\,,

(7.9)

so that the sums in
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
in (
7.6
) are respectively equal to
2
and
12
. We end up with

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-q}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{q}\Big]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq 24\,e^{\frac{\alpha}{q}t}\,.

(7.10)

For a fixed
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
, we can now deduce the upper bound in (
2.16
) from (
7.10
). We consider
\beta
sufficiently small so that
q=q(\beta)\geq p
.
Using first (
7.4
) and then Jensen’s inequality, we get

\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-p}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{p}\Big]\leq\frac{p}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,0)^{-q}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,0)^{q}\Big]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\frac{p\log 24}{t}+\frac{p\alpha}{q}.

(7.11)

Using (
7.8
) and replacing
t
by its value (
7.7
) yield the upper bound in (
2.16
) since
p/q\leq 1
.
Let us finally comment on our choice (
7.7
). The parameters
\alpha
and
t
have been chosen so that (
7.9
), hence (
7.10
), holds. The value of
q=q(\beta)
is chosen so that
{\alpha}
, seen as a function of
q
for fixed
\beta
, is minimized.
This completes the proof of the upper bounds in (
2.15
) and (
2.16
). ∎

The lower bounds in (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) will be shown after Proposition
9.5
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Recall the definitions (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) of
\Lambda(k,t,p)
and
G_{p}(s)
. For fixed
j\in\llbracket k\rrbracket
, using a symmetry argument, we have that

\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta t_{k+1}=\max\limits_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket}\Delta t_{i}\}}(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\\
=\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta t_{j}=\max\limits_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket}\Delta t_{i}\}}(\Delta t_{j})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket\setminus\{j\}}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\\
\geq\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta t_{j}=\max\limits_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket}\Delta t_{i}\}}(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,,

where we have used that
