(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}G_{p}(\Delta t_{j})\leq(\Delta t_{j})^{\nu_{p}-1}G_{p}(\Delta t_{k+1})
if
\Delta t_{k+1}\leq\Delta t_{j}
for the second step.
As a consequence, again by a symmetry argument, we have

\Lambda(k,t,p)\leq(k+1)\,t^{1-\nu_{p}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta t_{k+1}=\max\limits_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket}\Delta t_{i}\}}(\Delta t_{k+1})^{\nu_{p}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,.

Together with the inequality
\max_{i\in\llbracket k+1\rrbracket}\Delta t_{i}\geq t/(k+1)
, this implies

\Lambda(k,t,p)\leq(k+1)^{2-\nu_{p}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}.

We can conclude our proof by observing that

\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}G_{p}(\Delta t_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\leq e^{\alpha t}\bigg(\int^{\infty}_{0}G_{p}(s)e^{-\alpha s}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)^{k}

and

\int^{\infty}_{0}G_{p}(s)e^{-\alpha s}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\int^{1}_{0}s^{\frac{1}{3}\nu_{p}-1}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{\infty}_{0}s^{\nu_{p}-1}e^{-\alpha\nu_{p}}\,\mathrm{d}s=3\nu_{p}^{-1}+{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})\alpha^{-\nu_{p}}\leq 4\nu_{p}^{-1}\alpha^{-\nu_{p}}\,,

where the last step is valid because
\nu_{p}\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
2.12
(first half: upper bounds)

Again, by (
7.3
) it suffices
to prove (
2.18
) for
p>1
. Moreover, (
2.19
) follows immediately from (
2.18
).

If
d\geq 2
, the arguments from Section
7.2
remain valid if we let
q
be equal to the value corresponding to our assumption
\mu_{1,\infty}(q)<\infty
(we consider
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that (
7.5
) is valid for this value of
q
) but retain the choice in (
7.7
) for
\alpha
and
t
.
The conclusion (
7.11
) is still valid for any
p\in(1,q]
, and because
\alpha(\beta)=C_{3}\beta^{q/\nu_{q}}
,
(
2.18
) follows.

If
d=1
, it suffices by convexity to treat the case when
p=q
.
By (
6.2
) (with
\eta=1
) and our assumptions, there are constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, which may depend on
q
, such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-q}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,x)^{q}\Big]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}(C_{1}\beta t^{\frac{1}{4}})^{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{2}\beta t^{\frac{\nu_{q}}{q}})^{k_{2}}}{\Gamma(\nu_{q}(k_{2}+1))^{\frac{1}{q}}}.

(7.12)

Let us choose
t=t(\beta):=(2C_{1})^{4}\beta^{-4}
so that the first sum is equal to
2
.

For the second sum, consider the three-parameter function
F_{{\alpha},\delta}^{({\gamma})}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}x^{m}/{\Gamma}({\alpha}m+\delta)^{\gamma}
. A precise asymptotic of
F_{{\alpha},\delta}^{({\gamma})}
is given in
[
55
, Theorem 1]
, from which one gets that there is
K_{{\alpha},\delta}^{({\gamma})}>0
such that for all
x\geq 0
,

F_{{\alpha},\delta}^{({\gamma})}(x)\leq K_{{\alpha},\delta}^{({\gamma})}e^{2{\gamma}x^{\frac{1}{\alpha\gamma}}}\,.

(7.13)

Thanks to this estimate, the second sum in (
7.12
) is bounded by
C_{3}(1+\beta^{c_{q}})\exp(\frac{1}{q}(C_{2}\beta)^{q/\nu_{q}}t)
with
c_{q}=(q-1)(\frac{1}{\nu_{q}}-\frac{4}{q})<0
.
In view of (
7.4
), we obtain that

\bar{\gamma}_{{\beta}}(q)\leq\frac{q\log(2C_{3}(1+{\beta}^{c_{q}}))}{t(\beta)}+C_{2}\beta^{\frac{q}{\nu_{q}}}\leq C_{4}{\beta}^{4}\log(1+{\beta}^{-1})+C_{2}{\beta}^{\frac{q}{\nu_{q}}}\,.

Recalling that
q/\nu_{q}<4
if
p<2
, this gives the upper bound in (
2.18
).
Finally, the lower bound in (
2.22
) is an immediate consequence of (
2.19
) since our assumption

\lim_{z\to\infty}\log{\lambda}([z,\infty))/\log z=\alpha
implies that

\mu_{1,\infty}(q)<\infty
for all
q<\alpha
.
∎

The second half of Theorem
2.12
will be shown after Proposition
9.5
.

8.
Existence and uniqueness of solutions to the SHE

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorems
2.5
and
2.7
.
The existence part, that is, Theorem
2.5
is detailed in the Sections
8.1
–
8.4
.
Theorem
2.7
, is addressed afterwards: in Section
8.5
, we verify that the solution defined in Theorem
2.5
satisfies the condition (
2.10
), while uniqueness is shown in Section
8.6
.

8.1.
Overview of the proof of Theorem
2.5

For the ease of exposition, we first present the case where the initial condition
u_{0}
is the Dirac function
\delta_{0}
.
By (
1.16
), we have

\begin{multlined}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+\beta\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{a}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\\
+\beta\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y).\end{multlined}

(8.1)

We want to let
a
tend to
0
and conclude that

\begin{multlined}{\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(t,x)=\rho(t,x)+\beta\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{\omega}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\\
\quad+\beta\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y).\end{multlined}

(8.2)

We have to prove that the two integrals in the on the right-hand side are well defined in Itô’s and Lebesgue’s sense, respectively (cf. Remark
1.2
), that they are finite and that they are the limit of the integrals displayed in (
8.1
). For this last point, setting

\displaystyle I^{a}_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y))\,\xi^{a}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y),

\displaystyle I^{a}_{2}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{[0,a)}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y),

\displaystyle I^{a}_{3}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y))\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y),

with
\xi^{[a,b)}_{{\omega}}:=\xi^{a}_{\omega}-\xi^{b}_{\omega}
(and
\xi^{0}_{\omega}:=\xi_{\omega}
), we need to prove that
for all
(t,x)
and
j=1,2,3
, the following convergence holds in probability:

\lim_{a\to 0}I^{a}_{j}(t,x)=0.

(8.3)

The finiteness and convergence of these integrals are proved in Section
8.2
and Section
8.3
, respectively. The case of general initial condition is discussed in Section
8.4
.

8.2.
Finiteness of stochastic integrals

We first introduce a technical statement that is a direct consequence of Corollary
6.5
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Assuming (
1.14
) and (
2.1
), for all
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
,
T>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\beta>0
, there exist positive constants
\beta^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}(\beta,p,T)
and
C=C(\beta,p,T)
such that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\mathbb{E}_{<}\bigg[\sup_{a\in(0,1)}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t,x)\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}_{\geq}$-a.s.}

Proof.

By Doob’s maximal inequality, it is sufficient to bound

\mathbb{E}_{<}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}]^{1/p}
uniformly in
a
,
t
and
x
.
With the conventions that
t_{k+1}:=t
,
x_{k+1}:=x
and the term corresponding to
k=0
is equal to

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(t,x)
, we have that

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(t_{i-1},x_{i-1};t_{i},x_{i})\prod_{j-1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{j},\mathrm{d}x_{j}).

(8.4)

Averaging with respect to
{\omega}_{<}
and using the Minkowski inequality, translation invariance
and Corollary
6.5
(we let
C_{0}=C_{0}(\beta,p,T)
denote the constant obtained from Corollary
6.5
), we obtain the desired bound as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\prod_{j-1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{j},\mathrm{d}x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}[C_{0}\beta]^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{j=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{j},\mathrm{d}x_{j})=C_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{C_{0}\beta}(t,x).\qed

Remark 8.2
.

In this section and the next, the dependence in
\beta,p
and
T
of the constant do not have a major importance: most of the time, they are omitted in computations.

We now proceed to showing that the integrals in (
8.2
) are well defined and finite. The integral
I_{3}^{a}
is a Lebesgue integral. Because of (
2.2
), it clearly suffices to show that

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)\sup_{a\in(0,1)}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)<\infty.

(8.5)

In fact, we are going to show that the
L^{p}(\mathbb{P}_{<})
-norm of the quantity in (
8.5
) is finite
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s. Using Minkowski’s integral inequality and Lemma
8.1
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}&\rho(t-s,x-y)\sup_{a\in(0,1)}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{p}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\leq\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)\mathbb{E}_{<}\bigg[\sup_{a\in(0,1)}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)^{p}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\\
&\leq C\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y).\end{split}

(8.6)

By (
8.1
), applied with
a=1
, the integral in the last line is equal to
({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t,x)-\rho(t,x))/\beta^{\prime}
,
which is finite and therefore proves (
8.5
).

Let us now deal with
I_{1}^{a}
and
I_{2}^{a}
.
We show that for
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.e. realization of
{\omega}_{\geq}
, the process
({\omega}_{<},s,y)\mapsto\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<}\cup{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta}(s,y)
is
L^{p}(\mathbb{P}_{<})
-integrable with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
, in the sense described in Section
4.3
.
Thanks to Lemma
4.4
, this implies that
({\omega},s,y)\mapsto\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)
is
L^{0}(\mathbb{P})
-integrable with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
.
To this end, according to (
4.6
) and (
4.7
),
we only need to prove that for some
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,1)}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{2}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]<\infty.

(8.7)

Using Fatou’s Lemma for the inner integral, we can replace
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)

by
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)
and add a restriction
\mathbf{1}_{\{z\geq a\}}
provided that the bound we prove is uniform in
a
.
Now using the BDG inequality in the reverse direction, we obtain that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,1)}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{2}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)^{2}z^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\geq a\}}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]\\
&\qquad\leq C\,\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{a}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg].\end{split}

(8.8)

Now using (
8.1
) and the Minkowski inequality, we have

\begin{split}&\beta\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg|\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{a}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\quad\leq\rho(t,x)+\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}+\beta\,\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{p}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}.\end{split}\!\!\!

(8.9)

We conclude by observing that all summands on the right-hand side are uniformly bounded in
a
:
we use Lemma
8.1
for the second one, while the third one has been controlled in the previous paragraph (see (
8.6
)). ∎

8.3.
Convergence of stochastic integrals

We give the proof of (
8.3
) in this section.
The convergence of
I^{a}_{3}
follows from (
8.5
) and dominated convergence.
The convergence of
I^{a}_{2}
follows from dominated convergence for stochastic integrals.
The main piece of work consists in proving the convergence of
I^{a}_{1}
.
We define

\begin{split}J^{a,b}_{1}(t,x)&:=\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y))\,\xi^{[a,b)}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\,,\\
J^{a,b}_{2}(t,x)&:=\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)\big({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\big)\,\xi^{b}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\,,\end{split}

and we prove that the following holds in
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-probability:

\lim_{b\to 0}\sup_{a\in(0,1)}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert J^{a,b}_{1}(t,x)\rvert\Big]=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert J^{a,b}_{2}(t,x)\rvert\Big]=0.

Convergence of
J^{a,b}_{2}(t,x)

By Jensen’s inequality,
it suffices to prove that
\mathbb{E}_{<}[|J^{a,b}_{2}(t,x)|^{p}]\to 0

for some
p\in(1,\min(2,1+\frac{2}{d}))
.
By the BDG inequality, we can further reduce this to proving

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[b,1)}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{2}({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y))^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]=0.

(8.10)

By subadditivity (
5.4
), for
a<b
this quantity is smaller than

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[b,1)}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\lvert{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\rvert^{p}z^{p}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z)\Bigg]\\
=\mu_{b,1}(p)\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)-{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y.

As the integrand tends to zero thanks to (
2.3
),
by dominated convergence we will get that the last integral converges to
0
for
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s. if
we show that for
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.e. realization of
{\omega}_{\geq}
,

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\bigg[\sup_{a\in(0,1)}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)^{p}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\,.

(8.11)

To this end, we use Lemma
8.1
to bound the left-hand side of (
8.11
) by a constant times

\begin{multlined}\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(s,y)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\\
\leq\begin{multlined}\Bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta^{\prime})^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\Bigg(\int_{(t_{k},t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\rho(s-t_{k},y-x_{k})^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\Bigg)^{p},\end{multlined}\end{multlined}

(8.12)

where the last inequality follows from Minkowski’s inequality (for the
L^{p}
-norm of the measure
\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y
).
Recalling (
5.5
) and using Lemma
4.5
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{(t_{k},t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\rho(s-t_{k},y-x_{k})^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\vartheta(p)^{2}\frac{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{2}}{{\Gamma}(2\nu_{p})}(t-t_{k})^{2\nu_{p}-1}\rho\big(\tfrac{1}{p}(t-t_{k}),x-x_{k}\big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta(p)\frac{{\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{2}}{{\Gamma}(2\nu_{p})}(t-t_{k})^{\nu_{p}}\rho(t-t_{k},x-x_{k})^{p}.

(8.13)

Therefore, the right-hand side of (
8.12
) is further bounded by
\vartheta(p){\Gamma}(\nu_{p})^{2}{\Gamma}(2\nu_{p})^{-1}t^{\nu_{p}}
times

\Bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta^{\prime})^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\Bigg)^{p}={\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t,x)^{p},

(8.14)

which is finite. This concludes the proof of (
8.10
).
∎

Convergence of
J^{a,b}_{1}(t,x)

Using Jensen’s inequality, we need to show that for some
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
,

\begin{split}\lim_{b\to 0}\sup_{a\in(0,1)}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{[a,b)}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]=0,\\
\lim_{b\to 0}\sup_{a\in(0,1)}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{[a,b)}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]=0.\end{split}

(8.15)

For the second line, we apply the BDG inequality
and realize that

\sup_{a\in(0,1)}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\,\xi^{[a,b)}_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]\\
\leq C\,\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,b)}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{2}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg].

Thanks to (
8.7
), this converges to
0
as
b\to 0
, by dominated convergence.
Concerning the first line in (
8.15
), we rely on the proof of Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
but with a small variation.
Let us define a modified partition function by

\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(s,x;t,y):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta^{k-1}\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}(s,t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{s,x;t,y}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\xi^{a}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\Bigg)\,\xi^{[a,b)}_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{k},\mathrm{d}x_{k})

and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x):={\mathcal{Y}}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(0,0;t,x)
.
The reader can check that the
\mathbb{E}_{<}[(\cdots)]
-term in the first line of (
8.15
) is simply
\mathbb{E}_{<}[\lvert\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)\rvert^{p}]
.
In analogy with (
7.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega}_{<},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega}_{<},a,b}_{\beta}(t_{k},x_{k};t,x)\prod_{i=1}^{k}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(t_{i-1},x_{i-1};t_{i},x_{i})\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).

Applying Minkowski’s inequality, we get

\!\!\!\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega}_{<},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\beta}^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega}_{<},a,b}_{\beta}(\Delta t_{k+1},\Delta x_{k+1})\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<},a}_{\beta}(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).

We then need the following estimate, which comes from a small adaptation of the proofs of Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
and Corollary
6.5
. The proof of this lemma is postponed to the end of the section.

Lemma 8.3
.

Assume that
{\lambda}([1,\infty))=0
. Given
\beta,T>0
, and
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})

there exists a function
\delta(b)=\delta_{\beta,p,T}(b)
such that
\lim_{b\to 0}\delta(b)=0
and

\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{a\in(0,1]}\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\lvert\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\delta(b).

By Corollary
6.5
and Lemma
8.3
, we obtain a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\beta,p,T)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta(b)\Bigg(\rho(t,x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(C_{0}{\beta})^{k}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(t)\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\rho_{t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\delta(b){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t,x)\,,

with
\beta^{\prime}:=C_{0}\beta
.
Since
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t,x)
is
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s. finite, this shows the first line of (
8.15
). Thus, the proof of the convergence to zero of
J^{a,b}_{1}(t,x)
is complete. ∎

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

The proof is considerably easier for
d=1
, so we only provide details in the case
d\geq 2
.
We follow exactly the plan of the proof of Proposition
6.3
up to Equation (
6.15
). Because of the restriction on the last iteration of the integral over the noise, the quantity we need to bound is not
\mathbb{E}[\lvert V_{a,k}(t)^{p}\rvert]
but rather
\mathbb{E}[\lvert V^{b}_{a,k}(t)\rvert^{p}]
where

V^{b}_{a,k}(t):=\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k)}}\frac{\rho_{t,0}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})}{{\rho(t,0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{k}<b\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{i}\geq a\}}\,(\delta_{{\omega}_{i}}-\nu)(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i}).

To conclude, we only need to slightly improve the bounds in further computations.
The reader can check that the proof of Lemma
6.7
yields the inequality (
6.17
) for
V^{b}_{k}(t)
with an additional
\mathbf{1}_{\{z_{k}<b\}}
in each of the integrals on the right-hand side.
Let
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P},t,b)
denote the corresponding integrals in the case
\theta=1+\frac{2}{d}
, cf. (
6.32
).
Adapting the proof of Proposition
6.10
, we want to prove that

\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{P},t,b)\leq{\varepsilon}(b,\eta)\zeta_{1}(\eta,p,t)^{k_{1}}\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)^{k_{2}}\Lambda(k_{2},t,p)

(8.16)

for some
{\varepsilon}(b,\eta)
that converges to
0
as
b\to 0
for any fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
.
Because of (
8.16
), the factor
{\varepsilon}(b,\eta)
appears on the right-hand side of (
6.34
) so that, provided that
\eta
has been chosen sufficiently small, we obtain that for every
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a\in(0,b)
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\rho(t,x)^{-p}\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)^{p}\Big]\leq\beta^{-1}{\varepsilon}(b,\eta)C(\beta,p,T)

with the constant
C(\beta,p,T)
of Corollary
6.5
. This is exactly the desired result.
Note that the factor
{\beta}^{-1}
comes from the fact that in
the definition of
\mathcal{Y}^{{\omega},a,b}_{\beta}(t,x)
,
we have
\beta^{k-1}
instead of
\beta^{k}
.

To prove (
8.16
), we follow the proof of Proposition
6.10
and realize that
we can improve the upper bound by a factor of

({\mu^{\log}_{0,b}(1+\frac{2}{d})}/{\mu^{\log}_{0,\eta}(1+\frac{2}{d})})^{{p}/(1+2/d)}
if
k\in J_{1}

and a factor of
{\mu^{\log}_{0,b}(1+\frac{2}{d})}/{\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)}
if
k\in J_{2}
so that (
8.16
) is valid for

{\varepsilon}(b,\eta):=\Bigg(\frac{\mu^{\log}_{0,b}(1+\frac{2}{d})}{\mu^{\log}_{0,\eta}(1+\frac{2}{d})}\Bigg)^{\frac{p}{1+2/d}}\vee\frac{\mu^{\log}_{0,b}(1+\frac{2}{d})}{\zeta_{2}(\eta,p)}.\qed

8.4.
General initial condition

Using translation invariance, we have already shown that the point-to-point partition function
{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(y;t,x)
is a solution to (
1.6
) with
u_{0}=\delta_{y}
, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The fact that
v
as defined in (
2.8
) is a mild solution to the SHE with initial condition
u_{0}
follows immediately by integrating (
2.6
) on both sides with respect to
u_{0}
, provided that integrals can be permuted in the following manner:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y^{\prime}){\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(y;s,y^{\prime})\,\xi_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\right)\,u_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\\
=\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y^{\prime})\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\beta}}^{{\omega}}(y;s,y^{\prime})\,u_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\right)\,\xi_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}).

Writing
\xi_{\omega}=\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}+\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}
, we can use the ordinary Fubini theorem for the
\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}
-integral
and a stochastic version of Fubini’s theorem
[
37
, Theorem A.3]
for the integration with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
.
Both cases require some integrability properties, which
we are going to show by recycling the estimates from Section
8.2
.

Concerning the integral with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}
, we simply need to check integrability in the Lebesgue sense, that is,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y^{\prime}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(y;s,y^{\prime})\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty\qquad\mathbb{P}_{\geq}\text{-a.s.}

By Minkowski’s integral inequality and Lemma
8.1
,
for
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
, the
L^{p}(\mathbb{P}_{<})
-norm of the left-hand side is bounded by a constant times

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y^{\prime}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;s,y^{\prime})\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)\\
=(\beta^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t,x-y)\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)\right),

where the equality follows from (
8.1
) with
a=1
.
The second integral on the right-hand side is finite as a consequence of our assumption (
2.7
). The first one is finite by
[
13
, Prop. 2.21]
.

For the integral with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
, according to
[
37
, Theorem A.3]
, changing the order of integration is permitted if we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,1)}\rho(t-s,x-y^{\prime})^{2}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(y;s,y^{\prime})^{2}z^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime},\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty.

Using (
8.8
) and (
8.9
), together with Lemma
8.1
and (
8.6
), we can bound the quantity above by a constant times

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho(t,x-y)+{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)+\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y^{\prime}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;s,y^{\prime})\,\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\right)\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y),

The integrability of each of the three terms has already been shown above.
∎

8.5.
Integrability property of the solution

Let us show that the solution
v
defined in Equation (
2.8
) satisfies the integrability condition (
2.10
). The important part is to show that whenever
u_{0}
satisfies (
2.7
) for a given
T>0
, then for any
p\in(1,1+\tfrac{2}{d})
, we have

\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(T-t),x)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(y;t,x)\,u_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

(8.17)

In our proof of (
8.17
), we will check along the way that
the expectation term, which equals
\mathbb{E}_{<}[\lvert v(t,x)\rvert^{p}]
, is finite for any
t
and
x
, proving the first line of (
2.10
). By (
2.7
), we can find
{\varepsilon}>0
such that

2T\limsup_{r\to\infty}r^{-2}\log\Big(|u_{0}|([-r,r]^{d}))\Big)<1-2{\varepsilon}\,.

(8.18)

We also let
\theta:=(1-{\varepsilon})^{-1}
.
In the following proof, the parameters
p\in(1,1+\tfrac{2}{d})
,
T
,
\beta
and
{\lambda}
and
{\varepsilon}
are all fixed, and the constants appearing in the inequalities may depend on them.

Using Minkowski’s inequality first and Lemma
8.1
afterwards, we have for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Bigg[\bigg\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(y;t,x)\,u_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\bigg\rvert^{p}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(y;t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)\,.

The a.s. finiteness of the right-hand side for fixed
x
and
t
is a consequence of
[
13
, Prop. 2.19]
.
Using the inequality above, to prove (
8.17
), we now need to show that
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s.,

\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(T-t),x)^{p}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)\,\lvert u_{0}\rvert(\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

(8.19)

We do so by comparing
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)
with a variable with finite
p
th moment. For a small
{\varepsilon}>0
,
we introduce the quantity

{\mathcal{T}}:=\sup_{k\geq 1}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{i},x_{i},z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq{\omega}_{\geq}:\\
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<T\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log z_{i}-{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=2}^{k}\frac{\lVert x_{i}-x_{i-1}\rVert^{2}}{2(t_{i}-t_{i-1})}-{\varepsilon}\bigg(\frac{\|y\|^{2}}{2T}+\frac{\lVert x_{1}-y\rVert^{2}}{2t_{1}}\bigg)\right].

(8.20)

Note that optimizing over
y
leads to the simpler expression

{\mathcal{T}}=\sup_{k\geq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{i},x_{i},z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq{\omega}_{\geq}:\\
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<T\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log z_{i}-{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=2}^{k}\frac{\lVert x_{i}-x_{i-1}\rVert^{2}}{2(t_{i}-t_{i-1})}-\frac{{\varepsilon}\lVert x_{1}\rVert^{2}}{2(T+t_{1})}\right].

(8.21)

The following lemma is an easy consequence of
[
13
, Lemma 4.17]
.

Lemma 8.4
.

For any
T>0
and
{\varepsilon}>0
,

\mathcal{T}
is finite
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s.

Proof.

By
[
13
, Lemma 4.17]
, we have (with the convention
x_{0}=0
and
t_{0}=0
)

\sup_{k\geq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{i},x_{i},z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq{\omega}_{\geq}:\\
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<2T\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Biggl(\log z_{i}-{\varepsilon}\frac{\lVert x_{i}-x_{i-1}\rVert^{2}}{2(t_{i}-t_{i-1})}\Biggr)<\infty.

Taking a further restriction (namely
t_{1}>T
) and shifting time by
-T
, we obtain by translation invariance that

\sup_{k\geq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{i},x_{i},z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq{\omega}_{\geq}:\\
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<T\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\log z_{i})-{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=2}^{k}\frac{\lVert x_{i}-x_{i-1}\rVert^{2}}{2(t_{i}-t_{i-1})}-{\varepsilon}\frac{\lVert x_{1}\rVert^{2}}{2(t_{1}+T)}\bigg)\right]<\infty.\qed

As a consequence of Lemma
8.4
, we have for every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
that

{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(y;t,x)\leq e^{{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{{\varepsilon}\|y\|^{2}}{2T}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta^{\prime})^{k}\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k)}}\rho_{y;t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}e^{{\varepsilon}\frac{\lVert\Delta x_{i}\rVert^{2}}{2\Delta t_{i}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\delta_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{j},\mathrm{d}x_{j},\mathrm{d}z_{j}),

(8.22)

with the convention that
x_{0}=y
.
Because

\rho(t,x)e^{\eta\frac{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}{2t}}=\theta_{\eta}^{\frac{d}{2}}\rho(\theta_{\eta}t,x)\,\qquad\text{with}\qquad\theta_{\eta}=(1-\eta)^{-1}

(8.23)

and
\theta=(1-{\varepsilon})^{-1}
,
we have
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(y;t,x)\leq e^{{\mathcal{T}}+\frac{{\varepsilon}\|y\|^{2}}{2T}}\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(y;t,x)
, where

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(y;t,x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\theta^{\frac{d}{2}}\beta^{\prime})^{k}\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k)}}\rho_{y;\theta t,x}(\theta\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{j=1}^{k}\delta_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{j},\mathrm{d}x_{j},\mathrm{d}z_{j})\,.

(8.24)

Setting
\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y):=e^{\frac{{\varepsilon}\|y\|^{2}}{2T}}|u_{0}|(\mathrm{d}y)
, we can reduce the proof of (
8.19
) to showing

\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(T-t),x)^{p}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

(8.25)

We
take the expectation and apply Minkowski’s inequality twice to get

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho&(\theta(T-t),y)^{p}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}y\Bigg]\\
&\leq\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(T-t),y)^{p}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\leq\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigg(\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(T-s),y)^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\Bigg)^{p}.\end{split}

(8.26)

Observe now that

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)
is a (non-normalized) point-to-point partition function corresponding to a Poisson environment with intensity measure
{\lambda}([1,\infty))\delta_{\theta^{d/2}\beta^{\prime}}
and a time-rescaling by
\theta
.
Therefore, by Corollary
6.5
,

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(y;t,x)^{p}]\leq C\rho(\theta t,x-y)^{p},

(8.27)

for a constant
C
that depends on all parameters but not on
y
,
t
and
x
.
Hence, the
\frac{1}{p}
th power of (
8.26
) is bounded by a constant times

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigg(\int_{(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(T-t),x)^{p}\rho(\theta t,x-y)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\leq C^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}T^{\nu_{p}}e^{-\frac{\lVert y\rVert^{2}}{2\theta T}}\,\hat{u}_{0}(\mathrm{d}y)\,,

where the inner integral has been computed exactly, as in (
8.13
).
The above integral is finite thanks to our choice of
{\varepsilon}
and
\theta
in (
8.18
). Thus, (
8.25
) holds and the proof is complete.
∎

8.6.
Uniqueness of solutions to the SHE

We assume in this section that
\lambda
satisfies (
1.21
) and prove uniqueness among solutions satisfying (
2.10
).
By the fact that the condition (
2.10
) is stable under linear combinations, it is sufficient to show that any solution to (
1.6
) with
u_{0}\equiv 0
is equal to zero.
Consider
v
that satisfies (
2.10
) and is such that for every
t\in(0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

v(t,x)=\beta\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-s,x-y)v(s,y)\,\xi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\,.

Applying this identity to
v(s,y)
in the integrand and repeating this, we obtain that for any
k\geq 1
,

v(t,x)=\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}}\rho_{t_{1},x_{1};t,x}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{x}^{(1)})v(t_{1},x_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}),

(8.28)

where
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{(1)}
are obtained from
\boldsymbol{t}
and
\boldsymbol{x}
, respectively, by removing the first component.
Fixing
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in(0,T)
, we are going to prove that
v(t,x)=0
by showing that the right-hand side in (
8.28
) is summable in
k
. More precisely, we set

{\mathcal{U}}(t,x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}}\rho_{t_{1},x_{1};t,x}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{x}^{(1)})v(t_{1},x_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})\,,

the term for
k=0
being simply
v(t,x)
.
We fix
p\in(1,1+\frac{2}{d})
for which
\mu_{0,1}(p)<\infty
(when
d=1
we fix
p=2
) and we are going to prove that

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\big|{\mathcal{U}}(t,x)\big|^{p}\Big]<\infty\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}_{\geq}$-a.s.}

(8.29)

Separating
{\omega}_{<}
and
{\omega}_{\geq}
in
\xi_{\omega}
, we have that

{\mathcal{U}}(t,x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}}{\mathcal{V}}(t_{1},x_{1})\prod_{i=2}^{k+1}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{<}}_{\beta}(t_{i-1},x_{i-1};t_{i},x_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}),

(8.30)

where

{\mathcal{V}}(t,x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta^{k}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}}\rho_{t_{1},x_{1};t,x}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{x}^{(1)})v(t_{1},x_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i})

(8.31)

and the terms for
k=0
in (
8.30
) and (
8.31
) are
{\mathcal{V}}(t,x)
and
v(t,x)
respectively.
By Corollary
6.5
and translation invariance, there exists
\beta^{\prime}>0
such that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{U}}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\beta^{\prime})^{k}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(t_{1},x_{1})\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\prod_{i=2}^{k+1}\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i}).\end{split}

(8.32)

To prove (
8.29
), we bound each of the
two summands in (
8.32
) separately.

Let us start with
\mathbb{E}_{<}[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(t,x)\rvert^{p}]^{1/p}
.
Using the triangle inequality and iterating the BDG inequality and the subadditivity property (
5.4
) for each term (as done in (
A.1
), for example), we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(C_{p}\beta\mu_{0,1}(p)^{\frac{1}{p}})^{k}\Bigg(\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{t}}\rho_{t_{1},x_{1};t,x}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{x}^{(1)})^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(t_{1},x_{1})\rvert^{p}\Big]\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,.

Then, applying (
6.20
) and Lemma
4.5

with the trivial inequality
t-t_{1}\leq t
,
we get that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(t,x)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\quad+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left[C_{p}\beta(\mu_{0,1}(p){\Gamma}(\nu_{p})t^{\nu_{p}})^{\frac{1}{p}}\right]^{k}}{{\Gamma}(k\nu_{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}}\Bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t-t_{1},x-x_{1})^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(t_{1},x_{1})\rvert^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}.\end{split}

(8.33)

Since the sum in
k
in finite, the two conditions in our assumption (
2.10
) implies the
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s. finiteness of the right-hand side of (
8.33
).

To show that the series in (
8.32
) is finite, we apply a similar trick to (
8.22
) and replace the
z_{i}
’s coming from the atoms of
{\omega}_{\geq}
by one. More precisely,
we set

{\mathcal{T}}(t,x):=\sup_{k\geq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{i},x_{i},z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq{\omega}_{\geq}:\\
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<t\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\log z_{i})-\eta\sum_{i=2}^{k+1}\frac{\lVert x_{i}-x_{i-1}\rVert^{2}}{2\Delta t_{i}}\right]\,,

(8.34)

with the convention that
t_{k+1}=t
and
x_{k+1}=x
.
The value of
\eta>0
in (
8.34
)
is chosen such that
\theta=(1-\eta\frac{3p-1}{2})^{-1}
, with the same
\theta
as in the assumption (
2.10
).
By the translation invariance and symmetry properties of
{\omega}_{\geq}
,
[
13
, Lemma 4.17]
guaranties that

{\mathcal{T}}(t,x)<\infty
,
\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s. Defining

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(s,y;t,x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\beta^{\prime})^{k}\int_{\mathbb{X}_{t}^{(k)}}\rho_{s,y;t,x}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x})\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}e^{\eta\frac{\lVert\Delta x_{i}\rVert^{2}}{2\Delta t_{i}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}t_{j},\mathrm{d}x_{j}\,\mathrm{d}z_{j})\,,

(8.35)

we find that the series in (
8.32
) is smaller than the following integral (we have replaced
t_{1},x_{1},z_{1}
by
s,y,z
):

\beta^{\prime}e^{{\mathcal{T}}(t,x)}\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[1,\infty)}\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(s,y;t,x)\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z).

(8.36)

Note that the integral in (
8.36
) is a sum and that all terms are finite almost surely by (
8.33
) and (
8.27
). In order to show that the integral in (
8.36
) is finite, we proceed in several steps. The first one is to replace

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}(s,y;t,x)
by
\rho(t-s,x-y)e^{\eta{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}/{(t-s)}}
.
This is the purpose of the following lemma whose short proof is postponed to the end of the section.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let

\begin{split}\Theta(t,x)&:=\bigg\{(s,y)\in(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\ :\ \widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(s,y;t,x)\geq\rho(t-s,x-y)e^{\eta\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}\bigg\},\\
\bar{\omega}_{\geq}&:=\Big\{(s,y)\in(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\ :\ \exists z\geq 1,\ (s,y,z)\in{\omega}\Big\}.\end{split}

(8.37)

Then we have
\#\left(\Theta(t,x)\cap\bar{\omega}_{\geq}\right)<\infty

\mathbb{P}_{\geq}
-a.s.

As a consequence of Lemma
8.5
, the integral in (
8.36
) is finite if

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[1,\infty)}N(s,y)\,\delta_{{\omega}_{\geq}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z)<\infty

(8.38)

where

N(s,y):=\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\rho(t-s,x-y)e^{\eta\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}.

By
[
77
, Prop. 12.1]
,
(
8.38
) holds if we can show that

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\rho(t-s,x-y)e^{\frac{\eta\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\,.

(8.39)

To this end,
we use the bound (
8.33
) for
\mathbb{E}_{<}[\lvert{\mathcal{V}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(s,y)\rvert^{p}]^{1/p}
and check that the integrals corresponding to each of the two summands on the right-hand side of (
8.33
) are finite. In the first case, we must show that

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\big\lvert v(s,y)\big\rvert^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\rho(t-s,x-y)e^{\eta\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\,.

(8.40)

Applying Jensen’s inequality for the finite measure
e^{-\eta{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}/{2(t-s)}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y
, we can bound the quantity above by

\Bigg(\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\big\lvert v(s,y)\big\rvert^{p}\Big]\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}e^{\eta(3p-1)\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{2(t-s)}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(8.41)

Recalling (
8.23
) and the relation
\theta=(1-\eta\frac{3p-1}{2})^{-1}
, the finiteness of (
8.41
) follows from our assumption (
2.10
).

For the second summand on the right-hand side of (
8.33
),
we have to show that

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\eta\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}\rho(t-s,x-y)\Bigg(\int_{(0,s)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(s-r,y-v)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(r,v)\rvert^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}v\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\,.

(8.42)

Using the same trick as in (
8.41
),
we see that it is sufficient to prove the finiteness of

\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{(0,s)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\eta(3p-1)\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{2(t-s)}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}\rho(s-r,y-v)^{p}\mathbb{E}_{<}\Big[\lvert v(r,v)\rvert^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

Now, by (
8.23
) and thanks to our choice of
\eta
, we can replace
e^{-\eta(3p-1){\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}/{2(t-s)}}\rho(t-s,x-y)^{p}
by a constant times
\rho(\theta(t-s),x-y)^{p}

and bound
\rho(s-r,y-v)^{p}
by a constant times
\rho(\theta(s-r),y-v)^{p}
.
Then, using (
5.5
) as in(
8.13
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{(r,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(\theta(t-s),x-y)^{p}\rho(\theta(s-r),y-v)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\leq C^{\prime}_{\eta,p}(t-r)^{\nu_{p}}\rho(\theta(t-r),x-v)^{p}\,.

Hence, the finiteness of (
8.42
) is also consequence of (
2.10
),
which finally concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.5
.

By Markov’s inequality and (
8.27
),
we have for any given
s,y\in(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\geq}\bigg[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}_{\geq}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(s,y;t,x)\geq\rho(t-s,x-y)e^{\eta\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\frac{\rho\big(\frac{t-s}{1-\eta},x-y\big)}{\rho(t-s,x-y)}e^{-\eta\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{(t-s)}}\bigg)^{p}

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}e^{-\eta p\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{2(t-s)}}\,,

using (
8.23
) for the last inequality.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\geq}\Big[\#\left(\Theta(t,x)\cap\bar{\omega}_{\geq}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\int_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[1,\infty)}e^{-\eta p\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{2(t-s)}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle=C_{p}{\lambda}([1,\infty))\int_{(0,t)}(t-s)^{\frac{d}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty.\qed

9.
Moments of order
p\in(0,1)
and lower bounds in Theorems
2.11
and
2.12

This section is dedicated to estimating fractional moments of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
. More precisely, we focus on

\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{1/2}]
to simplify notation, but the method would be equally efficient to directly estimate
\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)^{p}]
for
p\in(0,1)
.
The estimates obtained in this section allow us to complete the proof of Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
, since by convexity and the fact that
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(1)=0
and
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(0)=0
,
we have

\begin{cases}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq 2p\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})\quad&\text{ for }p\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),\\
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\leq 2(1-p)\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})\quad&\text{ for }p\in(\frac{1}{2},1),\\
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)\geq-2(p-1)\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})\quad&\text{ for }p>1.\end{cases}

(9.1)

We also
prove Proposition
2.2
in Section
9.4
,
partially using ideas developed in Sections
9.1
–
9.3
.

In Section
9.1
, we introduce a method that combines a coarse-graining and a change-of-measure argument, which allows to obtain upper bounds that decay exponentially in
t
(this is crucial for the proof of Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
).
This approach originates in the study of the discrete pinning model
[
50
,
58
]
: in its refined form, which first appeared in
[
91
]
, it can be used in a continuum setup such as the SHE. Note that the method found many implementations in the last decade, such as for disordered pinning
[
12
,
57
]
, directed polymers and variants
[
11
,
16
,
74
,
75
]
, the random walk pinning model
[
15
,
21
]
, large deviations of random walks in a random environment
[
95
]
, self-avoiding walks in a random environment
[
76
]
, and anomalous path detection in a random environment
[
29
]
.

The achievement of the method (presented in Lemma
9.1
) is to reduce the problem of estimating moments to that of showing that the original measure
\mathbb{P}
significantly differs (in total variation) from an alternative measure where the environment has been modified along a Brownian trajectory (the size-biased measure).
This last statement is proved in Sections
9.2
(for
d=1
or heavy-tailed noises) and
9.3
(for
d\geq 2
and light-tailed noises).
While Lemma
9.1
is quite general and can be adapted to various settings, the proof that the original and the tilted measures differ in total variation heavily depends on context.

The proof presented in Section
9.2
for
d=1
relies on ideas found in
[
74
, Section 3]
: in essence, we discriminate between the original and the tilted environments by looking at the average in a box of length
T
and wdith
\sqrt{T}
.
The proof for heavy-tailed environments, also found in Section
9.2
, relies on a similar idea to that found in
[
93
, Section 2]
.

On the other hand, the approach taken in Section
9.3
and Section
9.4
are completely new and have no discrete analogue. We show that what makes the tilted measure
different from the original one is the presence of clusters of points that are very close to one another. It requires a fine analysis to identify exactly what the characteristics of these clusters are.

9.1.
A general coarse-graining lemma

For notational simplicity, we set
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}:=\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta}^{{\omega}}(t,\ast)
in the remainder of this section.
Our coarse-graining approach reduces the problem of bounding fractional moments of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\beta,t}^{{\omega}}
to identifying a single event that is unlikely under the original measure
\mathbb{P}
but becomes likely under the size-biased measure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}_{\beta,t}
; recall Section
4.1
and notation therein.

We say that a measurable event
\mathcal{A}
for the point process
{\omega}
has
time range

[0,T)
if
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}}({\omega})
is a function of
{\omega}\cap([0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))
. Moreover, defining
\widehat{\omega}=\widehat{\omega}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B):={\omega}\cup\widetilde{\omega}({\omega}^{\prime},B)
, we will use, for a generic real-valued function
f
defined on the set of environments, the abbreviation

\widehat{f}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B):=f\circ\widehat{\omega}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B).

(9.2)

Also, recall that
\mathbf{Q}_{x}
is the law of a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion starting from
x
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Assume that
\mu<\infty
.
There exists a constant
K\in(0,\infty)
, which only depends on the dimension
d
, with the following property:
For any
\beta>0
and
T>0
, if there exists an event
\mathcal{A}

with time range
[0,T)
satisfying both

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\omega}\in\mathcal{A})\leq e^{-K}\,,

(H1)

\displaystyle\max_{x\in[0,\sqrt{T})^{d}}\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}(\widehat{\omega}\notin\mathcal{A})\leq e^{-K}\,,

(H2)

then for all
m\geq 1
we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]\leq 2^{-m}\,,

(9.3)

and as a consequence

\bar{\gamma}_{{\beta}}(\frac{1}{2})\leq-(\log 2)T^{-1}.

Below, we are going to apply Lemma
9.1
to events of the type

\mathcal{A}:=\{{\omega}\ :\ f({\omega})-\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]>h\}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathcal{A}:=\{{\omega}\ :\ f({\omega})>h\}

(9.4)

where
f
is a function that only depends on
{\omega}\cap([0,T)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))
and
h\in\mathbb{R}
is a threshold to be chosen appropriately.
More precisely, we consider functions
f
of the form

f({\omega})=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{(k)}_{T}}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i}),

(9.5)

where
k
is a positive integer and
\Upsilon
is a non-negative function on
\mathbb{X}^{(k)}_{T}
(recall the notation (
6.11
)).
Given
f
as above, we define

f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}^{(k)}_{T}}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{t},(B_{t_{i}})^{k}_{i=1},\boldsymbol{z})\prod_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i}).

(9.6)

In other words,
f^{\prime}
is the total contribution of the additional terms in
\widehat{f}
that are obtained by considering (only) the atoms on the added “environment spine”
({\omega}^{\prime},B)
(recall (
9.2
)).
Then, for
f
of the form (
9.5
), since
\Upsilon
is non-negative, we have

\widehat{f}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\geq f({\omega})+f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B).

(9.7)

In this setting, verifying (
H1
) and (
H2
) boils down to first and second moment computations for
f
and
f^{\prime}
.

Corollary 9.2
.

Assume that
f
is as in (
9.5
)
and satisfies one of the two following conditions:

(C1)

For all
x\in[0,\sqrt{T})^{d}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]^{2}\geq 4e^{K}\Bigl\{{\mathrm{Var}}(f({\omega}))+{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B))\Bigr\};

(C2)

The function
\Upsilon
is integer-valued, we have
\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]\leq e^{-K}
and

\forall x\in[0,\sqrt{T})^{d}:\quad\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]^{2}\geq e^{K}{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)).

In both cases, also assume that the variances on the right-hand side are finite.
Then (
H1
) and (
H2
) are satisfied and
\gamma_{{\beta}}(\frac{1}{2})\leq-(\log 2)T^{-1}.

Proof.

If
(C1)
is satisfied, define

m:=\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma:=\sqrt{{\mathrm{Var}}(f({\omega}))+{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B))}

and consider the event

\mathcal{A}:=\{{\omega}\ :\ f({\omega})>m+e^{\frac{K}{2}}\sigma\}.

We can apply Chebyshev’s inequality, using that
\sigma^{2}\geq\mathrm{Var}(f({\omega}))
, to check the validity of (
H1
). For (
H2
), we observe that as a consequence of (
9.7
), we have

\{\widehat{\omega}\notin\mathcal{A}\}\subseteq\{f({\omega})+f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)\leq m+e^{\frac{K}{2}}\sigma\}.

Using Chebyshev’s inequality, we deduce that

\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}(\widehat{\omega}\notin\mathcal{A})\leq\frac{{\mathrm{Var}}(f({\omega}))+{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B))}{\Big(e^{\frac{K}{2}}\sigma-\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]\Big)^{2}}\leq e^{-K}.

If
(C2)
is satisfied, we set

\mathcal{A}:=\{{\omega}\ :\ f({\omega})\geq 1\}=\{{\omega}\ :\ f({\omega})>0\}\,.

We then deduce (
H1
) from Markov’s inequality applied to
f({\omega})
and (
H2
) from Chebychev’s inequality applied to
f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)
.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

We will use the assumption
(C1)
for
d=1
in the light-tailed case and the assumption
(C2)
in all heavy-tailed cases and for
d\geq 2
. These two assumptions correspond to two different ways of distinguishing between the original measure and the size-biased one. In
Assumption
(C1)
,
f
is increased under the size-biased measure by an amount
f^{\prime}
that, on average, exceeds the typical fluctuation (measured as
{\mathrm{Var}}(f({\omega}))
) under the original measure. Assumption
(C2)
implies that there are patterns in
{\omega}
that appear with large probability under the size-biased measure and are most likely absent under the original one.

Proof of Lemma
9.1
.

Let us assume that
T
and
\mathcal{A}
satisfy (
H1
) and (
H2
).
We divide
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into cubes of side length
\sqrt{T}
: define

\mathcal{C}_{j}=\mathcal{C}^{(T)}_{j}:=j\sqrt{T}+[0,\sqrt{T})^{d},\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,.

Given
\boldsymbol{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{m}
, we define the function
\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}:C([0,mT])\to\mathbb{R}

by

\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}(\varphi):=\mathbf{1}\{\forall i\in\llbracket m\rrbracket:\ \varphi(iT)\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{i}}\}.

In other words,
\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}(\varphi)
is the indicator that the function
\varphi
passes through a prescribed sequence of cubes of diameter
\sqrt{T}
at times
iT
, where
i\in\llbracket m\rrbracket
. If
\varphi=(B_{t})_{t\in[0,mT]}
is a Brownian motion, the unique value of
\boldsymbol{j}
such that
\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}(B)=1
corresponds to a coarse-grained trajectory (or skeleton) of
B
on time scale
T
.
Now, recalling that we have set
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,t}=\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
and recalling the notation from Section
4.1
, we have

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{M}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}((i-1)T,x_{i-1};iT,x_{i})\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,.

(9.8)

This can be checked directly from the definition if
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}
and
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}((i-1)T,x_{i-1};iT,x_{i})
are replaced by

\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(mT,\ast)
and
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}((i-1)T,x_{i-1};iT,x_{i})
, respectively. The case
a=0
then follows from the
L^{1}
-convergence in Theorem
2.1
.
We therefore get

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{m}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})\,,\qquad{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}):=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{M}\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta}((i-1)T,x_{i-1};iT,x_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{i}}\}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}.

As a consequence, by subadditivity (
5.4
), we have

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{mT})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigr]\leq\sum_{\boldsymbol{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{m}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigr].

Our aim is to obtain a good bound on

\mathbb{E}[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}))^{1/2}]
. This is where we introduce a change-of-measure procedure.
We use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality as follows:

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigr]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega})^{-1}\Bigr]\mathbb{E}\Bigl[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})\Bigr]\,,

(9.9)

for some non-negative function
G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega})
that penalizes the
{\omega}
’s that contribute most to
\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})]
.
This procedure is referred to as a change of measure since
\mathbb{E}[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})]
corresponds to the expectation of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})
under a new measure whose density with respect to the original one is given by
G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega})
.
We now construct
G_{\boldsymbol{j}}
with a product structure in order to gain a constant factor per coarse-grained step of the trajectory.
For
K>0
, a fixed constant to be determined below, we define (with
j_{0}:=0
)

\begin{split}g({\omega}):=\exp(-K\,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}}({\omega})),\quad g_{i,j}({\omega}):=g\circ\theta_{((i-1)T,j\sqrt{T})}({\omega}),\quad G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega}):=\prod_{i=1}^{m}g_{i,j_{i-1}}({\omega}),\end{split}

(9.10)

where
\theta_{(t,x)}
is the space-time shift operator acting on point collections in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)
, that is,

\theta_{(t,x)}({\omega})=\{(s-t,y-x,z)\ :\ (s,y,z)\in{\omega}\}.

Because
{\mathcal{A}}
has time range
T
, for given
\boldsymbol{j}
, the variables
(g_{i,j_{i-1}}({\omega}))_{i=1}^{m}
are independent and identically distributed under
\mathbb{P}
, so that, by (
H1
),

\mathbb{E}[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega})^{-1}]=(1+(e^{K}-1)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}))^{m}\leq 2^{m}.

We then derive from (
9.9
) that

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigr]\leq 2^{\frac{m}{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{m}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Bigl[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})\Bigr]}.

(9.11)

Using the size-biased representation of Lemma
4.1
and recalling the notation (
9.2
), we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})\Bigr]=\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\Bigl[\widehat{G}_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}(B)\Bigr].

Letting
B^{(i)}_{t}:=B_{(i-1)T+t}-j_{i-1}\sqrt{T}
, using the stationarity of
{\omega}
and
{\omega}^{\prime}
and the fact that
\mathcal{A}
has time-range
T
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\Bigl[\widehat{G}_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}(B)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{g}_{i,j_{i-1}}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B^{(i)})\mathbf{1}_{\{B^{(i)}_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{i}-j_{i-1}}\}}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\Bigl[\widehat{g}_{i,j_{i-1}}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B^{(i)})\mathbf{1}_{\{B^{(i)}_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{i}-j_{i-1}}\}}\Bigr]\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\Bigl[\widehat{g}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B^{(i)})\mathbf{1}_{\{B^{(i)}_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{i}-j_{i-1}}\}}\Bigr]\Biggr].

Conditionally on
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,(i-1)T]}
,
(B^{(i)}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Brownian motion starting from
B_{(i-1)T}-j_{i-1}\sqrt{T}
, which belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. Thus,
using the Brownian motion’s Markov property iteratively (starting with
i=m
), we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\Bigl[\widehat{G}_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}}(B)\Bigr]\leq\prod_{i=1}^{m}\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}\Bigl[\widehat{g}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{i}-j_{i-1}}\}}\Bigr]\,.

Reindexing the sums yields

\sum_{\boldsymbol{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{m}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Bigl[G_{\boldsymbol{j}}({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT}(\chi_{\boldsymbol{j}})\Bigr]}\leq\Biggl(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}\Bigl[\widehat{g}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j}\}}\Bigr]}\Biggr)^{m}.

(9.12)

Hence, it remains to show that the sum on the right-hand side is small.
Given an integer
N
, since
g\leq 1
, we have

\begin{multlined}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}\Big[\widehat{g}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j}\}}\Big]}\\
\leq\sum_{\|j\|_{\infty}\geq N}\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\sqrt{\mathbf{Q}_{x}[B_{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{j}]}+(2N)^{d}\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}\Big[\widehat{g}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\Big]}.\end{multlined}

(9.13)

We then choose
N
to be the smallest integer such that the first sum
is smaller than
(4\sqrt{2})^{-1}
; note it depends on
d
but not on
T
. For the second term, recalling the definition (
9.10
) of
g
and using assumption (
H2
) for the last inequality,
we have

\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[\widehat{g}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)]=e^{-K}+(1-e^{-K})\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}(\widehat{\omega}\notin\mathcal{A})\leq 2e^{-K}.

(9.14)

Finally, combining (
9.11
)–(
9.14
),
we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega}}_{\beta,mT})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigr]\leq 2^{\frac{m}{2}}\Biggl(\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}+(2N)^{d}\sqrt{2e^{-K}}\Biggr)^{m}=2^{-m}\,,

where the last inequality holds by choosing

K=2[(3+d)\log 2+d\log N]
.
∎

9.2.
One-body estimates

We now present a simple choice for
f
that yields the desired bounds on the Lyapunov exponents both in dimension
d=1
and in any dimension
d\geq 2
for heavy-tailed environments. This gives the second half of Theorem
2.11

(i)
and of Theorem
2.12
, that is, the lower bounds on
|\gamma_{{\beta}}(p)|
. More precisely, we prove the following.

Proposition 9.4
.

Assume that
\mu<\infty
.

(i)

In dimension
d=1
,
we have

\limsup_{\beta\to 0+}\beta^{-4}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})<0.

(9.15)

(ii)

In any dimension
d\geq 1
, if for some
\alpha<1+\frac{2}{d}
,
we have

\liminf_{A\to\infty}A^{\alpha}\lambda([A,\infty))>0,

then

\limsup_{\beta\to 0+}\beta^{-\frac{\alpha}{\nu_{\alpha}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})<0.

(9.16)

If we only assume

\limsup_{A\to\infty}A^{\alpha}\lambda([A,\infty))>0
,
then

\liminf_{\beta\to 0+}\beta^{-\frac{\alpha}{\nu_{\alpha}}}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})<0.

(9.17)

Proof.

The proofs of
(i)
and
(ii)
slightly differ but have the same starting point, that is, the same function
f
(to which we will apply Corollary
9.2
), up to a choice of parameter.

Preparing the setup.

We start by fixing
R>1
such that

\Bigg((2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{[-R,R-1]}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}u\Bigg)^{d}\geq 1-\frac{1}{64}\,e^{-K}.

(9.18)

where
K
is the constant from Lemma
9.1
. Given
T>0
and
0<a<b\leq\infty
,
we then consider the functional

f({\omega}):=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{(1)}_{T}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\lVert x\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{T},z\in[a,b)\}}\,\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z),

(9.19)

which is Poisson distributed with

\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]={\mathrm{Var}}(f({\omega}))={\lambda}([a,b))(2R\sqrt{T})^{d}T.

(9.20)

Using the formalism of the previous subsection, we further have

f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)=\int_{(0,T)\times(0,\infty)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|B_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{T},z\in[a,b)\}}\,\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}z).

To estimate the expectation and variance of
f^{\prime}
,
we define

\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime}):=\int_{(0,T)\times(0,\infty)}\mathbf{1}_{\{z\in[a,b)\}}\,\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}z).

Note that we have
f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)\leq\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})
and, recalling that
{\omega}^{\prime}
has intensity measure
\beta z\,\mathrm{d}t\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)
, also

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]=\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T,\qquad\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})^{2}]=\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T+(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T)^{2}.

(9.21)

The condition (
9.18
) guarantees that for any
x\in[0,\sqrt{T})^{d}
and
t\in(0,T]

\mathbf{Q}_{x}\Big(\lVert B_{t}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{T}\Big)\geq 1-\frac{1}{64}\,e^{-K}\,,

so that we have

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]\geq\bigg(1-\frac{1}{64}\,e^{-K}\bigg)\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T\,.

(9.22)

We also have, combining (
9.21
) and the first inequality of (
9.22
),

{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B))\leq\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})^{2}]-\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]^{2}\leq\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T+\frac{e^{-K}}{32}(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T)^{2}.

(9.23)

We are now ready to apply these estimates to specific cases.

Proof of (i).

We first choose
a
and
b
such that

{\lambda}([a,b))>0
(in a way that does not depend on
T
or
\beta
)
and then we let

T=T(\beta):=\frac{2^{10}R^{2}e^{2K}{\lambda}([a,b))^{2}}{\beta^{4}\mu_{a,b}(1)^{4}}.

With this choice and for
\beta
sufficiently small, the second term on the right-hand side of (
9.23
) dominates: using (
9.22
) for the second inequality,
we get

{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B))\leq\frac{e^{-K}}{16}(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T)^{2}\leq\frac{e^{-K}}{8}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]^{2}.

Now the reader can check that with our definition of
T
, we have (recall (
9.20
))

{\mathrm{Var}}(f({\omega}))=2R{\lambda}([a,b))T^{\frac{3}{2}}=\frac{e^{-K}}{16}(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1)T)^{2}\leq\frac{e^{-K}}{8}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]^{2}.

Therefore, condition
(C1)
in Corollary
9.2
is indeed satisfied for small
\beta
, and we thus have

\limsup_{\beta\to 0+}\beta^{-4}\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})\leq-\frac{(\log 2)\mu_{a,b}(1)^{4}}{2^{10}R^{2}e^{2K}{\lambda}([a,b))^{2}}.

Proof of (ii).

We let
\ell>0
denote the limit superior/inferior of
A^{-\alpha}{\lambda}([A,\infty))
. We choose

A=A(\beta):=M\,\beta^{-\frac{d+2}{2-d(\alpha-1)}}\,,

(9.24)

where
M=M(K,R,\ell)
is a large constant that depends on
K
,
R
and
\ell
.
For the remainder of the proof, we assume that
\beta
is such that we have,
for the value of
A
specified above,

{\lambda}([A,\infty))\geq\frac{\ell A^{-\alpha}}{2}\,.

(9.25)

(We assume that
\beta
is sufficiently small if the assumption is about the limit inferior, and we take
\beta
along a well-chosen subsequence tending to
0
if the assumption is about the limit superior.)
We are going to use Corollary
9.2
for the function (
9.19
), with the choices
a=A
,
b=\infty
and

T:=\Big[e^{-K}(2R)^{-d}{\lambda}([A,\infty))^{-1}\Big]^{\frac{2}{2+d}}.

(9.26)

With our choice of
A
and our tail assumption, we have

T\leq\bigg[e^{-K}(2R)^{-d}\frac{2}{\ell}M^{\alpha}\bigg]^{\frac{2}{2+d}}\beta^{-\frac{\alpha}{\nu_{\alpha}}}\,.

Therefore, once we show that
(C2)
is satisfied, Corollary
9.2
yields the desired bound on
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})
.

For the expectation of
f
, by (
9.20
), our choice for
T
directly yields

\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]=(2R)^{d}{\lambda}([A,\infty))T^{1+\frac{d}{2}}=e^{-K}.

To check the condition on the variance of
f^{\prime}
, in view of (
9.22
) and (
9.23
), it is sufficient to have

\beta\mu_{A,\infty}(1)T\geq 4e^{K}.

To this end, we bound
\mu_{A,\infty}(1)\geq A{\lambda}([A,\infty))
and use the definition (
9.26
) of
T

to get that
\beta\mu_{A,\infty}(1)T
is bounded below by a constant (that depends on
K,R
) times
{\beta}A\lambda([A,\infty))^{d/(2+d)}
. By (
9.25
) and
the definition of
A
in (
9.24
), the latter can be made large by choosing
M=M(K,R,\ell)
sufficiently large.
∎

9.3.
Multi-body estimates

The aim of this section is to prove the following.

Proposition 9.5
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2
and
\mu<\infty
. Then

\limsup_{\beta\to 0}\frac{\log\lvert\log\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})\rvert\rvert}{\lvert\log\beta\rvert}\leq 1+\tfrac{2}{d}.

(9.27)

With Propositions
9.4
and
9.5
at hand, we can complete the proof of Theorems
2.11
and

2.12
. Recall that the first half of the proof was given in Sections
7.2
and
7.3
, respectively.

Proof of Theorems
2.11
and

2.12
(second half).

By (
9.1
), the lower bounds in (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) directly follow from (
9.15
) and (
9.27
). Next, (
2.20
) follows from (
9.17
) since

\mu_{1,\infty}(q)=\infty
implies that
\limsup_{A\to\infty}A^{\alpha}\lambda([A,\infty))>0
for every
\alpha>q
.
Finally, the upper bound in (
2.22
) follows from (
2.20
) because (
2.21
) implies
\mu_{1,\infty}(q)=\infty
for all
q>{\alpha}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.5
.

In Section
9.2
, counting the number of atoms in
{\omega}
with size in a certain range was sufficient to determine the correct order of magnitude (in
\beta
) of
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})
(and hence of
\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(p)
for other values of
p
). If
d\geq 2
and if
{\lambda}
is light-tailed, in order to obtain optimal estimates, we must consider certain
clusters
of atoms. Furthermore, the size of these point configurations, which we denote by
k
, must be taken large.
We shall obtain our result (
9.27
) by proving that for any fixed
k\geq 3
, we have

\limsup_{\beta\to 0}\frac{\log\lvert\log\lvert\bar{\gamma}_{\beta}(\frac{1}{2})\rvert\rvert}{\lvert\log\beta\rvert}\leq(1+\tfrac{2}{d})\frac{k}{k-2}.

This will be achieved again by using Corollary
9.2
. We need to introduce a few parameters for the functional
f
used in this proof. First, we fix
0<a<b<\infty
such that
{\lambda}([a,b))>0
as well as two parameters
0<\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}
satisfying

\kappa\in\bigg(\frac{1}{k},\frac{1}{k-1}\bigg)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(k-2)\kappa^{\prime}+\kappa<1.

(9.28)

Furthermore, we let
R=R(k,d)
be such that

\Bigg((2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{[-\sqrt{2}R,\sqrt{2}(R-1)]}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}u\Bigg)^{d}\Bigg((2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{[-R,R]}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}u\Bigg)^{d(k-1)}\geq 1-\frac{e^{-K}}{8},

(9.29)

where
K
is the constant from Lemma
9.1
.
With this choice of
R
, we have, for any
T>0
and
\boldsymbol{t}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{k}(T)
such that
t_{1}\leq\frac{T}{2}
,

\inf_{x\in[0,\sqrt{T})^{d}}\mathbf{Q}_{x}\Big(\lVert B_{t_{1}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{T},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket:\lVert\Delta B_{t_{i}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{\Delta t_{i}}\Big)\geq 1-\frac{e^{-K}}{8}.

(9.30)

Finally, for some small
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
(independent of
\beta
and to be chosen later), we set

T:=\exp\bigg({\varepsilon}^{-\frac{4+d}{4(k-2)}}\beta^{-(1+\frac{2}{d})\frac{k}{k-2}}\bigg).

(9.31)

Using the notation
v_{I}:=\prod_{i\in I}v_{i}
for
I\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket
, we consider the multi-body functional

\begin{multlined}f({\omega})=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{(k)}_{T}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket:\ z_{i}\in[a,b)\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{t_{1}\leq\frac{T}{2},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k-1\rrbracket:\ \Delta t_{i}\in[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}],\ \Delta t_{\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket}\leq{\varepsilon}\beta^{{2k}/{d}}T^{-1}\}}\\
\times\mathbf{1}_{\{\lVert x_{1}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{T},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket:\ \lVert\Delta x_{i}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{\Delta t_{i}}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i}).\end{multlined}

(9.32)

This function
f
counts clusters of atoms that are so close to each other (in time) that the benefit of visiting all of them, which is
\prod_{i=2}^{k}z_{i}\rho(\Delta t_{i},\Delta x_{i})
or roughly
(\Delta t_{\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket})^{-d/2}
, outweighs the cost of visiting the first atom of the group, which is of order
T^{-d/2}
in the bulk of the box.
Limiting the possible range for
\Delta t_{i}
is convenient in the computations and our particular choice for this limitation is largely
ad hoc
. If

{\varepsilon}\beta^{\frac{2k}{d}}T^{-1+(k-2)\kappa^{\prime}}\leq T^{-\kappa},

(9.33)

then the conditions on the
t_{i}
’s in the first line of (
9.32
) imply
\Delta t_{k}\leq T^{-\kappa}
.
With our choice (
9.31
) for
T
and (
9.28
), the condition (
9.33
) is satisfied for any
\beta<1
.

Let us now check that condition
(C2)
in Corollary
9.2
is satisfied. We start with the condition on
\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]
.
Integrating over the
x_{i}
’s and
t_{1}
, making the change of variable
s_{i}=\Delta t_{i+1}
and using that we have
\Delta t_{k}\leq T^{-\kappa}
, we get

\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]=\frac{T^{1+\frac{d}{2}}}{2}\Big[(2R)^{d}{\lambda}([a,b))\Big]^{k}\int_{[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}]^{k-2}\times[0,T^{-\kappa}]}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{\llbracket k-1\rrbracket}\leq{\varepsilon}\beta^{{2k}/{d}}T^{-1}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}s_{i}^{\frac{d}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s_{i}.

(9.34)

Next, we integrate with respect to
s_{k-1}
and use (
9.33
) to obtain

\begin{multlined}T^{1+\frac{d}{2}}\int_{[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}]^{k-2}\times[0,T^{-\kappa}]}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{k-1}\leq{\varepsilon}\beta^{{2k}/{d}}T^{-1}/s_{\llbracket k-2\rrbracket}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}s_{i}^{\frac{d}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s_{i}\\
=\frac{{\varepsilon}^{1+\frac{d}{2}}\beta^{(1+\frac{2}{d})k}}{1+\frac{d}{2}}\int_{[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}]^{k-2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-2}s_{i}^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}s_{i}=\frac{{\varepsilon}^{1+\frac{d}{2}}\beta^{(1+\frac{2}{d})k}[(\kappa^{\prime}-\kappa)\log T]^{k-2}}{d+2}.\end{multlined}

(9.35)

Therefore, by (
9.31
), there exists a constant
C=C(R,{\lambda},a,b,\kappa,\kappa^{\prime},k,d)
such that

\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]=C{\varepsilon}^{1+\frac{d}{2}}\beta^{(1+\frac{2}{d})k}(\log T)^{k-2}=C{\varepsilon}^{\frac{d}{4}}.

Hence, choosing
{\varepsilon}\leq(Ce^{K})^{-{4}/{d}}
fulfills the assumption on
\mathbb{E}[f({\omega})]
in
(C2)
; let us stress that
{\varepsilon}
must satisfy a second condition which will be specified later in the proof.

Let us now estimate the expectation and variance of
f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)
(recall the definition (
9.6
)). We consider an alternative function

\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime}):=\int_{([0,T]\times[a,b))^{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\{t_{1}\leq\frac{T}{2},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k-1\rrbracket:\ \Delta t_{i}\in[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}],\ \Delta t_{\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket}\leq{\varepsilon}\beta^{{2k}/{d}}T^{-1}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})

(9.36)

that does not include any restriction for
B
.
Using (
9.30
), we obtain, similarly to (
9.22
) and (
9.23
), that for any
x\in[0,\sqrt{T})^{d}

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}[f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]&\geq\Bigg(1-\frac{e^{-K}}{8}\Bigg)\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})],\\
{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{x}}(f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B))&\leq{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime}))+\frac{e^{-K}}{4}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]^{2}\,.\end{split}

(9.37)

We can thus conclude that the variance bound in
(C2)
is satisfied if

{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime}))\leq\frac{e^{-K}}{4}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]^{2}.

(9.38)

Proceeding as in (
9.34
)–(
9.35
) and recalling that
{\omega}^{\prime}
has intensity measure
{\beta}z\,\mathrm{d}t\,\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)
,
we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]&=(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1))^{k}\frac{T}{2}\int_{[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}]^{k-2}\times[0,T^{-\kappa}]}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{\llbracket k-1\rrbracket}\leq{\varepsilon}\beta^{{2k}/{d}}T^{-1}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\\
&=\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\mu_{a,b}(1)^{k}\beta^{(1+\frac{2}{d})k}\int_{[T^{-\kappa^{\prime}},T^{-\kappa}]^{k-2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-2}s_{i}^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}s_{i}\\
&=C^{\prime}{\varepsilon}\beta^{(1+\frac{2}{d})k}(\log T)^{k-2}=C^{\prime}{\varepsilon}^{-\frac{d}{4}}\,,\end{split}

(9.39)

with
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}({\lambda},a,b,\kappa,\kappa^{\prime},k,d)
, and where we have used the value (
9.31
) of
T
for the last identity.
In order to compute the second moment of
\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})
, we expand the integral in (
9.36
) by writing

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})=\sum_{I\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket}\prod_{i\in I}\Big(\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})-\beta z_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\Big)\prod_{j\in I^{c}}\beta z_{j}\,\mathrm{d}t_{j}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{j}),

(9.40)

where
I^{c}:=\llbracket k\rrbracket\setminus I
.
We let
f_{I}
denote the integral corresponding to the term
I
.
Since
f_{\emptyset}=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]
, we have

{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime}))=\sum_{I,J\subseteq\llbracket k\rrbracket:\ I,J\neq\emptyset}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[f_{I}({\omega}^{\prime})f_{J}({\omega}^{\prime})].

(9.41)

The terms in the sum above are equal to zero if
I
and
J
do not have the same cardinality. If
\lvert I\rvert=\lvert J\rvert
, then
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[f_{I}({\omega}^{\prime})f_{J}({\omega}^{\prime})]
is obtained by matching the values of
(t_{i})_{i\in I}
to that of
(t_{i})_{i\in J}
in the integral before averaging.
More precisely,
we have

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[f_{I}({\omega}^{\prime})f_{J}({\omega}^{\prime})]\\
=(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1))^{2k-\lvert I\rvert}\int_{[0,T]^{2k-\lvert I\rvert}}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}\Big((t^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\Big)\prod_{j\in I^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathrm{d}t^{(1)}_{j}\bigg)\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}\Big((t^{(2)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\Big)\prod_{j\in J^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathrm{d}t^{(2)}_{j}\bigg)\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{d}t_{i},

where
\mathcal{Q}
is the subset of
[0,T]^{k}
induced by the indicator function in (
9.36
), and the vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
are completed by setting

t^{(1)}_{i}=t^{(2)}_{\sigma(i)}=t_{i}\qquad\text{ for }i\in I\,,

(9.42)

with
\sigma
being the unique increasing bijection from
I
to
J
.
In particular, we have

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[f_{\llbracket k\rrbracket}({\omega}^{\prime})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})].

(9.43)

When
\lvert I\rvert=\lvert J\rvert=\ell\in\llbracket k-1\rrbracket
,
we are going to show that
