\int_{[0,T]^{2k-\lvert I\rvert}}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}\Big((t^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\Big)\prod_{j\in I^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathrm{d}t^{(1)}_{j}\bigg)\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}\Big((t^{(2)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\Big)\prod_{j\in J^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathrm{d}t^{(2)}_{j}\bigg)\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{d}t_{i}\leq T^{1-(2k-\ell-1)\kappa}.

(9.44)

So combining (
9.43
) with (
9.44
), we obtain that

{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime}))\leq\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\binom{k}{\ell}^{2}(\beta\mu_{a,b}(1))^{2k-\ell}T^{1-(2k-\ell-1)\kappa}\leq\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]+1,

where the last inequality is valid for sufficiently small
\beta
, because
1-(2k-\ell-1)\kappa\leq 0
thanks to (
9.28
).
For this reason, (
9.38
) is satisfied if
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}({\omega}^{\prime})]\geq 8e^{K}
, which according to (
9.39
) holds true provided that
{\varepsilon}
is chosen small enough.

To complete the proof, let us show how (
9.44
) is obtained: the key is to integrate
in the correct order the different variables.
We construct a graph
G_{I,J}
with
2k-\ell
vertices where each vertex is identified with one of the variables
(t_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(t^{(1)}_{i})_{i\in I^{\mathrm{c}}}
,
(t^{(2)}_{i})_{i\in J^{\mathrm{c}}}
. We start with two (initially disconnected) paths of
k
vertices labeled
(t^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
and
(t^{(2)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
, respectively,
the edges being
\{t^{(r)}_{i},t^{(r)}_{i+1}\}
for
i\in\llbracket k-1\rrbracket
.
Then we glue the two paths together by identifying the vertices
(t^{(1)}_{i})_{i\in I}
with
(t^{(2)}_{i})_{i\in J}
according to (
9.42
); any double edge that might have been created is replaced by a single edge.
As
I\neq\emptyset
, the graph we obtain is connected.
Now we consider an enumeration
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\dots,v_{2k-\ell}
of the vertices of
G_{I,J}
such that for every
j\in\llbracket 2k-\ell-1\rrbracket
, the subgraph
G^{(j)}_{I,J}
of
G_{I,J}
induced by
v_{j+1},\dots,v_{2k-\ell}
is connected. One way of finding such an enumeration is, for example, to construct a spanning tree
T_{I,J}
of
G_{I,J}
and then, after
v_{1},\dots,v_{j}
have been determined for some
j\in\{0,\dots,2k-\ell-1\}
, to take any leaf of
T_{I,J}\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j}\}
as
v_{j+1}
.
We refer to Figure
2
for one example of
G_{I,J}
together with a permitted and a forbidden enumeration according to the rule we just introduced.

(a)
t^{(2)}_{1}
t^{(1)}_{1}
t^{(2)}_{2}
t^{(1)}_{2}
t^{(1)}_{3}
t^{(2)}_{3}
t^{(1)}_{4}
t^{(2)}_{4}
t^{(1)}_{5}
t^{(2)}_{5}
t^{(1)}_{6}
t^{(2)}_{6}
t^{(1)}_{7}
t^{(2)}_{7}
t^{(1)}_{8}
t^{(2)}_{8}

(b)
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
v_{6}

(c)
v_{1}

Figure 2
.
(a) The graph
G_{I,J}
with
k=8
,
I=\{1,3,6,7\}
and
J=\{2,3,6,7\}
and a spanning tree
T_{I,J}
(thick edges); (b) a permitted enumeration of
G_{I,J}
based on
T_{I,J}
; (c) a forbidden enumeration of
G_{I,J}
based on
T_{I,J}
. Integration in the forbidden order only yields a suboptimal bound:
once
v_{1},\dots,v_{4}
have been integrated (
i.e.
, removed from the graph), we obtain two disconnected components, so if integration is continued,
v_{6}
becomes isolated and contributes a factor
T
when integrated
(similarly, both
v_{11}
and
v_{12}
become isolated when
v_{1},\ldots,v_{10}
have been integrated: each of them contributes a factor of
T
.

Having fixed such an enumeration, in order to
obtain (
9.44
), we integrate
v_{1},\dots,v_{2k-\ell}
in this order.
Note that if
v_{j}=t^{(r)}_{i}
for some
j\in\llbracket 2k-\ell-1\rrbracket
,
r\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in I^{c}
, then, by construction,

\{t^{(r)}_{i-1},t^{(r)}_{i+1}\}\cap\{v_{j+1},\dots,v_{2k-\ell}\}\neq\emptyset;

indeed, if both
t_{i-1}^{(r)}
and
t_{i+1}^{(r)}
had been picked before, then
v_{j}=t_{i}^{(r)}
would be an isolated vertex in
G_{I,J}^{(j-1)}
because
i\in I^{c}
.
In the same manner, if
v_{j}=t_{i}
for some
j\in\llbracket 2k-\ell-1\rrbracket
and
i\in I
, then

\{t^{(1)}_{i-1},t^{(1)}_{i+1},t^{(2)}_{\sigma(i)-1},t^{(2)}_{\sigma(i)+1}\}\cap\{v_{j+1},\dots,v_{2k-\ell}\}\neq\emptyset.

As a result, for each
j\in\llbracket 2k-\ell-1\rrbracket
, the range when we integrate with respect to the variable
v_{j}
and the values of the variables
v_{j+1},\dots,v_{2k-\ell}
are fixed is included in an interval of length
T^{-\kappa}
:
Indeed,
by (
9.33
),
the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}
in the integral implies
that
\Delta t^{(r)}_{i}\leq T^{-\kappa}
for all
i\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket

and
r\in\{1,2\}
.
We no longer have constraints only for the last variable
v_{2k-\ell}
, which yields a factor of
T
.
∎

9.4.
Proof of Proposition
2.2

First, notice that it suffices to consider the free-end partition function. Since
({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,x))_{a\in(0,1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(e^{-{\|x\|^{2}}/({2t})}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,0))_{a\in(0,1]}
, the a.s. positivity of the limit when
a\to 0
does not depend on
x
. Hence the result in the point-to-point case follows from the free-end case by Fatou’s Lemma.
Although this is not essential to the reasoning, it is practical for our computations to assume that
\int_{(0,1)}z^{1+{2}/{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)<\infty
.
If this assumption is violated, then the result has already been proved in
[
13
, Proposition 2.10]
.
We also assume that
\mu<\infty
: the general case follows from a truncation procedure, see the end of Section 5 in
[
13
]
. For notational simplicity, we actually assume that
\mu=0
(
i.e.
, that
{\lambda}
has support in
(0,1)
).

While the proof does not rely on Lemma
9.1
, it partially builds on the same idea, which is to show that the size-biased measure transforms an atypical event under the original probability measure into a typical one. Let us summarize this in a lemma that is similar in spirit to Corollary
9.2
. Recall (
1.20
) and the notation
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t}:=\bar{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta}(t,\ast)
.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let us assume that there exists a sequence of integer-valued functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the form (
9.5
) (with
\Upsilon
integer-valued and with
T
replaced by
t
) with the following properties:

(i)

For every
n
,
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{G}_{a}
-measurable for some
a=a_{n}>0
.

(ii)

Using the notation (
9.6
), we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f_{n}({\omega})]=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}}(f^{\prime}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime},B))}{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}[f^{\prime}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]^{2}}=0.

(9.45)

Then we have

\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]=0.

Proof.

Consider
{\varepsilon}>0
and let
n
be sufficiently large such that both quantities in (
9.45
) are smaller than
{\varepsilon}
. Furthermore, let
a=a_{n}
be such that

f_{n}
is
\mathcal{G}_{a}
-measurable.
Defining
K:=\log{\varepsilon}
and

G({\omega}):=\exp(-K\mathbf{1}_{\{f_{n}({\omega})\geq 1\}})
, we
have by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\mathbb{E}\Big[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[G({\omega})^{-1}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[G({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

Clearly, by Markov’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}[G({\omega})^{-1}]\leq 1+e^{K}\mathbb{P}(f_{n}({\omega})\geq 1)\leq 2\,.

Using Lemma
4.1
and the fact that
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{G}_{a}
-measurable, we further have (recall the notation (
9.2
))

\mathbb{E}[G({\omega}){\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t}]=\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\Big[\widehat{G}_{a,t}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\Big].

As
f_{n}
is integer-valued, the inequality (
9.7
) implies that

\widehat{G}_{a,t}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\leq e^{-K}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\widehat{f}_{n}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)=0\}}\leq e^{-K}+\mathbf{1}_{\{f^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)=0\}},

which, by an application of the Chebychev inequality, results in

\mathbb{E}\otimes\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\Big[\widehat{G}_{a,t}({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime},B)\Big]\leq e^{-K}+\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}(f^{\prime}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime},B)\leq 0)\leq e^{-K}+{\varepsilon}=2{\varepsilon}.

Because
a\mapsto({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t})^{1/2}
is a supermartingale, we obtain that

\limsup_{a^{\prime}\to 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a^{\prime}}_{\beta,t})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[({\mathcal{Z}}^{{\omega},a}_{\beta,t})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]\leq 2\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},

which finishes the proof since
{\varepsilon}
is arbitrary.
∎

Before we specify the sequence of functions
f_{n}
, we need to introduce a few parameters.
We let
k_{n}:=1+\lfloor n^{1/3}\rfloor
and we set for
n\geq 1

M_{n}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{k_{i-1}(k_{i-1}+\frac{1}{2})}{(k_{i-1}+1)(k_{i-1}-\frac{1}{2})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b_{n}:=e^{-M_{n}}\,.

Let us stress that since we have

\frac{M_{n+1}}{M_{n}}=1+\frac{1}{2k^{2}_{n}}+O(k^{-3}_{n})

we have
\log M_{n}\sim\frac{3}{2}\,n^{1/3}\sim\frac{3}{2}k_{n}
as
n\to\infty
.
Now, as the quantity
{|\log z|}/{(\log\lvert\log z\rvert)^{5+4/d+{\varepsilon}}}
is of order
M_{n}(\log M_{n})^{-(5+4/d+{\varepsilon})}
on the interval
[b_{n+1},b_{n})
, the assumption (
2.5
) is equivalent to

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{M_{n}}{(\log M_{n})^{5+\frac{4}{d}+{\varepsilon}}}\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty\,.

(9.46)

Then, using that
\log M_{n}\sim c\,n^{1/3}
and in particular that
M_{n}^{1/k_{n}}\leq C
for some constant
C>0
,
we get that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}k_{n}^{-2(1+\frac{2}{d})-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}}M_{n}^{1-\frac{1}{k_{n}}}\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)=\infty\,,

(9.47)

because
k_{n}^{-3-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}}
is summable.
Considering a subsequence if necessary, we assume from now on, without loss of generality, that (
9.47
) holds with a limit instead of a
\limsup
.
We set

V_{n}:=M_{n}^{1-\frac{1}{k_{n}}}\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\,,

(9.48)

so that
\lim_{n\to+\infty}k_{n}^{-2(1+\frac{2}{d})-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}}V_{n}=\infty
. We let
\delta=\delta_{{\varepsilon}}

be sufficiently small so that

\frac{d-2\delta}{d+2}\left(2(1+\tfrac{2}{d})+\tfrac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)>2\,,

(9.49)

and we set

R_{n}:=V_{n}^{\delta/d}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\eta_{n}:=1-\Bigg[(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{[-R_{n},R_{n}]}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}u\Bigg]^{dk_{n}}.

(9.50)

One can easily check that
\lim_{n\to\infty}R_{n}=\infty
and also
\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta_{n}=0
(using that
V_{n}\geq k_{n}^{2(1+2/d)}
).

We are now ready to introduce
f_{n}
. As in the proof of Proposition
9.5
, we want to count clusters of
k_{n}
atoms that are beneficial to visit; note that here we have
k_{n}\to\infty
. More precisely, assuming without loss of generality that
t=1
, we define

\begin{multlined}f_{n}({\omega}):=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{(k_{n})}_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(\Delta t_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket})^{-{d}/{2}}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\geq A_{n},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k_{n}\rrbracket:\ \Delta t_{i}\in[b_{n}^{{2\theta^{\prime}_{n}}/{d}},b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}]\}}\\
\times\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall i\in\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket:\ \lVert\Delta x_{i}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R_{n}\sqrt{\Delta t_{i}},\ z_{i}\in[b_{n+1},b_{n})\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}x_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i})\,,\end{multlined}

(9.51)

where we have used the shorthand notation

A_{n}:=V_{n}^{\frac{d}{d+2}(1+\delta)k_{n}}\,,\qquad\theta_{n}:=\frac{2k_{n}+1}{2k_{n}-1}\qquad\text{ and }\quad\theta_{n}^{\prime}:=\frac{k_{n}}{k_{n}-1}\,.

(9.52)

Note that
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{G}_{b_{n+1}}
-measurable.
The restriction on the range for
x_{i}
and the requirement that

(\Delta t_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket})^{-{d}/{2}}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}
be large are important features of
f
. The remaining constraints are
ad hoc
and mainly serve the purpose to lighten the computations.
If
z_{i}
and
t_{i}
satisfy the constraints above, then recalling the definition of
\theta_{n}^{\prime}
, we have

t^{\frac{d}{2}}_{1}\leq A_{n}^{-1}(\Delta t_{\llbracket 2,k_{n}\rrbracket})^{-\frac{d}{2}}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\leq A_{n}^{-1}\exp(M_{n}[(k_{n}-1)\theta_{n}^{\prime}-k_{n}])=A_{n}^{-1}\,.

(9.53)

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{n}=\infty
(recall that
\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=\infty
)
and
k_{n}b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}\leq k_{n}\exp(-\frac{2}{d}M_{n})
goes to
0
,
we have

A_{n}^{-1}+(k_{n}-1)b_{n}^{2\theta_{n}/d}\leq 1
for sufficiently large
n
,
and hence

\left\{\big(\Delta t_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\big)^{-\frac{d}{2}}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\geq A_{n},\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k_{n}\rrbracket:\ \Delta t_{i}\in[b_{n}^{{2\theta^{\prime}_{n}}/{d}},b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}]\right\}\Longrightarrow t_{k_{n}}\leq 1.

Consequently, integrating first with respect to the
x_{i}
’s, making a change of variables
s_{i}=\Delta t_{i}
and then integrating only with respect to
s_{k_{n}}
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[f_{n}({\omega})]&=(2R_{n})^{dk_{n}}\int_{[b_{n}^{{2\theta^{\prime}_{n}}/{d}},b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}]^{k_{n}-1}\times[0,1]\times[b_{n+1},b_{n})^{k_{n}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(s_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket})^{{d}/{2}}\leq A_{n}^{-1}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{n}}s^{\frac{d}{2}}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\\
&=\frac{2}{d+2}(2R_{n})^{dk_{n}}A_{n}^{-(1+\frac{2}{d})}\int_{[b_{n}^{{2\theta^{\prime}_{n}}/{d}},b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}]^{k_{n}-1}\times[b_{n+1},b_{n})^{k_{n}}}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{n}-1}s^{-1}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}s_{j}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{n}}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\\
&=\frac{2}{d+2}2^{dk_{n}}V_{n}^{-k_{n}}\bigg(\frac{2(\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n})}{d}M_{n}\bigg)^{k_{n}-1}\bigg(\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{k_{n}}\,,\end{split}\!

(9.54)

where we have used the definition of
R_{n}
and
A_{n}
in the last line.
Using the definition of
V_{n}
, together with the fact that
\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n}\leq\frac{1}{2(k_{n}-1)^{2}}
,
we get that

\mathbb{E}[f_{n}({\omega})]\leq\frac{2^{1+d}}{d+2}\bigg(\frac{2^{d}}{d(k_{n}-1)^{2}}\bigg)^{k_{n}-1}\,.

Since
k_{n}\to\infty
,
this shows the first condition in (
9.45
).

To check the second condition in (
9.45
), we compute the expectation and variance of

\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime}):=\int_{\mathfrak{X}_{k_{n}}(1)\times[b_{n+1},b_{n})^{k_{n}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(\Delta t_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket})^{-{d}/{2}}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\geq A,\ \forall i\in\llbracket 2,k_{n}\rrbracket:\ \Delta t_{i}\in[b_{n}^{{2\theta^{\prime}_{n}}/{d}},b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}]\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\delta_{{\omega}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}t_{i},\mathrm{d}z_{i}).\!

(9.55)

Recalling the notation (
9.50
) of
\eta_{n}
, we have

\mathbf{Q}(\forall i\in\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket:\lVert\Delta B_{t_{i}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq R\sqrt{\Delta t_{i}})=1-\eta_{n}\,.

Hence, repeating the computations that led to (
9.37
) in the previous section, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}[f^{\prime}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]&=(1-\eta_{n})\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})],\\
{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}}[f_{n}^{\prime}({\omega}^{\prime},B)]&\leq{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime}))+2\eta_{n}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]^{2}.\end{split}

Since
\eta_{n}
tends to zero, the reader can check that the second condition in (
9.45
) follows from the following claims:

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]&=\infty,\\
{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime}))&\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]\qquad\text{ for sufficiently large $n$.}\end{split}

(9.56)

The first property in (
9.56
) follows from
direct calculation. We have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]&=\int_{[b_{n}^{{2\theta^{\prime}_{n}}/{d}},b^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}]^{k_{n}-1}\times[0,1]\times[b_{n+1},b_{n})^{k_{n}}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(s_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket})^{{d}/{2}}\leq A_{n}^{-1}z_{\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\,{\beta}z_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i})\\
&=\beta^{k_{n}}A_{n}^{-\frac{2}{d}}\bigg(\frac{2(\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n})M_{n}}{d}\bigg)^{k_{n}-1}\bigg(\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z^{1+\frac{2}{d}}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{k_{n}}\\
&=\frac{d}{2(\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n})}\Big(\frac{2{\beta}}{d}(\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n})V_{n}^{\frac{d-2\delta}{d+2}}\Big)^{k_{n}}\,,\end{split}

(9.57)

where the computation is similar to (
9.54
).
Now, since
\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n}\sim\frac{1}{2}k_{n}^{-2}
and
k_{n}^{-2(1+2/d)-{{\varepsilon}}/{2}}V_{n}\to\infty
, we have from our choice (
9.49
) for
\delta

that
(\theta^{\prime}_{n}-\theta_{n})V_{n}^{({d-2\delta})/({d+2})}\to\infty
,
which leads to the first part of (
9.56
).

As in the previous section, to compute the second moment of
\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})
, we can use (
9.40
) to write
{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime}))
in the form (
9.41
). As seen before, only sets with
\lvert I\rvert=\lvert J\rvert
contribute, and the term corresponding to
I=J=\llbracket k_{n}\rrbracket
is simply
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]
.
If
\lvert I\rvert=\lvert J\rvert=\ell\in\llbracket k_{n}-1\rrbracket
, then

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[f_{I}({\omega}^{\prime})f_{J}({\omega}^{\prime})]=\beta^{2k_{n}-\ell}\int_{((0,1)\times[b_{n+1},b_{n}))^{2k_{n}-\ell}}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{R}}\Big((t^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k_{n}},(z^{(1)}_{i})^{k_{n}}_{i=1}\Big)\prod_{j\in I^{\mathrm{c}}}z^{(1)}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t^{(1)}_{j}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z^{(1)}_{i})\bigg)\\
\times\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{R}}\Big((t^{(2)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k_{n}},(z^{(2)}_{i})^{k_{n}}_{i=1}\Big)\prod_{j\in J^{\mathrm{c}}}z^{(2)}_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t^{(2)}_{j}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z^{(2)}_{i})\Bigg)\prod_{i\in I}z_{i}\,\mathrm{d}t_{i}\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}),

where
\mathcal{R}
is the subset of
((0,1)\times[b_{n+1},b_{n}))^{k_{n}}
induced by the indicator function in (
9.55
) and for
i\in I
, we used the notation

t^{(1)}_{i}=t^{(2)}_{\sigma(i)}=t_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad z^{(1)}_{i}=z^{(2)}_{\sigma(i)}=z_{i}\,.

Next, we relax the constraints imposed by
\mathcal{R}

by only keeping

\Delta t^{(r)}_{i}\leq b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}
.
Then we proceed as in the proof of (
9.44
):
by choosing an optimal order, we obtain that integration over each time variable produces a factor of at most
b_{n}^{{2\theta_{n}}/{d}}
, except for the last integration, which produces a factor of one. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[f_{I}({\omega}^{\prime})f_{J}({\omega}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{2k_{n}-\ell}\,b_{n}^{\frac{2\theta_{n}}{d}(2k_{n}-\ell-1)}\,\bigg(\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)^{2k_{n}-\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{2k_{n}-\ell}\exp\bigg(\frac{2}{d}\Big[(2k_{n}-\ell)M_{n+1}-(2k_{n}-\ell-1)\theta_{n}M_{n}\Big]\bigg)\mu_{0,1}(1+\tfrac{2}{d})^{2k_{n}-\ell},

where in the second line we simply used
the fact that

\int_{[b_{n+1},b_{n})}z\,{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)\leq(b_{n+1})^{-\frac{2}{d}}\mu_{0,1}(1+\tfrac{2}{d})\,,

together with the definition of
b_{n}
and
b_{n+1}
.
Recalling the definition of
\theta_{n}
and of
M_{n}
,
we have
M_{n+1}=\frac{k_{n}}{k_{n}+1}M_{n}\theta_{n}
, so that

(2k_{n}-\ell)M_{n+1}-(2k_{n}-\ell-1)\theta_{n}M_{n}=M_{n}\theta_{n}k_{n}\left[\frac{2k_{n}-\ell}{k_{n}+1}-\frac{2k_{n}-\ell-1}{k_{n}}\right]\leq 0

for any
\ell\leq k_{n}-1
.
We therefore end up with

\begin{split}{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\beta}}(\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime}))\leq\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{n}-1}\binom{k_{n}}{\ell}^{2}\beta^{2k_{n}-\ell}\mu_{0,1}(1+\tfrac{2}{d})^{2k_{n}-\ell}\leq\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]+C^{\prime}(d,\lambda,{\beta})^{k_{n}}\,.\end{split}

Recalling (
9.57
), we have that for any constant
C>0

the expectation
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{\beta}[\bar{f}_{n}({\omega}^{\prime})]
is larger than
C^{k_{n}}
for sufficiently large
n
.
This proves the second claim in (
9.56
) and concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Technical results

A.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.3

The result is contained in
[
67
]
, but only implicitly, so we give a short proof. Let us first check that (
4.3
)
ensures that all integrals in (
4.4
) are well defined and finite.
Applying the BDG inequality and using the subadditivity (
5.4
) of the function
x\mapsto x^{p/2}
for
x>0
iteratively, we deduce that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\biggr\rvert^{p}\Biggr]\\
&\qquad\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggr(\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-1}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N-1})\Biggr)^{2}\,\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr)^{p/2}\Biggr]\\
&\qquad\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\int_{\mathbb{X}}\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-1}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N-1})\biggr\rvert^{p}\,\delta_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr]\\
&\qquad=C_{p}\int_{\mathbb{X}}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-1}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N-1})\biggr\rvert^{p}\Biggr]\,\nu(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\\
&\qquad\leq\cdots\leq C_{p}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}\lvert f(\boldsymbol{w})\rvert^{p}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots\nu(\mathrm{d}w_{N})<\infty.\end{split}

(A.1)

These inequalities remain unchanged if
{\omega}
is replaced by
{\omega}_{1},\dots,{\omega}_{N}
and
\mathbb{E}
is replaced by
\mathbb{E}^{\otimes N}
.

Now we move to the proof of (
4.4
), for which we shall prove the second inequality. To obtain the reverse inequality it is sufficient follow the same proof and observe that all estimates are two-sided (
i.e.
, one can always substitute “
\geq
” for “
\leq
” if one also replaces
1/C
by
C
).
We now consider a state space on which
{\omega}
is jointly defined with our i.i.d. copies
{\omega}_{1},\dots,{\omega}_{N}
(and
{\omega}
is independent of
({\omega}_{1},\dots,{\omega}_{N})
). We let
\bar{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\otimes N}
denote the associated probability.
In analogy with (
1.5
), we consider a filtration
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on this state space defined by

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\mathcal{F}_{t}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{t}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(N)}_{t},\quad\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{t}:=\sigma\Big({\omega}\cap([0,t]\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))\Big),\quad i=1,\dots,N.

Our result follows from applying the following inequality, valid for
i=1,\dots,N
, iteratively:

\begin{multlined}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\dots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\bigg]\\
\leq\frac{C}{1-p}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i-1})\,M^{{\omega}_{i}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\bigg].\end{multlined}

(A.2)

Let us first spend some time on the first step
i=N
.
By elementary properties of Itô integrals, the processes

(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined by

\begin{split}X_{t}&:=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\mathbf{1}_{(0,t]}(t_{N})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N}),\\
Y_{t}&:=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\mathbf{1}_{(0,t]}(t_{N})\,M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N-1})\,M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\end{split}

are martingales with respect to
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, whose quadratic variation processes are given by

\displaystyle[X]_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-1}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\mathbf{1}_{(0,t]}(t_{N})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N-1})\Biggr)^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N}),

\displaystyle[Y]_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-1}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\mathbf{1}_{(0,t]}(t_{N})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{N-1})\Biggr)^{2}\,\delta_{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N}),

respectively. Since
{\omega}
and
{\omega}_{N}
are Poisson random measures with the same
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
-intensity measures (namely
\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}x\otimes{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}z)
), the jump measures associated to
[X]
and
[Y]
have the same predictable compensator in
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. As a result,
X
and
Y

are
weakly tangential
martingales
in the sense of
[
67
, Section 3]
.
Thus, by
[
67
, Thm. 4.1]
and Doob’s inequality, there is
C_{p}>0
such that

\bar{\mathbb{E}}[\lvert X_{\infty}\rvert^{p}]\leq\bar{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{t\geq 0}{\lvert X_{t}\rvert^{p}}\bigg]\leq C_{p}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{t\geq 0}{\lvert Y_{t}\rvert^{p}}\bigg]\leq C_{p}\bigg(\frac{p}{p-1}\bigg)^{p}\bar{\mathbb{E}}[|Y_{\infty}|^{p}].

(A.3)

The reader can check that for the implicit constant
C_{p}
from
[
67
, Thm. 4.2]
, one may take
C_{p}:=(24p^{2})^{p}[2\times 3^{p/2}(2^{p/2+1}+14\times 3^{p/2}(28\times 3^{p/2})^{p/2})]
.
The expression in brackets
comes from the last equation in
[
67
, p. 38]
(with
{\varphi}(x)=\lvert x\rvert^{p/2}
and
c=(14\times 3^{p/2})^{-1}
), while the computation in
[
67
, p. 39]
, combined with the BDG inequality as in
[
49
, Ch. VII, Thm. 92]
, entails an additional factor of
(24p^{2})^{p}
.
Most importantly,
C_{p}
is bounded uniformly in
p\in(1,2]
, so that there exists a universal constant
C
such that for
p\in(1,2]
,

\bar{\mathbb{E}}[\lvert X_{\infty}\rvert^{p}]\ \leq\bigg(\frac{C}{p-1}\bigg)\bar{\mathbb{E}}[\lvert Y_{\infty}\rvert^{p}],

which concludes the proof of (
A.2
) for
i=N
.

In order to iterate and prove (
A.2
) for
i\leq N-1
, we
wish to interchange
M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{i})
with
M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})
, that is, we want to write

\begin{multlined}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\\
=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{i}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-i}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr)\,M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{i}).\end{multlined}

(A.4)

Even though the integral on the right-hand side is anticipative when considering the filtration
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
we can recover an integral in Itô’s sense by constructing the inner integrals

\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-i}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})

using the filtration
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and the outer integrals using the filtration
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}
defined
by

\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}_{t}:=\mathcal{F}_{t}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{t}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(i)}_{t}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(i+1)}_{\infty}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(N)}_{\infty}.

Note that the inner integrals are
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}_{0}
-measurable.
With this convention, we can justify (
A.4
) as follows: It certainly holds if
f
is a step function, that is, if
f
only assumes finitely many values (in that case, the integrals are simply finite sums).
For general
f
, take a sequence of step functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lvert f_{n}\rvert\leq\lvert f\rvert
for all
n
and
f_{n}\to f
pointwise as
n\to\infty
. Equation (
A.4
) holds for
f_{n}
, and arguing similarly to (
A.1
) and using dominated convergence, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\Biggl[\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N}}(f-f_{n})(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr\rvert^{p}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\Biggl[\Biggl|\int_{\mathbb{X}^{i}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-i}}(f-f_{n})(\boldsymbol{w})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr)\,M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{i})\Biggr|^{p}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=0,

proving (
A.4
). The fact that we are able to interpret the latter integral in Itô’s sense is crucial for the BDG inequality, which was needed in (
A.1
), to apply.
Once (
A.4
) is established, we can prove (
A.2
) by considering the weakly tangential martingales (for the filtration
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(i)}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
)

\displaystyle X^{(i)}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{i}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-i}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\mathbf{1}_{(0,t]}(t_{i})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr)\,M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{\omega}(\mathrm{d}w_{i}),

\displaystyle Y^{(i)}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{X}^{i}}\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb{X}^{N-i}}f(\boldsymbol{w})\mathbf{1}_{(0,t]}(t_{i})\,M^{{\omega}_{i+1}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i+1})\cdots M^{{\omega}_{N}}(\mathrm{d}w_{N})\Biggr)\,M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\cdots M^{{\omega}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i-1})\,M^{{\omega}_{i}}(\mathrm{d}w_{i}),

applying the same estimate as in (
A.3
) and re-arranging the integrals similarly to (
A.4
).

A.2.
Proof of Lemma
4.4

For
A,u,{\varepsilon}>0
and
H\in{\mathcal{S}}
that satisfies
\lvert H\rvert\leq u\lvert K\rvert
, we have that

\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}}H({\omega},t,x)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)\Biggr\rvert>{\varepsilon}\Biggr)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\geq}\Bigl(Au\lVert K\rVert_{\xi_{{\omega}_{<}},p;\mathbb{P}_{<}}>{\varepsilon}\Bigr)\\
+\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}}H({\omega},t,x)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)\Biggr\rvert>uA\lVert K\rVert_{\xi_{{\omega}_{<}},p;\mathbb{P}_{<}}\Biggr).

The second probability is bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{\geq}\Biggl[\frac{1}{u^{p}A^{p}\lVert K\rVert^{p}_{\xi_{{\omega}_{<}},p;\mathbb{P}_{<}}}\mathbb{E}_{<}\left[\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}}H({\omega},t,x)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)\right\rvert^{p}\right]\Biggr]\leq A^{-p}.

Therefore, using dominated convergence for the first term, we get

\lim_{u\to 0}\sup_{H\in{\mathcal{S}},\lvert H\rvert\leq u\lvert K\rvert}\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\Biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{X}}H({\omega},t,x)\,\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)\Biggr\rvert>{\varepsilon}\Biggr)\leq\lim_{u\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\geq}[\mathbf{1}_{\{Au\lVert K\rVert_{\xi_{{\omega}_{<}},p;\mathbb{P}_{<}}>{\varepsilon}\}}]+A^{-p}\leq A^{-p}.

Sending
A\to\infty
shows that the left-hand side is
0
, which is obviously equivalent to
\lVert uK\rVert_{\xi_{<},0}\to 0
as
u\to 0
. Therefore,
K
is integrable with respect to
\xi_{{\omega}_{<}}
by (
4.6
).
∎
